Semiclassical Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem and asymptotics of the orthogonal Bergman kernel
Siarhei Finski

Abstract




Abstract.
We study the asymptotics of Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension operator and orthogonal Bergman projector associated with high tensor powers of a positive line bundle.

More precisely, for a fixed complex submanifold in a complex manifold, we consider the operator which associates to a given holomorphic section of a positive line bundle over the submanifold the holomorphic extension of it to the ambient manifold with the minimal
L^{2}
-norm.
When the tensor power of the line bundle tends to infinity, we obtain an explicit asymptotic expansion of this operator.
This is done by proving an exponential estimate for the associated Schwartz kernel and showing that this Schwartz kernel admits a full asymptotic expansion.
We prove similar results for the projection onto holomorphic sections orthogonal to those which vanish along the submanifold.

1
Introduction

One of the main goals of this article is to give an asymptotic version of Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem when the powers of the twisting positive line bundle tend to infinity. Another goal is to establish asymptotic expansion of the Schwartz kernel of the orthogonal Bergman projector onto the holomorphic sections orthogonal to those which vanish along a submanifold.

More precisely, we fix two (not necessarily compact) complex manifolds
X,Y
, of dimensions
n
and
m
respectively.
We fix also a complex embedding
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to X
, a positive line bundle
(L,h^{L})
over
X
and an arbitrary Hermitian vector bundle
(F,h^{F})
over
X
.
In particular, we assume that for the curvature
R^{L}
of the Chern connection on
(L,h^{L})
, the closed real
(1,1)
-form

\omega\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}R^{L}

(1.1)

is positive.
We denote by
g^{TX}
the Riemannian metric on
X
induced by
\omega
as follows

g^{TX}(\cdot,\cdot)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\omega(\cdot,J\cdot),

(1.2)

where
J\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}TX\to TX
is the complex structure on
X
.
We denote by
g^{TY}
the induced metric on
Y
.

We assume throughout the whole article that the triple
(X,Y,g^{TX})
, and the Hermitian vector bundles
(L,h^{L})
,
(F,h^{F})
, are of bounded geometry in the sense of Definitions
2.3
,
2.4
.

Now, we fix some positive (with respect to the orientation given by the complex structure) volume forms
dv_{X}
,
dv_{Y}
on
X
and
Y
.
For smooth sections
f,f^{\prime}
of
L^{p}\otimes F
,
p\in\mathbb{N}
, over
X
, we define the
L^{2}
-scalar product using the pointwise scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{h}
, induced by
h^{L}
and
h^{F}
, by

\big<f,f^{\prime}\big>_{L^{2}(X)}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\int_{X}\big<f(x),f^{\prime}(x)\big>_{h}dv_{X}(x).

(1.3)

Similarly, using
dv_{Y}
, we introduce the
L^{2}
-scalar product for sections of
\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F)
over
Y
.
We denote by
H^{0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
and
H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
the vector spaces of holomorphic sections of
L^{p}\otimes F
over
X
and
Y
respectively with bounded
L^{2}
-norm.

We assume that for the Riemannian volume forms
dv_{g^{TX}}
,
dv_{g^{TY}}
of
(X,g^{TX})
,
(Y,g^{TY})
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C_{k}>0
, such that over
X
and
Y
, the following bounds hold

\Big\|\frac{dv_{g^{TX}}}{dv_{X}}\Big\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(X)},\Big\|\frac{dv_{X}}{dv_{g^{TX}}}\Big\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(X)},\Big\|\frac{dv_{g^{TY}}}{dv_{Y}}\Big\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(Y)},\Big\|\frac{dv_{Y}}{dv_{g^{TY}}}\Big\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(Y)}\leq C_{k}.

(1.4)

By extending Ohsawa-Takegoshi theorem,
[
35
]
,
[
34
]
,
[
13
, §13]
, in Theorem
4.4
, we prove that there is
p_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{0}
,
g\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, there is
f\in H^{0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
, satisfying
f|_{Y}=g
.
Then, for the
Bergman projector

B_{p}^{Y}
, given by the orthogonal projection from the space of
L^{2}
-sections
L^{2}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
to
H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, we define the
extension
operator

{\rm{E}}_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}L^{2}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))\to H^{0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F),

(1.5)

by putting
{\rm{E}}_{p}g=f
, where
f|_{Y}=B_{p}^{Y}g
, and
f
has the minimal
L^{2}
-norm among those
f^{\prime}\in H^{0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
satisfying
f^{\prime}|_{Y}=B_{p}^{Y}g
(the set of such
f^{\prime}
is closed and convex, hence
f
exists and it is unique by Hilbert projection theorem; moreover, the operator
{\rm{E}}_{p}
is easily seen to be linear).
In particular, for
g\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, we have
({\rm{E}}_{p}g)|_{Y}=g
.
Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem in this context means precisely that the operator
{\rm{E}}_{p}
is bounded.
In this article, we find an explicit asymptotic expansion of
{\rm{E}}_{p}
, as
p\to\infty
.
Remark that
\|{\rm{E}}_{p}\|
is the
optimal constant
for the estimate of the
L^{2}
-norm of the extension.

More precisely, we identify the normal bundle
N
of
Y
in
X
as an orthogonal complement of
TY
in
TX
(with respect to
g^{TX}
), so that we have the following orthogonal decomposition

TX|_{Y}\to TY\oplus N.

(1.6)

We denote by
g^{N}
the metric on
N
induced by
g^{TX}
.
Let
P^{N}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}TX|_{Y}\to N
,
P^{Y}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}TX|_{Y}\to TY
, be the projections induced by (
1.6
).
Clearly,
\nabla^{N}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=P^{N}\nabla^{TX}|_{Y}
defines a connection on
N
.

For
y\in Y
,
Z_{N}\in N_{y}
, let
\real\ni t\mapsto\exp_{y}^{X}(tZ_{N})\in X
be the geodesic in
X
in direction
Z_{N}
.
Bounded geometry condition means, in particular, that this map induces a diffeomorphism of
r_{\perp}
-neighborhood of the zero section in
N
with a tubular neighborhood
U
of
Y
in
X
.
From now on, we use this identification implicitly.
Of course, points
(y,0)
, for
y\in Y
then correspond to
Y
.

We denote by
\pi_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U\to Y
the natural projection
(y,Z_{N})\mapsto y
.
Over
U
, we identify
L,F
to
\pi_{0}^{*}(L|_{Y}),\pi_{0}^{*}(F|_{Y})
by the parallel transport with respect to the respective Chern connections along the geodesic
[0,1]\ni t\mapsto(y,tZ_{N})\in X
,
|Z_{N}|<r_{\perp}
. We also define a function
\kappa_{N}
as follows

dv_{X}=\kappa_{N}dv_{Y}\wedge dv_{N},

(1.7)

where
dv_{N}
is the relative Riemannian volume form on
(N,g^{N})
.
Of course, we have
\kappa_{N}|_{Y}=1
if

dv_{X}=dv_{g^{TX}},\qquad dv_{Y}=dv_{g^{TY}}.

(1.8)

Using the above identification, we define the operator
{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}L^{2}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))\to L^{2}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
as follows.
For
g\in L^{2}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, we let
({\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}g)(x)=0
,
x\notin U
, and in
U
, we put

({\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}g)(y,Z_{N})=(B_{p}^{Y}g)(y)\exp\Big(-p\frac{\pi}{2}|Z_{N}|^{2}\Big)\rho\Big(\frac{|Z_{N}|}{r_{\perp}}\Big).

(1.9)

where the norm
|Z_{N}|
,
Z_{N}\in N
, is taken with respect to
g^{N}
, and
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\real_{+}\to[0,1]
satisfies

\rho(x)=\begin{cases}1,\quad\text{for $x<\frac{1}{4}$},\\
0,\quad\text{for $x>\frac{1}{2}$}.\end{cases}

(1.10)

Now, for
g\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, the section
{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}g
satisfies
({\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}g)|_{Y}=g
, but
{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}g
is not holomorphic over
X
unless
g
is zero.
Nevertheless, as we shall see,
{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}g
can be used to approximate very well the holomorphic section
{\rm{E}}_{p}g
.
More precisely, we have the following result.

Theorem 1.1
.

There are
C>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
, we have

\big\|{\rm{E}}_{p}-{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}\big\|\leq\frac{C}{p^{\frac{n-m+1}{2}}}.

(1.11)

where
\|\cdot\|
is the operator norm.
Also, as
p\to\infty
, we have

\big\|{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}\big\|\sim\frac{1}{p^{\frac{n-m}{2}}}\cdot\sup_{y\in Y}\kappa_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}(y).

(1.12)

Moreover, under assumption (
1.8
), in (
1.11
), one can replace
p^{\frac{n-m+1}{2}}
by an asymptotically better estimate if and only if
Y
is a totally geodesic submanifold of
(X,g^{TX})
, i.e. the second fundamental form, see (
1.16
), vanishes.

Remark 1.2
.

a) From the bounded geometry condition
\kappa_{N}
is bounded, see Section
2.1
.

b) By the remark after (
1.5
), we see that this theorem gives an asymptotic of the optimal constant in Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem.
For other versions of optimal Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem, see Błocki
[
6
]
, Guan-Zhou
[
24
]
.

c)
Our result refines a theorem of Randriambololona
[
37
, Théorème 3.1.10]
, stating in the compact case that for any
\epsilon>0
, there is
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that
\big\|{\rm{E}}_{p}\big\|\leq\exp(\epsilon p)
for
p\geq p_{1}
.
Remark, however, that theorem of Randriambololona also works for smooth nonreduced subvarieties
Y
.
For a generalization of Theorem
1.1
in this context, see the subsequent work
[
19
]
.

Corollary 1.3
.

There is
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
g\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, we have

\big\|\nabla^{k}({\rm{E}}_{p}g)\big\|_{L^{2}(X)}\leq\frac{C}{p^{\frac{n-m-k}{2}}}\cdot\mathinner{\!\left\lVert g\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(Y)},

(1.13)

where the connection
\nabla
is induced by the Chern and Levi-Civita connections.

Remark 1.4
.

The estimate of type (
1.13
) was lacking in Demailly’s approach to the invariance of plurigenera for Kähler families, see
[
15
, (4.19)]
.
Remark that in
[
15
]
, the manifold
X
is an open strictly pseudoconvex domain
U
, given by the neighborhood of the diagonal of certain product manifold (hence,
X
is never compact).
Our theorem applies to
X
of this form because the Bergman metric on any strictly pseudoconvex domain has bounded geometry, see Klembeck
[
26
, Theorem 1 and p. 279]
and Greene-Krantz
[
22
, p. 8]
.
We, however, couldn’t find a complex-geometric description of submanifolds
Y
for which there is a metric
g^{TX}
on
X
coming from a strictly plurisubharmonic exhaustion function, such that the triple
(X,Y,g^{TX})
is of bounded geometry.

Both Theorem
1.1
and Corollary
1.3
appear as almost direct consequences of more precise results about the asymptotics of the
Schwartz kernel

{\rm{E}}_{p}(x,y)\in(L^{p}\otimes F)_{x}\otimes(L^{p}\otimes F)_{y}^{*}
,
x\in X
,
y\in Y
, of
{\rm{E}}_{p}
with respect to
dv_{Y}
.
To state them, recall that
{\rm{E}}_{p}(x,y)
is defined so that for any
g\in L^{2}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
,
x\in X
, we have

({\rm{E}}_{p}g)(x)=\int_{Y}{\rm{E}}_{p}(x,y)\cdot g(y)\cdot dv_{Y}(y).

(1.14)

As we show, this Schwartz kernel shares similar asymptotic behavior with the Bergman kernel, previously studied by Dai-Liu-Ma
[
11
]
and Ma-Marinescu
[
32
]
.
More precisely, similarly to
[
32
]
, we first show that this Schwartz kernel has exponential decay.

Theorem 1.5
.

There are
c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
x\in X
,
y\in Y
, the following estimate holds

\Big|{\rm{E}}_{p}(x,y)\Big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(X\times Y)}\leq Cp^{m+\frac{k}{2}}\exp\big(-c\sqrt{p}{\rm{dist}}(x,y)\big),

(1.15)

where the pointwise
\mathscr{C}^{k}
-norm of an element from
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X\times Y,(L^{p}\otimes F)\boxtimes(L^{p}\otimes F)^{*})
at a point
(x,y)\in X\times Y
is the sum of the norms induced by
h^{L},h^{F}
and
g^{TX}
, evaluated at
(x,y)
, of the derivatives up to order
k
with respect to the connection induced by the Chern connections on
L,F
and the Levi-Civita connection on
TX
.

Theorem
1.5
implies that to understand fully the asymptotics of the Schwartz kernel of the extension operator, it suffices to do so in a neighborhood of a fixed point
(y_{0},y_{0})\in Y\times Y
in
X\times Y
.
Our next result shows that after a reparametrization, given by a homothety with factor
\sqrt{p}
in certain coordinates around
(y_{0},y_{0})
, the kernel of this extension operator admits a complete asymptotic expansion in integer powers of
\sqrt{p}
, as
p\to\infty
, similarly to the off-diagonal asymptotic expansion of Bergman kernel
[
11
]
.
To state it, let us fix some notation.

We define the
second fundamental form

A\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(Y,T^{*}Y\otimes\text{End}{(TX|_{Y})})
by

A\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\nabla^{TX}|_{Y}-\nabla^{TY}\oplus\nabla^{N}.

(1.16)

Trivially,
A
takes values in skew-symmetric endomorphisms of
TX|_{Y}
, interchanging
TY
and
N
.

We fix a point
y_{0}\in Y
and an orthonormal frame
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2m})
(resp.
(e_{2m+1},\ldots,e_{2n})
) in
(T_{y_{0}}Y,g_{y_{0}}^{TY})
(resp. in
(N_{y_{0}},g^{N}_{y_{0}})
), such that for
i=1,\ldots,n
, the following identity is satisfied

Je_{2i-1}=e_{2i}.

(1.17)

Define the coordinate system
\psi_{y_{0}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{B}_{0}^{\real{}^{2m}}(r_{Y})\times\mathbb{B}_{0}^{\real{}^{2(n-m)}}(r_{\perp})\to X
, for
Z=(Z_{Y},Z_{N})
,
Z_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
Z_{N}\in\real^{2(n-m)}
,
Z_{Y}=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{2m})
,
Z_{N}=(Z_{2m+1},\ldots,Z_{2n})
,
|Z_{Y}|<r_{Y}
,
|Z_{N}|<r_{\perp}
, by

\psi_{y_{0}}(Z_{Y},Z_{N})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\exp_{\exp_{y_{0}}^{Y}(Z_{Y})}^{X}(\tau_{Z_{Y}}(Z_{N})),

(1.18)

where we identified
Z_{Y}
,
Z_{N}
to elements in
T_{y_{0}}Y
,
N_{y_{0}}
, using the fixed frames
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2m})
and
(e_{2m+1},\ldots,e_{2n})
,
\tau_{Z_{Y}}(Z_{N})\in N_{\exp_{y_{0}}^{Y}(Z_{Y})}
is the parallel transport of
Z_{N}
along the geodesic
\exp_{y_{0}}^{Y}(tZ_{Y})
,
t=[0,1]
, with respect to the connection
\nabla^{N}
on
N
, and
\mathbb{B}_{0}^{\real{}^{k}}(\epsilon)
,
\epsilon>0
means the euclidean ball of radius
\epsilon
around
0\in\real^{k}
.
The coordinates
\psi_{y_{0}}
are called the
Fermi coordinates
at
y_{0}
.
Define the functions
\kappa_{X,y_{0}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{B}_{0}^{\real{}^{2m}}(r_{Y})\times\mathbb{B}_{0}^{\real{}^{2(n-m)}}(r_{\perp})\to\real
,
\kappa_{Y,y_{0}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{B}_{0}^{\real{}^{2m}}(r_{Y})\to\real
, by

\displaystyle(\psi_{y_{0}}^{*}dv_{X})(Z)=\kappa_{X,y_{0}}(Z)dZ_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dZ_{2n},

(1.19)

\displaystyle((\exp_{y_{0}}^{Y})^{*}dv_{Y})(Z_{Y})=\kappa_{Y,y_{0}}(Z_{Y})dZ_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dZ_{2m}.

Clearly, under assumptions (
1.8
), we have
\kappa_{X,y_{0}}(0)=\kappa_{Y,y_{0}}(0)=1
.
Once the point
y_{0}
is fixed, we usually omit it from the subscript of functions.

We fix an orthonormal frame
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in F_{y_{0}}
, and define
\tilde{f}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}_{r}
by the parallel transport of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
with respect to the Chern connection
\nabla^{F}
of
(F,h^{F})
, done first along the path
\psi(tZ_{Y},0)
,
t\in[0,1]
, and then along the path
\psi(Z_{Y},tZ_{N})
,
t\in[0,1]
,
Z_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
Z_{N}\in\real^{2(n-m)}
,
|Z_{Y}|<r_{Y}
,
|Z_{N}|<r_{\perp}
.
Similarly, we trivialize
L
in the neighborhood of
y_{0}
.
These frames, as well as the induced frames of the dual vector bundles, allow us to interpret
{\rm{E}}_{p}(x,y)
as an element of
\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
for
x\in X
,
y\in Y
in a
\min(r_{\perp},r_{Y})
-neighborhood of
y_{0}
.

We also define the function
\mathscr{E}_{n,m}
over
\real{}^{2n}\times\real^{2m}
as follows

\mathscr{E}_{n,m}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})=\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\big(|z_{i}|^{2}+|z^{\prime}_{i}|^{2}-2z_{i}\overline{z}^{\prime}_{i}\big)-\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}|z_{i}|^{2}\Big),

(1.20)

where
Z=(Z_{Y},Z_{N})
,
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
Z_{N}\in\real^{2(n-m)}
and
z_{i}
,
z^{\prime}_{i}
are given by
z_{i}=Z_{2i-1}+\sqrt{-1}Z_{2i}
,
z^{\prime}_{j}=Z^{\prime}_{2j-1}+\sqrt{-1}Z^{\prime}_{2j}
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
and
j=1,\ldots,m
.
As we show in Section
3.2
,
\mathscr{E}_{n,m}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})
is the Schwartz kernel of the extension operator for a model space.
For general manifolds, the extension operator is comparable to this model one, as the following theorem shows.

Theorem 1.6
.

For any
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
y_{0}\in Y
, there are
J_{r}^{E}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})\in\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
polynomials in
Z\in\real^{2n}
,
Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
, with the same parity as
r
and
\deg J_{r}^{E}\leq 3r
,
whose coefficients are polynomials in
R^{TX}
,
A
,
R^{F}
,
(dv_{X}/dv_{g^{TX}})^{\pm\frac{1}{2n}}
,
(dv_{Y}/dv_{g^{TY}})^{\pm\frac{1}{2n}}
, and their derivatives of order
\leq 2r
, all evaluated at
y_{0}
, such that for the functions
F_{r}^{E}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{r}^{E}\cdot\mathscr{E}_{n,m}
over
\real{}^{2n}\times\real^{2m}
, the following holds.

There are
\epsilon,c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k,l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
y_{0}\in Y
,
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z=(Z_{Y},Z_{N})
,
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
Z_{N}\in\real^{2(n-m)}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\leq\epsilon
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2m}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, for
Q^{1}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=6(16(n+2)(k+1)+l^{\prime})+2l
, the following bound holds

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}_{Y}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{p^{m}}{\rm{E}}_{p}\big(\psi_{y_{0}}(Z),\psi_{y_{0}}(Z^{\prime}_{Y})\big)-\sum_{r=0}^{k}p^{-\frac{r}{2}}F_{r}^{E}(\sqrt{p}Z,\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime}_{Y})\kappa_{X}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z)\kappa_{Y}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z^{\prime}_{Y})\bigg)\bigg|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(Y)}\\
\leq Cp^{-\frac{k+1-l}{2}}\Big(1+\sqrt{p}|Z|+\sqrt{p}|Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\Big)^{Q^{1}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}\exp\Big(-c\sqrt{p}\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|\big)\Big),

(1.21)

where the
\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}
-norm is taken with respect to
y_{0}
.
Also, the following identity holds

J_{0}^{E}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})={\rm{Id}}_{F_{y_{0}}}\cdot\kappa_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}(y_{0}).

(1.22)

Moreover, under assumption (
1.8
), we have

J_{1}^{E}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})={\rm{Id}}_{F_{y_{0}}}\cdot\pi\cdot g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(z_{N},A(\overline{z}_{Y}-\overline{z}^{\prime}_{Y})(\overline{z}_{Y}-\overline{z}^{\prime}_{Y})\big),

(1.23)

where we implicitly identified
Z\in\real^{2n}
to an element in
T_{y_{0}}X
as
Z\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum Z_{i}\cdot e_{i}
, similar notations have been used for
Z_{Y},Z_{N},Z^{\prime}_{Y}
, and
z_{Y},z_{N}
,
z^{\prime}_{Y}
are the induced complex coordinates.

The operator
E_{p}
is very much related to the
orthogonal Bergman projector
. The last operator is defined as the orthogonal projector onto the holomorphic sections of
L^{p}\otimes F
over
X
, which are orthogonal to those vanishing along
Y
.
To prove Theorems
1.5
,
1.6
, and out of independent interest, we establish analogous results for this projector.
More precisely, consider the vector space

H^{0,0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Big\{f\in H^{0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}f|_{Y}=0\Big\}.

(1.24)

An easy verification shows that (
1.24
) is a closed subspace.
Let
H^{0,\perp}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
be the orthogonal complement of
H^{0,0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
in
H^{0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
with respect to the
L^{2}
-scalar product.

Denote by
B_{p}^{\perp}
,
B_{p}^{0}
,
B_{p}^{X}
the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
to
H^{0,\perp}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
,
H^{0,0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
and
H^{0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
respectively.
As we shall explain in Lemma
5.14
, there is an algebraic relation between
E_{p}
and
B_{p}^{\perp}
.
Similarly to (
1.14
), we denote by
B_{p}^{\perp}(x_{1},x_{2})
,
B_{p}^{0}(x_{1},x_{2})
,
B_{p}^{X}(x_{1},x_{2})
the Schwartz kernels of
B_{p}^{\perp}
,
B_{p}^{0}
,
B_{p}^{X}
with respect to
dv_{X}
.

Theorem 1.7
.

There are
c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
, the following estimate holds

\Big|B_{p}^{\perp}(x_{1},x_{2})\Big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(X\times X)}\leq Cp^{n+\frac{k}{2}}\exp\Big(-c\sqrt{p}\big({\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})+{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},Y)+{\rm{dist}}(x_{2},Y)\big)\Big),

(1.25)

where the norm
\mathscr{C}^{k}
is interpreted in the same way as in Theorem
1.5
.

Hence, for the asymptotics of the Schwartz kernel of
B_{p}^{\perp}
, it is only left to study it in the neighborhood of a fixed point
(y_{0},y_{0})\in Y\times Y
in
X\times X
.
To state our result in this direction, we define the function
\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}
over
\real{}^{2n}\times\real^{2n}
as follows

\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})=\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(|z_{i}|^{2}+|z^{\prime}_{i}|^{2}\big)+\pi\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}\overline{z}^{\prime}_{i}\Big),

(1.26)

where
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
, and
z_{i}
,
z^{\prime}_{i}
are given by
z_{i}=Z_{2i-1}+\sqrt{-1}Z_{2i}
,
z^{\prime}_{i}=Z^{\prime}_{2i-1}+\sqrt{-1}Z^{\prime}_{2i}
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
.
As we show in Section
3.2
,
\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})
is the Schwartz kernel of the orthogonal Bergman projector for the model space.
The following theorem shows that the general situation is comparable to this model one.
We use the same trivialization of
F,F^{*}
and
L,L^{*}
in the neighborhood of
y_{0}
as in Theorem
1.6
, and interpret
B_{p}^{\perp}(x_{1},x_{2})
as an element of
\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
, for
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
in a
\min(r_{\perp},r_{Y})
-neighborhood of
y_{0}
.

Theorem 1.8
.

For any
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
y_{0}\in Y
, there are polynomials
J_{r}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
,
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
, with the same properties as in Theorem
1.6
, such that for
F_{r}^{\perp}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{r}^{\perp}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}
, the following holds.

There are
\epsilon,c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k,l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
y_{0}\in Y
,
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z=(Z_{Y},Z_{N})
,
Z^{\prime}=(Z^{\prime}_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{N})
,
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
Z_{N},Z^{\prime}_{N}\in\real^{2(n-m)}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, for
Q^{2}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=3(8(n+2)(k+1)+l^{\prime})+l
, we have

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{p^{n}}B_{p}^{\perp}\big(\psi_{y_{0}}(Z),\psi_{y_{0}}(Z^{\prime})\big)-\sum_{r=0}^{k}p^{-\frac{r}{2}}F_{r}^{\perp}(\sqrt{p}Z,\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime})\kappa_{X}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z)\kappa_{X}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(Y)}\\
\leq Cp^{-\frac{k+1-l}{2}}\Big(1+\sqrt{p}|Z|+\sqrt{p}|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q^{2}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}\exp\Big(-c\sqrt{p}\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|\big)\Big),

(1.27)

where the
\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}
-norm is taken with respect to
y_{0}
.
Also, we have

J_{0}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})={\rm{Id}}_{F_{y_{0}}}.

(1.28)

Moreover, under the assumptions (
1.8
) and notations as in (
1.23
), we have

J_{1}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})={\rm{Id}}_{F_{y_{0}}}\cdot\pi\cdot g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(z_{N}+\overline{z}^{\prime}_{N},A(Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y})(Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y})\big).

(1.29)

Remark 1.9
.

We present an algorithmic way to compute the polynomials
J_{r}^{E},J_{r}^{\perp}
.

In the final part of the introduction, we describe an application of Theorem
1.6
to the
L^{\infty}
-estimates of holomorphic extensions.
Remark that Theorem
1.6
only studies the holomorphic extensions which are optimal for the
L^{2}
-norm.
Nevertheless, as an explicit asymptotic formula is given, our theorem can be applied to study extension problems associated with other norms.

Theorem 1.10
.

There are
C>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
f\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
,

\big\|{\rm{E}}_{p}f\big\|_{L^{\infty}(X)}\leq\Big(1+\frac{C}{\sqrt{p}}\Big)\cdot\big\|f\big\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}.

(1.30)

Remark 1.11
.

a) Clearly, for any extension
\tilde{f}
of
f
to
X
, we have
\|\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(X)}\geq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}
.
Hence, the theorem above tells that
{\rm{E}}_{p}f
saturates the optimal
L^{\infty}
-bound asymptotically.

b) Theorem
1.10
refines the results of Zhang
[
44
, Theorem 2.2]
and Bost
[
8
, Proposition 3.6, Theorem A.1]
, cf. also Randriambololona
[
38
, Théorème B]
, which prove in various settings and generalities that for any
\epsilon>0
, there is
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
f\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
,
p\geq p_{1}
, there is a holomorphic extension
\tilde{f}
of
f
to
X
, verifying
\|\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(X)}\leq\exp(\epsilon p)\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}
.
As explained in
[
44
]
,
[
8
]
,
[
38
]
, these
L^{\infty}
-bounds are important in Arakelov geometry.

c)
In realms of non-Archimedean geometry, a related extension problem has been considered by Chen-Moriwaki
[
10
]
.

d) It is interesting to study to which extent the
L^{2}
-optimal extension becomes optimal for other
L^{p}
-norms,
p\in[1,+\infty[
. See Beatrous
[
1
]
for related results.

Let us finally say few words about the tools we use in this article.
The proofs of Theorems
1.5
,
1.7
rely on the exponential estimate for the Bergman kernel, cf. Ma-Marinescu
[
32
, Theorem 0.1]
, and the refinement – in our asymptotic setting – of the Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem, see Theorem
4.1
, the proof of which is inspired by Bismut-Lebeau
[
4
]
and Demailly
[
12
]
.

The proofs of Theorems
1.6
,
1.8
rely on some techniques from spectral analysis, inspired by
[
4
]
; on the existence of the so-called uniform Stein atlases (see Definition
2.21
) over Kähler manifolds of bounded geometry, which we establish using Hörmander’s
L^{2}
-estimates; on the full off-diagonal asymptotic expansion of the Bergman kernel due to Dai-Liu-Ma
[
11
]
, and on some technical results about the algebras of operators with Taylor-type expansion of the Schwartz kernel, which are inspired by the work of Ma-Marinescu
[
31
]
, cf.
[
30
, §7]
.
The general strategy for dealing with semi-classical limits here is inspired by Bismut
[
3
]
and Bismut-Vasserot
[
5
]
.

The technical novelty of this paper, compared to
[
11
]
, is that, unlike
B_{p}^{X}
, the operator
B_{p}^{\perp}

is not
the spectral projector associated to the Kodaira Laplacian.
This breaks apart most of the techniques used in
[
11
]
,
[
30
]
, as, for example, the relation with the heat kernel is no longer available.
To remedy this, instead of Laplacian, we construct an “ad hoc” operator, see (
5.2
), based on the restriction map and
B_{p}^{X}
, so that
B_{p}^{\perp}

is
the spectral projector associated to this new operator.

A similar idea of using spectral theory of operators other than Laplacian in the study of Bergman kernel has been used in symplectic reduction setting by Ma-Zhang
[
33
]
.
The role of normal direction in the decay of Schwartz kernels as in Theorems
1.6
and
1.8
was already present in
[
33
]
, even though the contexts of the two problems are completely different.
In their manuscript, authors use the deformation of Laplacian by the Casimir operator, coming from Hamiltonian action of a compact Lie group.
This deformation, differently from our setting, is a differential operator itself.
This makes technical details of our article different, as here the spectral theory is applied to an operator, which is no longer local, and actually has a smooth Schwartz kernel.

We note that Theorems
1.7
,
1.8
can be reformulated in terms of the so-called
logarithmic Bergman kernel
, which corresponds to the Schwartz kernel of
B_{p}^{0}
.
This is due to the obvious relation
B_{p}^{X}=B_{p}^{0}+B_{p}^{\perp}
, and the fact that the Schwartz kernel of
B_{p}^{X}
is already well-understood by the results of Tian
[
42
]
, Catlin
[
9
]
, Zelditch
[
43
]
, Dai-Liu-Ma
[
11
]
and Ma-Marinescu
[
32
]
.

This paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we recall the definitions of manifolds (resp. pairs of manifolds, vector bundles) of bounded geometry.
We compare Fermi coordinates to geodesic coordinates.
We then introduce and study the notion of a uniform Stein atlases.
In Section
3
, we prove that the set of operators over manifolds of bonded geometry, admitting certain bounds on the Schwartz kernels, forms an algebra under the composition.
In Section
4
, we establish a spectral bound for the restriction operator.
Finally, in Section
5
, by using all the above results, we establish the results announced in this section.

Notations.

We use notations
X,Y
for complex manifolds and
M,H
for real manifolds.
The complex (resp. real) dimensions of
X,Y
(resp.
M,H
) are denoted here by
n,m
.
An operator
\iota
always means an embedding
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Y\to X
(resp.
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H\to M
).
We denote by
{\rm{Res}}_{Y}
(resp.
{\rm{Res}}_{H}
) the operator of restriction of sections of a certain vector bundle over
X
to
Y
(resp.
M
to
H
).

For a Riemannian manifold
(M,g^{TM})
, we denote the Levi-Civita connection by
\nabla^{TM}
, and by
dv_{g^{TM}}
the Riemannian volume form.
For a closed subset
W\subset M
,
r\geq 0
, let
\mathbb{B}_{W}^{M}(r)
be the geodesic tubular neighborhood of radius
r
around
W
.
For a Hermitian vector bundle
(E,h^{E})
, note

\mathbb{B}_{r}(E)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{Z\in E\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}|Z|_{h^{E}}<r\}
.

For a fixed volume form
dv_{M}
on
M
, we denote by
L^{2}(dv_{M},h^{E})
the space of
L^{2}
-sections of
E
with respect to
dv_{M}
and
h^{E}
. When
dv_{M}=dv_{g^{TM}}
, we also use the notation
L^{2}(g^{TM},h^{E})
. When there is no confusion about the data, we also use the simplified notation
L^{2}(M)
or even just
L^{2}
.

We denote by
dv_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}
the standard volume form on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
We view
\mathbb{C}^{m}
(resp.
2m
) embedded in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
(resp.
2n
) by the first
m
coordinates.
For
Z\in\real^{k}
, we denote by
Z_{l}
,
l=1,\ldots,k
, the coordinates of
Z
.
If
Z\in\real^{2n}
, we denote by
z_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
the induced complex coordinates
z_{i}=Z_{2i-1}+\sqrt{-1}Z_{2i}
.
We frequently use the decomposition
Z=(Z_{Y},Z_{N})
, where
Z_{Y}=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{2m})
and
Z_{N}=(Z_{2m+1},\ldots,Z_{2n})
.
For a fixed frame
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n})
in
T_{y}X
,
y\in Y
, we implicitly identify
Z
(resp.
Z_{Y}
,
Z_{N}
) to an element in
T_{y}X
(resp.
T_{y}Y
,
N_{y}
) by

Z=\sum_{i=1}^{2n}Z_{i}e_{i},\quad Z_{Y}=\sum_{i=1}^{2m}Z_{i}e_{i},\quad Z_{N}=\sum_{i=2m+1}^{2n}Z_{i}e_{i}.

(1.31)

If the frame
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2n})
satisfies (
1.17
), we denote
\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{1}{2}(e_{2i-1}-\sqrt{-1}e_{2i})
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{1}{2}(e_{2i-1}+\sqrt{-1}e_{2i})
, and identify
z,\overline{z}
to vectors in
T_{y}X\otimes\mathbb{C}
as follows

z=\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}},\qquad\qquad\overline{z}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{z}_{i}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}.

(1.32)

Clearly, in this identification, we have
Z=z+\overline{z}
,
({\rm{Id}}-\sqrt{-1}J)Z=2z
, and
({\rm{Id}}+\sqrt{-1}J)Z=2\overline{z}
.
We define
z_{Y},\overline{z}_{Y}\in T_{y}Y\otimes\mathbb{C}
,
z_{N},\overline{z}_{N}\in N_{y}\otimes\mathbb{C}
in a similar way.

For
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
,
B=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
, we note

|\alpha|=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i},\quad\alpha!=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}!,\quad B^{\alpha}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}.

(1.33)

Acknowledgements.
Author would like to warmly thank Jean-Pierre Demailly, the numerous enlightening discussions with whom inspired this article.
We also thank Jingzhou Sun for his interest in this article and for pointing out several misprints.
Finally, we would like to thank the anonymous referees for many helpful comments.

2
Manifolds of bounded geometry and uniform Stein atlases

We recall the definitions of manifolds (resp. pairs of manifolds, vector bundles) of bounded geometry and study some of their properties.
Then, we introduce and study uniform Stein atlases.

Manifolds of bounded geometry are certain complete manifolds for which some uniform boundness conditions on the curvature and injectivity radii are assumed.
The reason for considering those types of manifolds in this article is twofold.

First, they appear naturally already in the study of compact manifolds.
This is due to the fact that our main philosophy here is to reduce all the statements from general manifolds to
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
As we would like our theory to work with
\mathbb{C}^{n}
as well, which is no longer compact, the setting of compact manifolds is not appropriate for our needs.
It turns out that the class of manifolds of bounded geometry is wide enough to contain both compact manifolds and
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and restrictive enough to satisfy some essential estimates in our approach.

Second, the full force of Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem comes from the fact that it can be applied to local problems over general weakly pseudoconvex domains.
Also, many geometric applications of the extension theorem, as
[
15
]
(which partially motivated the current article), are formulated for non-compact manifolds, see Remark
1.4
.
As we would like our theorems to be useful in these contexts as well, we need to abandon the compactness assumption.

This section is organized as follows.
In Section
2.1
, we recall the definitions of manifolds (resp. pairs of manifolds, vector bundles) of bounded geometry.
In Section
2.2
, we compare the geodesic and Fermi coordinates.
In Section
2.3
, we calculate the holonomy of the vector bundle along the paths, adapted to the two coordinate systems.
In Section
2.4
, we give a formula for the complex structure in Fermi coordinates and study quasi-plurisubharmonicity of some functions on manifolds of bounded geometry.
Finally, in Section
2.5
, we introduce and study uniform Stein atlases, and prove that any Kähler manifold of bounded geometry admits uniform Stein atlas.

A reader, willing to understand only the compact case, might safely skip Sections
2.1
and
2.5
, and skim through most of Section
2.4
, as for compact manifolds, the statements are well-known.

2.1
Coordinate-free and coordinate-wise descriptions of bounded geometry

In this section, we recall the definitions of manifolds (resp. pairs of manifolds, vector bundles) of bounded geometry.
There are mainly two ways to define objects of bounded geometry.
Either one uses some bounds on the curvature (and related objects) – this is the coordinate-free description – or one uses charts constructed by geodesics and bounds the relevant structures (as the metric tensor) and its derivatives in these coordinates – the coordinate-wise description.
The equivalence of these two perspectives is established in the works of Eichhorn
[
17
]
, Schick
[
39
]
and Große-Schneider
[
23
]
.
In this section, we recall these statements precisely.

Definition 2.1
.

We say that a Riemannian manifold
(M,g^{TM})
is of bounded geometry if the following two conditions hold.

(i)
The injectivity radius of
(M,g^{TM})
is bounded below by a positive constant
r_{M}
.

(ii)
Every covariant derivative of the Riemann curvature tensor
R^{TM}
of
M
is bounded, i.e. for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a constant
C_{k}>0
such that for any
l=0,\ldots,k
, we have

|(\nabla^{TM})^{l}R^{TM}|\leq C_{k},

(2.1)

where
\nabla^{TM}
is the connection induced by the Levi-Civita connection, and the pointwise norm is taken with respect to
g^{TM}
.

Remark 2.2
.

The condition
(i)
from Definition
2.1
implies that
(M,g^{TM})
is complete.

Let us fix
x_{0}\in M
and an orthonormal frame
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
of
(T_{x_{0}}M,g^{TM}_{x_{0}})
.
We identify
n
to
T_{x_{0}}M
implicitly as in (
1.31
).
We introduce the map
\phi_{x_{0}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\real^{n}\to M
,
x_{0}\in M
, as follows

\phi_{x_{0}}(Z)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\exp^{M}_{x_{0}}(Z).

(2.2)

As a general rule, whenever the point
x_{0}
is implicit, we drop it out from the subscript.
As it was proved, for example, in
[
17
, Theorem A]
and
[
39
, Proposition 3.3]
, the metric tensor, written in geodesic coordinates, has bounded derivatives, and the transition maps between two different geodesic coordinates can be bounded uniformly in a similar way. This gives us
the coordinate-wise approach
through geodesic coordinates to manifolds of bounded geometry.

Now, let
(H,g^{TH})
be an embedded submanifold of
(M,g^{TM})
,
g^{TH}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=g^{TM}|_{H}
.
We identify the normal bundle
N
of
H
in
M
as an orthogonal complement of
TH
in
TM
as in (
1.6
).
We denote by
g^{N}
the metric on
N
induced by
g^{TM}
, and define
\nabla^{N}
as after (
1.6
).
We denote by
A
the second fundamental form of the embedding of
H
in
M
, defined as in (
1.16
).

Definition 2.3
.

We say that the triple
(M,H,g^{TM})
is of bounded geometry if the following conditions are fulfilled.

(i)
The manifold
(M,g^{TM})
is of bounded geometry.

(ii)
The injectivity radius of
(H,g^{TH})
is bounded below by a positive constant
r_{H}
.

(iii)
There is a collar around
H
(a tubular neighborhood of fixed radius), i.e. there is
r_{\perp}>0
such that for any
x,y\in H
, the normal geodesic balls
B^{\perp}_{r_{\perp}}(x),B^{\perp}_{r_{\perp}}(y)
, obtained by the application of the exponential mapping to vectors, orthogonal to
H
, of norm, bounded by
r_{\perp}
, are disjoint.

(iv)
Every covariant derivative of
A
of
M
is bounded, i.e. for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a constant
C_{k}>0
such that for any
l=0,\ldots,k
, we have

|\nabla^{l}A|\leq C_{k},

(2.3)

where
\nabla
is the connection induced by the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{TH}
and
\nabla^{N}
, and the pointwise norm is taken with respect to
g^{TM}
.

Clearly, condition
(iii)
from Definition
2.3
means that the map
\mathbb{B}_{r_{\perp}}(N)\to M
,
(y,Z_{N})\mapsto\exp^{M}_{y}(Z_{N})
,
y\in H
,
Z_{N}\in N_{y}
,
|Z_{N}|<r_{\perp}
, is a diffeomorphism onto a tubular neighborhood
U\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{B}_{H}^{M}(r_{\perp})
of
H
in
M
.
We define the projection

\pi_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U\to H,\qquad\qquad\exp^{M}_{y}(Z_{N})\mapsto y.

(2.4)

We now fix a point
y_{0}\in H
and an orthonormal frame
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{m})
(resp.
(e_{m+1},\ldots,e_{n})
) in
(T_{y_{0}}H,g^{TH})
(resp. in
(N,g^{N})
).
We define the
Fermi coordinates
,
\psi_{y_{0}}
, at
y_{0}
as in (
1.18
).
Again, as both manifolds
(M,g^{TM})
,
(H,g^{TH})
, are complete, one can extend the domain of
\psi_{x_{0}}
to
n
.
As it was established, for example in
[
39
, Lemma 3.9]
and
[
23
, Theorem 4.9]
, the derivatives of the metric tensor in
\psi_{x_{0}}
coordinates are uniformly bounded. Similarly, the derivatives of the transition maps are uniformly bounded.
From this, we see directly that the function
\kappa_{N}
, defined as in (
1.7
), is uniformly bounded on the submanifold.

Define
R>0
as follows

R\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\min\Big\{\frac{r_{M}}{2},\frac{r_{H}}{4},\frac{r_{\perp}}{4}\Big\}.

(2.5)

Now, there is a diffeomorphism
h_{y_{0}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{B}_{0}^{\real{}^{n}}(R)\to\real^{n}
, such that the following holds

\psi_{y_{0}}=\phi_{y_{0}}\circ h_{y_{0}}.

(2.6)

As it was established, for example, in
[
23
, Lemma 4.7]
, the derivatives of
h_{y_{0}}
are then uniformly bounded. All in all, this gives the
coordinate-wise approach
through Fermi coordinates to triples of bounded geometry.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
(E,\nabla^{E},h^{E})
be a Hermitian vector bundle with a fixed Hermitian connection over a manifold
(M,g^{TM})
of bounded geometry.
We say that
(E,\nabla^{E},h^{E})
is of bounded geometry if for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a constant
C_{k}>0
such that for any
l=0,\ldots,k
, we have

|\nabla^{l}R^{E}|<C_{k},

(2.7)

where
\nabla
is the connection induced by the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{TM}
and
\nabla^{E}
, and the pointwise norm is taken with respect to
g^{TM}
.

If
(E,h^{E})
is a Hermitian vector bundle over a
complex manifold
, we say that it is of bounded geometry if
(E,\nabla^{E},h^{E})
is of bounded geometry for the Chern connection
\nabla^{E}
on
(E,h^{E})
.

Let us now give the coordinate-wise description for vector bundles of bounded geometry.
Let us first construct a trivialization of vector bundle
(E,\nabla^{E})
,
{\rm{rk}}(E)=r
, as follows.
We fix a point
x_{0}\in M
and an orthonormal frame
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in E_{x_{0}}
.
Let
\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{r}
be a frame of
E
over
\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{M}(r_{M})
, obtained by the parallel transport of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
along the curve
\phi_{x_{0}}(tZ)
,
t\in[0,1]
,
Z\in T_{x_{0}}M
,
|Z|<r_{M}
.
Clearly, as
\nabla^{E}
is Hermitian,
\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{r}
is an orthonormal frame over
\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{M}(r_{M})
.
We denote by
\Gamma^{E}{}^{\prime}
the connection form of
(E,\nabla^{E})
with respect to this frame.
As it was established, for example in
[
17
, Theorem B]
, the derivatives of
\Gamma^{E}{}^{\prime}
are uniformly bounded for vector bundles of bounded geometry.

Now, let us consider another trivialization of
(E,\nabla^{E})
.
We place ourselves in a setting where
(M,H,g^{TM})
is a triple of bounded geometry.
We fix a point
y_{0}\in H
and an orthonormal frame
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in E_{y_{0}}
.
We define
\tilde{f}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}_{r}
by the parallel transport as it was done before Theorem
1.6
.
We denote by
\Gamma^{E}
the connection form of
(E,\nabla^{E})
with respect to this frame.
As it was established, for example in
[
23
, Lemma 5.13]
, the derivatives of
\Gamma^{E}{}
are uniformly bounded for triples of bounded geometry.
Let
\xi_{E}
be the function, defined in
\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{M}(R)
, with values in
\text{End}{(\mathbb{C}^{r})}
, such that

(\tilde{f}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}_{r})=\exp(\xi_{E})\cdot(\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{r}),

(2.8)

where we view
(\tilde{f}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}_{r})
and
(\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{r})
as
r\times 1
matrices.
Clearly, for triples of bounded geometry, the derivatives of
\xi_{E}
are uniformly bounded.

2.2
Diffeomorphism between Fermi and geodesic coordinates

In this section, we study the Taylor expansion of the diffeomorphism comparing geodesic and Fermi coordinates.

We conserve the notations from Section
2.1
and we place ourselves in the setting of a triple
(M,H,g^{TM})
of bounded geometry.
Let
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(H,T^{*}H\otimes\text{End}{(TM|_{H})})
be as in (
1.16
).
We define an auxiliary form
B\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(H,{\rm{Sym}}^{2}(T^{*}M|_{H})\otimes TM|_{H})
in the notations (
1.16
) by

B(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=B(u,u)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{1}{2}A(P^{H}u)P^{H}u+A(P^{H}u)P^{N}u,\qquad u\in TM|_{H},

(2.9)

where
P^{H}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}TM|_{H}\to TH
is the orthogonal projection.

We fix a point
y_{0}\in H
and an orthonormal frame
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{m})
(resp.
(e_{m+1},\ldots,e_{n})
) in
(T_{y_{0}}H,g^{TH})
(resp. in
(N,g^{N})
).
Recall that in (
1.18
) and (
2.2
), we defined two coordinate systems
\psi_{y_{0}}
,
\phi_{y_{0}}
in a neighborhood of
y_{0}
, and in (
2.6
), we defined a diffeomorphism
h_{y_{0}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{B}_{0}^{\real{}^{n}}(R)\to U\subset\real^{n}
, for
R
from (
2.5
) and a certain open subset
U
.
We drop out
y_{0}
from the subscripts from now on.
The main goal of this section is to study the Taylor expansion of
h
at
0\in\real^{2n}
.

Proposition 2.5
.

The diffeomorphism
h
has the following Taylor expansion

h(Z)=Z+B(Z)+O(|Z|^{3}).

(2.10)

Moreover, the coefficients of order
r\in\mathbb{N}
in the above Taylor expansion can be expressed in terms of
R^{TM}
,
A
, and their derivatives up to order
r-2
with respect to
\nabla^{TM}
, evaluated at
y_{0}
.

Before presenting the proof of Proposition
2.5
, let us prove some auxiliary results.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\gamma(t)
be a geodesic in
(M,g^{TM})
, and let
v\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\gamma^{\prime}(t)
. Then for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the following identity between operators on smooth functions on
M
holds

v^{\otimes k}\cdot(\nabla^{TM})^{\otimes k}=\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\Big)^{k},

(2.11)

where we view
(\nabla^{TM})^{\otimes k}
as an operator
(\nabla^{TM})^{\otimes k}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M)\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M,(T^{*}M)^{\otimes k})
, and
\cdot
on the left-hand side of (
2.11
) means the contraction.

Proof.

The proof is a consequence of the simple fact that
\nabla_{v}^{TM}v=0
and the formula

(\nabla^{TM}\alpha)(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k+1})=X_{1}\alpha(X_{2},\ldots,X_{k+1})-\sum_{i=2}^{k+1}\alpha(X_{2},\ldots,\nabla^{TM}_{X_{1}}X_{i},\ldots,X_{k+1}),

(2.12)

where
\alpha
is a
k
-form and
X_{1},\ldots,X_{k+1}
are some vector fields.
∎

Corollary 2.7
.

Let
u
be a smooth function on
M
. Then for any smooth function
u
on
M
on the level of formal Taylor expansions, the following identity holds

u\big(\exp^{M}_{y_{0}}(Z)\big)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}Z^{\otimes k}\cdot(\nabla^{TM})^{k}u(y_{0}).

(2.13)

Proof.

We have the following formal identity

u\big(\exp^{M}_{y_{0}}(Z)\big)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}u\big(\exp^{M}_{y_{0}}(tZ)\big).

(2.14)

The proof now is a direct consequence of Lemma
2.6
.
∎

Both Lemma
2.6
and Corollary
2.7
appeared in Gavrilov
[
20
, §2]
.
Let us now define the connection
\nabla^{a}
on
TM|_{H}
as follows

\nabla^{a}=\nabla^{TH}\oplus\nabla^{N},

(2.15)

where we used the notation as in (
1.16
).

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\gamma(t)
be a geodesic in
(H,g^{TH})
, and let
v\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\gamma^{\prime}(t)
. Let
Z_{1}(t),\ldots,Z_{l}(t)\in N
,
l\in\mathbb{N}
be vector fields along
\gamma(t)
, which are parallel with respect to
\nabla^{N}
. Then for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the following identity between operators on smooth functions on
M
holds

\Big(v^{\otimes k}\cdot(\nabla^{TH})^{\otimes k}\Big)\circ{\rm{Res}}_{H}\circ\Big((Z_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes Z_{l})\cdot(\nabla^{TM})^{\otimes l}\Big)\\
={\rm{Res}}_{H}\circ\Big((v^{\otimes k}\otimes Z_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes Z_{l})\cdot(\nabla^{a})^{\otimes k}(\nabla^{TM})^{\otimes l}\Big),

(2.16)

where the compositions of the connections are interpreted in the same way as in Lemma
2.6
, and
{\rm{Res}}_{H}
is a map restricting sections over
M
to sections over
H
.

Proof.

The proof is a direct consequence of
\nabla^{a}_{v}Z_{i}=\nabla^{N}_{v}Z_{i}=0
,
\nabla_{v}^{TH}v=0
and (
2.12
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.5
.

The main idea is to fix a smooth function
u
on
M
and to use Lemmas
2.6
,
2.8
to get two different expressions for the Taylor expansion of
u(\psi(Z))
.
By comparing the two expansions, we will get the Taylor expansion (
2.10
).

In one way, since
\psi(Z_{H},Z_{N})=\phi(h(Z_{H},Z_{N}))
, we may apply Lemma
2.6
to get

u(\psi(Z_{H},Z_{N}))=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\Big(h(Z_{H},Z_{N})^{\otimes k}\cdot(\nabla^{TM})^{k}u\Big)(y_{0}).

(2.17)

In another way, let us first apply Lemma
2.6
to get

u\big(\psi(Z_{H},Z_{N})\big)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\Big(\tau_{Z_{H}}(Z_{N})^{\otimes k}\cdot(\nabla^{TM})^{k}u\Big)\big(\psi(Z_{H},0)\big),

(2.18)

where
\tau_{Z_{H}}(Z_{N})
is the parallel transport of
Z_{N}
along the curve
\exp_{y_{0}}^{H}(tZ_{H})
,
t\in[0,1]
, with respect to
\nabla^{N}
.
We now apply Lemma
2.6
again, but this time on
H
, to get

\Big(\tau_{Z_{H}}(Z_{N})^{\otimes k}\cdot(\nabla^{TM})^{\otimes k}u\Big)\big(\psi(Z_{H},0)\big)\\
=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}Z_{H}^{\otimes l}\cdot(\nabla^{TH})^{\otimes l}\Big(\tau_{Z_{H}}(Z_{N})^{\otimes k}\cdot(\nabla^{TM})^{\otimes k}u\Big)(y_{0}).

(2.19)

Finally, let us apply Lemma
2.8
, (
2.18
) and (
2.19
), to get

u(\psi(Z_{H},Z_{N}))=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{l!}\frac{1}{k!}\Big((Z_{H}^{\otimes l}\otimes Z_{N}^{\otimes k})\cdot(\nabla^{a})^{\otimes l}(\nabla^{TM})^{\otimes k}u\Big)(y_{0}).

(2.20)

Now, we denote by
h^{[r]}(Z)
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, the homogeneous polynomial in
(Z)
of degree
r
such that the Taylor expansion of
h(Z)
is given by
\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}h^{[r]}(Z)
. Let us now take the homogeneous parts of (
2.17
) and compare them with (
2.20
).
The comparison of the first degree gives us

\big(h^{[1]}(Z)u\big)(y_{0})=(Zu)(y_{0}).

(2.21)

By comparing now the second degree, we get

\Big(h^{[2]}(Z_{H},Z_{N})\cdot\nabla^{TM}\Big)u(y_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\Big(h^{[1]}(Z_{H},Z_{N})^{\otimes 2}\cdot(\nabla^{TM})^{\otimes 2}\Big)u(y_{0})\\
=\Big(\frac{1}{2}Z_{H}^{\otimes 2}\cdot(\nabla^{a})^{\otimes 2}+Z_{H}\otimes Z_{N}\cdot\nabla^{a}\nabla^{TM}+\frac{1}{2}Z_{N}^{\otimes 2}\cdot(\nabla^{TM})^{\otimes 2}\Big)u(y_{0}).

(2.22)

From (
1.16
), (
2.12
), (
2.22
) and the fact that
\nabla^{TM}
has no torsion, we deduce

h^{[2]}(Z_{H},Z_{N})=\frac{1}{2}A_{y_{0}}(Z_{H})Z_{H}+A_{y_{0}}(Z_{H})Z_{N}.

(2.23)

From (
2.21
) and (
2.23
), we deduce (
2.10
).

Now, by the definition of curvature, we also have

U\otimes V\otimes W\cdot(\nabla^{TM})^{\otimes 3}=U\otimes W\otimes V\cdot(\nabla^{TM})^{\otimes 3}\\
=V\otimes U\otimes W\cdot(\nabla^{TM})^{\otimes 3}-R^{TM}(U,V)W\cdot\nabla^{TM}.

(2.24)

From (
2.17
), (
2.20
) and (
2.24
), we see that the coefficients of
h^{[r]}(Z_{H},Z_{N})
can be expressed in terms of
A,R^{TM}
, and their derivatives up to order
r-2
.
∎

2.3
Holonomy along the paths adapted to the two coordinate systems

This section is devoted to the comparison of two different trivializations of vector bundles, done in a neighborhood of a submanifold.
One is for the parallel transport adapted to the Fermi coordinate system, another one is for the geodesic coordinates.

We conserve the notations and assumptions from Section
2.2
.
Let
(E,\nabla^{E},h^{E})
be a Hermitian vector bundle of bounded geometry and rank
r
over
(M,g^{TM})
.
We fix
y_{0}\in H
and an orthonormal frame
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in(E_{y_{0}},h^{E}_{y_{0}})
.
Recall that in Section
2.1
, over
\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{M}(R)
, for
R
defined as in (
2.5
), using the parallel transports, we defined two orthonormal frames
\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{r}
and
\tilde{f}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}_{r}
.
In (
2.8
), we defined the matrix function
\xi_{E}
which relates them.

Proposition 2.9
.

The following bound holds

\xi_{E}(\psi(Z))=O(|Z|^{2}).

(2.25)

If, moreover, we assume that
(E,\nabla^{E},h^{E})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(L,\nabla^{L},h^{L})
is a line bundle, and that there is a skew-adjoint endomorphism
Q
of
TM
, which is parallel with respect to
\nabla^{TM}
(i.e.
\nabla^{TM}Q=0
), which commutes with
A
, the restriction of which to
H
respects the decomposition (
1.6
), and such that for the curvature
R^{L}
of
\nabla^{L}
, and for any
u,v\in TM
, we have

\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}R^{L}(u,v)=g^{TM}(Qu,v),

(2.26)

then the following more precise bound holds

\xi_{L}(\psi(Z))=-\frac{1}{6}R^{L}_{y_{0}}\big(Z,B(Z)\big)+O(|Z|^{4}).

(2.27)

Moreover, the coefficients of order
r
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, in above Taylor expansions can be expressed in terms of
R^{TM}
,
R^{E}
,
R^{L}
,
A
, and their derivatives up to order
r-2
with respect to
\nabla^{TM}
,
\nabla^{E}
,
\nabla^{L}
, at
y_{0}
.

Remark 2.10
.

Assume
(M,g^{TM})
is endowed with a complex structure
J
, and
g^{TM}
is invariant under the action of it.
Assume, moreover, that (
2.26
) holds for
Q\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J
as in (
1.2
).
Then all the requirements are satisfied for
Q\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J
.
This is because the invariance of
g^{TM}
under the action of
J
, and the fact that
H
is a complex submanifold, imply that the restriction of
J
to
H
respects the decomposition (
1.6
).
Also (
2.26
) implies that
(M,J,g^{TM})
is Kähler, which implies that
J
is parallel with respect to
\nabla^{TM}
, cf.
[
30
, Theorem 1.2.8]
.
Finally, by the definition of
A
and the fact that
J|_{H}
preserves
TH
and
N
, we see that
J
commutes with
A
.
Proposition
2.9
will only be applied in this particular situation.

The proof is given in the end of this section.
Before, let us state some auxiliary results.

Lemma 2.11

(
[
30
, Lemma 1.2.3]
or
[
2
, p. 38]
)
.

Let
\tilde{t}^{\prime}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
be the vector fields, constructed by the parallel transport of
e_{i}
with respect to
\nabla^{TM}
along the curve
\phi(tZ)
,
t\in[0,1]
,
Z\in T_{y_{0}}M
,
|Z|<r_{M}
.
Then

\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial Z_{i}}=\tilde{t}^{\prime}_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}O(|Z|^{2})\tilde{t}^{\prime}_{j},

(2.28)

Moreover, the coefficients of order
r
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, of the Taylor expansion (
2.28
) can be expressed in terms of
R^{TM}
, and their derivatives up to order
r-2
with respect to
\nabla^{TM}
, evaluated at
y_{0}
.

We denote by
e_{H},e^{\prime}_{H}\in TM
(resp.
e_{N},e^{\prime}_{N}
) the vector fields
\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial Z_{i}}
for
i=1,\ldots,m
(resp.
i=m+1,\ldots,n
).

Lemma 2.12
.

For any endomorphism
T
of
TM
, which is parallel with respect to
\nabla^{TM}
, which commutes with
A
, the restriction of which to
H
respects the decomposition (
1.6
), we have

\displaystyle g^{TM}_{\psi(Z_{H},Z_{N})}(Te_{N},e^{\prime}_{N})=g^{TM}_{y_{0}}(Te_{N},e^{\prime}_{N})+O(|Z|^{2}),

(2.29)

\displaystyle g^{TM}_{\psi(Z_{H},Z_{N})}(Te_{N},e_{H})=O(|Z|^{2}).

\displaystyle g^{TM}_{\psi(Z_{H},Z_{N})}(Te_{H},e^{\prime}_{H})=g^{TM}_{y_{0}}(Te_{H},e^{\prime}_{H})+g^{TM}_{y_{0}}\big(TA(e_{H})Z_{N},e^{\prime}_{H}\big)

\displaystyle+g^{TM}_{y_{0}}\big(Te_{H},A(e^{\prime}_{H})Z_{N}\big)+O(|Z|^{2}).

Also, the following identity holds

g^{TM}_{\psi(Z_{H},0)}(Te_{N},e^{\prime}_{N})=g^{TM}_{y_{0}}(Te_{N},e^{\prime}_{N}).

(2.30)

Moreover, the coefficients of order
r
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, in above Taylor expansions can be expressed in terms of
R^{TM}
,
A
, their derivatives up to order
r-2
with respect to
\nabla^{TM}
,
\nabla^{L}
, evaluated at
y_{0}
, and endomorphism
T_{y_{0}}
.

Remark 2.13
.

For
H=\{y_{0}\}
and
T={\rm{Id}}
, the result follows from
[
2
, Proposition 1.28]
.
Similar calculations were done by Ma-Zhang in
[
33
]
and Bismut-Lebeau
[
4
]
.

Proof.

First of all, since
T
is parallel with respect to
\nabla^{TM}
, we see that

\frac{\partial}{\partial e_{H}}g^{TM}_{\psi(Z_{H},0)}(Te_{N},e^{\prime}_{N})=g^{TM}_{\psi(Z_{H},0)}(T\nabla^{TM}_{e_{H}}e_{N},e^{\prime}_{N})+g^{TM}_{\psi(Z_{H},0)}(Te_{N},\nabla^{TM}_{e_{H}}e^{\prime}_{N}).

(2.31)

However, by the definition of
\psi
, see (
1.18
), the restrictions of
e_{N}
,
e^{\prime}_{N}
to
H
are parallel with respect to
\nabla^{N}
.
By this, the fact that the form
A
exchanges
N
and
TH
, and the endomorphism
T
preserves the decomposition (
1.6
), we deduce that

\frac{\partial}{\partial e_{H}}g^{TM}_{\psi(Z_{H},0)}(Te_{N},e^{\prime}_{N})=0,

which readily implies (
2.30
).

Recall that the vector fields
\tilde{t}^{\prime}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, were introduced in Lemma
2.11
.
As
T
is parallel,
T\tilde{t}^{\prime}_{i}
is equal to the parallel transport of
Te_{i}
, along the same curve as the one used in the definition of
\tilde{t}^{\prime}_{i}
.
From this and (
2.28
), we deduce that

g^{TM}_{\psi(Z_{H},Z_{N})}\Big(T\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial Z_{i}},\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial Z_{j}}\Big)=g^{TM}_{y_{0}}\big(Te_{i},e_{j}\big)+O(|Z|^{2}).

(2.32)

Now, we see that Proposition
2.5
, along with the fact that
A
exchanges
N
with
TH
, implies that for
i=1,\ldots,m
and
j=m+1,\ldots,n
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial Z_{j}}=\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial Z_{j}}+\sum_{l=1}^{m}\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial Z_{l}}g^{TM}_{y_{0}}\big(A(Z_{H})e_{j},e_{l}\big)+O(|Z|^{2}),

(2.33)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial Z_{i}}=\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial Z_{i}}+\sum_{l=1}^{m}\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial Z_{l}}g^{TM}_{y_{0}}\big(A(e_{i})Z_{N},e_{l}\big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=m+1}^{n}\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial Z_{l}}g^{TM}_{y_{0}}\big(A\big(e_{i}\big)Z_{H},e_{l}\big)+O(|Z|^{2}).

From this, Proposition
2.5
and (
2.32
), we establish the first equation in (
2.29
) and the remark after it.
We also deduce that

g^{TM}_{\psi(Z_{H},Z_{N})}(Te_{N},e_{H})=g^{TM}_{y_{0}}(Te_{N},e_{H})\\
+g^{TM}_{y_{0}}(TA(Z_{H})e_{N},e_{H})+g^{TM}_{y_{0}}(Te_{N},A(e_{H})Z_{H})+O(|Z|^{2}).

(2.34)

Now, since both
\nabla^{TM}
and
\nabla^{TH}
have no torsion, we have

A(u)v=A(v)u,\quad\text{for any}\quad u,v\in TH.

(2.35)

From (
2.35
) and the fact that
A
is skew-adjoint and commutes with
T
, we deduce the second part of (
2.29
).
The third part of (
2.29
) follows directly from (
2.32
) and (
2.33
).
∎

Recall that the projection
\pi_{0}
and the identification of
E
to
\pi_{0}^{*}(E|_{H})
in a tubular neighborhood
U
of
H
were defined before (
1.9
).
We define the
1
-form
\Gamma^{E}
with values in
\text{End}{(\pi_{0}^{*}(E|_{H}))}
as follows

\Gamma^{E}=\nabla^{E}-\pi_{0}^{*}(\nabla^{E}|_{H}),

(2.36)

where we implicitly used the above isomorphism.
We introduce similar notations for
L
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Under the same assumptions as in Proposition
2.9
, the following holds

\displaystyle\Gamma^{E}_{\psi(Z)}(e_{N})=\frac{1}{2}R^{E}_{\pi_{0}(\psi(Z))}(Z_{N},e_{N})+O(|Z|^{2}),\,\Gamma^{E}_{\psi(Z)}(e_{H})=R^{E}_{\pi_{0}(\psi(Z))}(Z_{N},e_{H})+O(|Z|^{2}),

(2.37)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{L}_{\psi(Z)}(e_{N})=\frac{1}{2}R^{L}_{\pi_{0}(\psi(Z))}(Z_{N},e_{N})+O(|Z|^{3}),\,\Gamma^{L}_{\psi(Z)}(e_{H})=O(|Z|^{3}).

Moreover, the coefficients of order
r
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, in above Taylor expansions can be expressed in terms of
R^{TM}
,
R^{L}
,
R^{E}
,
A
, and their derivatives up to order
r-2
with respect to
\nabla^{TM}
,
\nabla^{L}
,
\nabla^{E}
,
\nabla^{a}
, evaluated at
y_{0}
.

Remark 2.15
.

Similar identities were obtained in
[
4
, (13.65)-(13.66)]
.

Proof.

We follow closely the proof from
[
2
, Proposition 1.18]
, cf.
[
30
, Lemma 1.2.4]
, which establishes Lemma
2.14
for
H=\{y_{0}\}
.
First of all, by (
2.36
), we have

(\pi_{0}^{*}(\nabla^{E}|_{H})\Gamma^{E})+\Gamma^{E}\wedge\Gamma^{E}+\pi_{0}^{*}(R^{E}|_{H})=R^{E}.

(2.38)

We denote by
\mathcal{R}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(N,TN)
the tautological section of
TN
.
As we identified a neighborhood
U
of
H
in
M
with a neighborhood of the zero section in
N
, we may look at
\mathcal{R}
as at the vector in the tangent space of
U
. If we write it in Fermi coordinates, we get

\mathcal{R}=\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}Z_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial Z_{i}}.

(2.39)

By our choice of the trivialization,
\iota_{\mathcal{R}}\Gamma^{E}=0
.
Also,
\iota_{\mathcal{R}}(\pi_{0}^{*}R^{E}|_{H})=0
. Hence, by (
2.38
), we get

L_{\mathcal{R}}\Gamma^{E}=\Big[\iota_{\mathcal{R}},\pi_{0}^{*}(\nabla^{E}|_{H})\Big]\Gamma^{E}=\iota_{\mathcal{R}}R^{E}.

(2.40)

From (
2.39
), for
i=1,\ldots,m
, and
j=m+1,\ldots,n
, we also obtain the following identities

L_{\mathcal{R}}dZ_{i}=0,\qquad L_{\mathcal{R}}dZ_{j}=dZ_{j}.

(2.41)

Using (
2.41
) and expanding both sides of (
2.40
) in Taylor series at
Z_{N}=0
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}(|\alpha|+1)(\partial^{\alpha}\Gamma^{E})_{\psi(Z_{H},0)}(e_{N})\frac{Z_{N}^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}=\sum_{\alpha}(\partial^{\alpha}R^{E})_{\psi(Z_{H},0)}(\mathcal{R},e_{N})\frac{Z_{N}^{\alpha}}{\alpha!},\quad\text{ }

(2.42)

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}|\alpha|(\partial^{\alpha}\Gamma^{E})_{\psi(Z_{H},0)}(e_{H})\frac{Z_{N}^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}=\sum_{\alpha}(\partial^{\alpha}R^{E})_{\psi(Z_{H},0)}(\mathcal{R},e_{H})\frac{Z_{N}^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}.

From (
2.42
), we deduce the first two equations of Lemma
2.14
.
From the fact that
N
is orthogonal to
TH
, (
2.26
) and the fact that the restriction of
Q
to
H
respects the decomposition (
1.6
), we see that
R^{L}_{\psi(Z_{H},Z_{N})}(e_{N},e_{H})=O(|Z|)
.
The last two equations of Lemma
2.14
follow directly from Lemma
2.12
, (
2.26
) and (
2.42
), applied for
E\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=L
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.9
.

We denote by
\nabla
the standard covariant derivative in
E
, defined locally in the frame
\tilde{f}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}_{r}
.
We define the
1
-form
\Gamma^{E}_{0}
with values in
\text{End}{(E_{y_{0}})}
as follows

\Gamma^{E}_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\nabla^{E}-\nabla.

(2.43)

By (
2.36
), we have the following identity

\Gamma^{E}_{0,\psi(Z_{H},Z_{N})}=\pi_{0}^{*}(\Gamma^{E}_{0,\psi(Z_{H},0)})+\Gamma^{E}_{\psi(Z_{H},Z_{N})},

(2.44)

where we implicitly identified
\Gamma^{E}_{\psi(Z_{H},Z_{N})}
with a
1
-form with values in
\text{End}{(E_{y_{0}})}
using
\tilde{f}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}_{r}
.
By (
2.26
) and Lemma
2.14
, applied once for the second summand in the right-hand side of (
2.44
), and once for
M\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=H,H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=y_{0}
, to treat the first summand, we see that for
e\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial Z_{i}}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
,

\displaystyle\Gamma^{L}_{0,\psi(Z)}(e)=\frac{1}{2}\Big(R^{L}_{y_{0}}(Z_{H},e)+R^{L}_{\psi(Z_{H},0)}(Z_{N},e)\Big)+O(|Z|^{3}),

(2.45)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{E}_{0,\psi(Z)}(e)=O(|Z|).

Now, by (
2.30
) and (
2.45
), we conclude that

\Gamma^{L}_{0,\psi(Z)}(e)=\frac{1}{2}R^{L}_{y_{0}}(Z,e)+O(|Z|^{3}),

(2.46)

Now, we denote by
\gamma(t)
the geodesic
\phi(th(Z))
,
t\in[0,1]
. By the definition of
h
, we have
\gamma(1)=\psi(Z)
.
From Proposition
2.5
, we easily see

\psi^{-1}(\gamma(t))=h^{-1}(th(Z))=tZ+(t-t^{2})B(Z)+O(|Z|^{3}).

(2.47)

Directly from the definition of
\xi_{E}
, the fact that
\nabla^{E}_{\gamma^{\prime}(t)}\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{i}=0
, (
2.8
), and the usual law of derivation of the exponential, we have

\int_{0}^{1}\exp\big((1-s)\xi_{E}(\gamma(t))\big)\cdot\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\xi_{E}(\gamma(t))\Big)\cdot\exp\big((s-1)\xi_{E}(\gamma(t))\big)ds=\Gamma^{E}_{0,\gamma(t)}(\gamma^{\prime}(t)).

(2.48)

From (
2.48
) and the last equation from (
2.45
), we deduce (
2.25
).
The statement about the coefficients of the Taylor expansions readily follows from (
2.48
) and the respective statement from Proposition
2.9
.

Now, it is only left to establish (
2.27
).
Since now
E\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=L
is a line bundle, (
2.48
) simplifies to

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\xi_{L}(\gamma(t))=\Gamma^{L}_{0,\gamma(t)}(\gamma^{\prime}(t)).

(2.49)

From (
2.47
), we deduce the following asymptotics

\int_{0}^{1}R^{L}_{y_{0}}\Big(\psi^{-1}(\gamma(t)),\frac{\partial\psi^{-1}(\gamma(t))}{\partial t}\Big)dt=-\frac{1}{3}R^{L}_{y_{0}}\Big(Z,B(Z)\Big)+O(|Z|^{4}).

(2.50)

From (
2.46
), (
2.49
) and (
2.50
), we deduce the result. The statement about the form of the coefficients follows from the respective statements in Proposition
2.5
and Lemma
2.14
.
∎

2.4
Complex structure in Fermi coordinates and quasi-plurisubharmonicity

The main goal of this section is to give for Kähler manifolds of bounded geometry an approximate formula for the complex structure in Fermi coordinates.
As a consequence, we establish quasi-plurisubharmonicity of several functions, crucial for certain
L^{2}
-estimates.

More precisely, assume
(X,Y,g^{TX})
is a Kähler triple of bounded geometry.
We fix a point
y_{0}\in Y
and an orthonormal frame
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2m})
(resp.
(e_{2m+1},\ldots,e_{2n})
) in
(T_{y_{0}}Y,g_{y_{0}}^{TY})
(resp. in
(N_{y_{0}},g^{N}_{y_{0}})
), satisfying (
1.17
).
Recall that in (
1.18
) and (
2.2
), we defined two coordinate systems
\psi_{y_{0}}
,
\phi_{y_{0}}
in a neighborhood of
y_{0}
.
We denote by
J
the complex structure of
X
, and by
\textbf{J}=(\textbf{J}_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{2n}
its coordinates with respect to the basis
\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial Z_{i}}
,
i=1,\ldots,2n
.
It is a function, defined in
\mathbb{B}_{0}^{\real{}^{2n}}(R)
, where
R
is as in (
2.5
), with matrix values of size
2n\times 2n
.
We write
J
in a block form
\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\textbf{J}_{0}&\textbf{J}_{1}\\
\textbf{J}_{2}&\textbf{J}_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\big)
,
where
\textbf{J}_{0}
has size
2m\times 2m
.
The first result of this section goes as follows.

Lemma 2.16
.

The matrix
J
has the following Taylor expansion

\textbf{J}=J^{0}+\bigg(\begin{matrix}J^{1}&0\\
0&0\end{matrix}\bigg)+O\big(|Z|^{2}\big),

(2.51)

where
J^{0}
is the diagonal block matrix with blocks

\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)
,
and
J^{1}=(J^{1}_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{2m}
is given by

J^{1}_{ij}=-2g^{TX}_{y_{0}}(A(Je_{i})Z_{N},e_{j}),

(2.52)

for
A
defined in (
1.16
).
Moreover, the constant in the
O
-terms is uniform on
y_{0}\in Y
, and depends only on
C_{1}
from the bounds (
2.1
), (
2.3
) for
k=1
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially based on Lemma
2.11
and (
2.33
).
Recall that the vector fields
\tilde{t}^{\prime}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,2n
, were constructed in Lemma
2.11
by the parallel transport of
e_{i}
with respect to
\nabla^{TX}
along the curve
\phi(tZ)
,
t\in[0,1]
,
Z\in T_{y_{0}}X
.
As
(X,g^{TX})
is Kähler, by
[
30
, Theorem 1.2.8]
,
J
is parallel with respect to
\nabla^{TX}
.
This means that by (
1.17
), for
i=1,\ldots,n
, we have

J\tilde{t}^{\prime}_{2i-1}=\tilde{t}^{\prime}_{2i}.

(2.53)

From (
2.28
) and (
2.53
), we conclude that

J\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial Z_{2i-1}}=\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial Z_{2i}}+O(|Z|^{2}).

(2.54)

Remark that (
2.54
) already implies Lemma
2.16
for
Y\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x_{0}\}
.
Now, let us establish the general case.
By the fact that
A
takes values in skew-adjoint matrices and commutes with
J
, the
J
-invariance of
g^{TX}
and (
2.35
), we deduce that for
i=1,\ldots,2m
,
j=2m+1,\ldots,2n
,
l=1,\ldots,2n
, for
Z_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
Z_{N}\in\real^{2(n-m)}
, we have

\displaystyle g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(A(Z_{Y})e_{j},e_{l}\big)=g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(A(Z_{Y})Je_{j},Je_{l}\big),

(2.55)

\displaystyle g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(A(e_{i})Z_{Y},e_{l}\big)=g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(A(Je_{i})Z_{Y},Je_{l}\big),

\displaystyle g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(A(e_{i})Z_{N},e_{l}\big)=-g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(A(Je_{i})Z_{N},Je_{l}\big),

By (
2.33
), (
2.54
) and (
2.55
), we deduce (
2.51
).
∎

Now, recall that a function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to[-\infty,+\infty[
on a complex Hermitian manifold
(X,\omega)
is called quasi-plurisubharmonic if it is upper-semicontinuous, and there is a constant
C\in\real
, such that the following inequality holds in the distributional sense

\sqrt{-1}\partial\overline{\partial}f\geq-C\omega.

(2.56)

We denote by
{\rm{PSH}}(X,C\omega)
the set of quasi-plurisubharmonic functions
f
, verifying (
2.56
).

Recall that by standard properties of the plurisubharmonic functions, cf.
[
14
, Theorem I.5.6]
, for any convex function
\chi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\real\to\real
, verifying
0\leq\chi^{\prime}\leq 1
,
f\in{\rm{PSH}}(X,C\omega)
,
C\geq 0
, we have

\chi\circ f\in{\rm{PSH}}(X,C\omega)\quad\text{for any}\quad f\in{\rm{PSH}}(X,C\omega).

(2.57)

Later on, on several occasions, we will need the following lemma.

Lemma 2.17

(
[
25
, Proposition 4.2]
)
.

Suppose that
J
is an almost complex structure on an open subset
\Omega\subset\real^{2n}
.
Let
v
be a constant coefficient vector field on
\Omega
(i.e.
v=\sum v_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial Z_{i}}
where
v_{i}
are constants).
Then for any smooth function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Omega\to\real
, the following identity holds

\partial\overline{\partial}f\Big(v-\sqrt{-1}Jv,v+\sqrt{-1}Jv\Big)=(D^{2}f)(v,v)+(D^{2}f)(Jv,Jv)\\
+(Df)\Big(D_{Jv}J(v)-D_{v}J(Jv)\Big),

(2.58)

where
D^{2}f
is the standard double derivative of
f
, and
D_{u}J
is the derivative in the direction
u\in\real^{2n}
, of the almost complex structure, written in the standard coordinates of
2n
.

For any
x_{0}\in X
, we consider the function
\alpha_{x_{0}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{X})\to\real
, given by

\alpha_{x_{0}}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\rm{dist}}_{X}(x_{0},x)^{2}.

(2.59)

The proof of the following lemma is a direct consequence of Lemmas
2.16
and
2.17
.

Lemma 2.18
.

Assume that a Kähler manifold
(X,g^{TX})
is of bounded geometry.
Then there is
r_{0}>0
, such that for any
x_{0}\in X
,
\alpha_{x_{0}}\in{\rm{PSH}}(\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{0}),-\frac{1}{2}\omega)
.
Moreover,
r_{0}
depends only on
C_{1}
from the bound (
2.1
) and
r_{X}
.

Assume that a Kähler triple
(X,Y,g^{TX})
is of bounded geometry.
Let us consider
\delta_{Y}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\setminus Y\to\real
,
\alpha_{Y}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\real
, defined as

\displaystyle\delta_{Y}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\log\big({\rm{dist}}_{X}(x,Y)\big)\cdot\rho\Big(\frac{{\rm{dist}}_{X}(x,Y)}{r_{\perp}}\Big),

(2.60)

\displaystyle\alpha_{Y}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\rm{dist}}_{X}(x,Y)^{2}\cdot\rho\Big(\frac{{\rm{dist}}_{X}(x,Y)}{r_{\perp}}\Big)+\Big(1-\rho\Big(\frac{{\rm{dist}}_{X}(x,Y)}{r_{\perp}}\Big)\Big),

where
\rho
was defined in (
1.10
).
Remark that since
{\rm{dist}}_{X}(x,Y)^{2}
is smooth over
\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(r_{\perp})
, both functions
\delta_{Y}
and
\alpha_{Y}
are smooth.

Lemma 2.19
.

There is
C>0
, such that
\delta_{Y}\in{\rm{PSH}}(X,C\omega)
,
\alpha_{Y}\in{\rm{PSH}}(X,C\omega)
,
-\alpha_{Y}\in{\rm{PSH}}(X,C\omega)
.
Moreover,
C
depends only on
C_{1}
from the bounds (
2.1
), (
2.3
) and
r_{\perp}
.

Proof.

The proofs for
\delta_{Y}
,
\alpha_{Y}
,
-\alpha_{Y}
are similar, so we concentrate on
\delta_{Y}
, which is slightly more difficult than the other two.
From Lemma
2.16
and bounded geometry condition, we see that
\sqrt{-1}\partial\overline{\partial}\delta_{Y}
is uniformly bounded away from in
\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})
.
So it is enough to study this quantity only on
\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})
.
We fix a point
y_{0}\in Y
, consider Fermi coordinates
\psi_{y_{0}}
, and introduce the vector field

v=\sum_{i=1}^{2m}a_{i}\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial Z_{i}}+\sum_{j=2m+1}^{2n}b_{j}\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial Z_{j}},

(2.61)

where
a_{i},b_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
,
i=1,\ldots,2m
,
j=2m+1,\ldots,2n
, are certain constants,
\sum|a_{i}|^{2}+\sum|b_{j}|^{2}=1
.
It is enough to verify that there is a constant
C>0
, as described in the statement of the proposition we’re proving, such that for any choice of
a_{i}
,
b_{j}
above, over
\{\psi_{y_{0}}(Z_{N})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}|Z_{N}|<\frac{r_{\perp}}{4}\}
, we have

\partial\overline{\partial}\delta_{Y}\Big(v-\sqrt{-1}Jv,v+\sqrt{-1}Jv\Big)\geq-C.

(2.62)

Let us now calculate each term on the right-hand side of (
2.58
) up to negligible terms.
First of all, remark that in Fermi coordinates, over
\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})
,
\delta_{Y}
has particularly simple form

\delta_{Y}(Z)=\log|Z_{N}|=\frac{1}{2}\log\Big(\sum_{j=2m+1}^{2n}|Z_{j}|^{2}\Big).

(2.63)

Hence, by (
2.61
), we deduce

(D^{2}\delta_{Y})(v,v)=\frac{1}{|Z_{N}|^{4}}\Big(\Big(\sum_{j=2m+1}^{2n}|b_{j}|^{2}\Big)|Z_{N}|^{2}-2\Big(\sum_{j=2m+1}^{2n}b_{j}Z_{j}\Big)^{2}\Big).

(2.64)

A similar calculation, using Lemma
2.16
, gives that over
\{\psi_{y_{0}}(Z_{N})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}|Z_{N}|<\frac{r_{\perp}}{4}\}
, we have

(D^{2}\delta_{Y})(Jv,Jv)=\\
\frac{1}{|Z_{N}|^{4}}\Big(\Big(\sum_{j=2m+1}^{2n}|b_{j}|^{2}\Big)|Z_{N}|^{2}-2\Big(\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\big(b_{2j-1}Z_{2j}-b_{2j}Z_{2j-1}\big)\Big)^{2}\Big)+O(1),

(2.65)

where the
O
-term can be bounded uniformly in terms of
C_{1}
from the bounds (
2.1
), (
2.3
) and
r_{\perp}
.
From Lemma
2.16
, we see that the last two terms in (
2.58
) are negligible.
From this (
2.58
), (
2.64
) and (
2.65
), we see that to deduce (
2.62
), it is now only left to prove the bound

\Big(\sum_{j=2m+1}^{2n}|b_{j}|^{2}\Big)|Z_{N}|^{2}\geq\Big(\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}\big(b_{2j-1}Z_{2j}-b_{2j}Z_{2j-1}\big)\Big)^{2}+\Big(\sum_{j=2m+1}^{2n}b_{j}Z_{j}\Big)^{2}.

(2.66)

But (
2.66
) follows easily from the fact that the vectors
(Z_{2m+2},-Z_{2m+1},\ldots,Z_{2n},-Z_{2n-1})
and
(Z_{2m+1},\ldots,Z_{2n})
are orthogonal.
∎

2.5
Existence of uniform Stein atlases and related extension theorems

The main goal of this section is to introduce and study manifolds and submanifolds with uniform Stein atlases.
In particular, we prove that any Kähler manifold of bounded geometry admits a uniform Stein atlas and we make a relation between the existence of a uniform Stein atlas and the existence of holomoprhic coordinates, defined on sufficiently big geodesic balls.

Before this, recall that by Cauchy formula, the
L^{2}
-bound on holomoprhic functions on the complex plane implies
\mathscr{C}^{k}
-bounds for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
As the following lemma shows, an analogous statement holds for general Kähler manifolds of bounded geometry.

Lemma 2.20
.

Assume that a Kähler manifold
(X,g^{TX})
and a Hermitian vector bundle
(E,h^{E})
over it have bounded geometry.
Then for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
r_{1}>r_{0}>0
,
r_{1}<\frac{r_{X}}{2}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
x_{0}\in X
, for any
f\in H^{0}_{(2)}(\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{1}),E)
, we have

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{0}))}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{1}))}.

(2.67)

Moreover,
C
depends only on
r_{0},r_{1}
and
C_{k+n+4}
from (
2.1
) and (
2.7
).

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of Sobolev embedding and elliptic estimates, cf.
[
30
, (A.1.15), (A.1.17)]
.
∎

Recall that a complex manifold is said to be
Stein
if it is holomorphically convex, its global holomorphic functions separate points and give local coordinates at every point, cf.
[
14
, Definition 1.6.16]
.
One can see, cf.
[
14
, Theorem 1.6.18]
, that any Stein manifold is strongly pseudoconvex, meaning that it carries a smooth strictly plurisubharmonic exhaustion function.
The famous result of Grauert from
[
21
, Theorem 2]
, states that the converse for relatively compact subdomains of complex manifolds holds as well.
In this section, we will use this perspective through strictly plurisubharmonic exhaustions on Stein manifolds.

Definition 2.21
.

We say that a Hermitian manifold
(X,g^{TX})
admits a uniform Stein atlas if there are
r_{1}>r_{0}>0
,
c,C>0
, such that for any
x_{0}\in X
, there is a Stein neighborhood
\Omega
of
x_{0}
, such that
\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{0})\subset\Omega\subset\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{1})
, and a smooth strictly plurisubharmonic exhaustion function
\delta
on
\Omega
, verifying the following bounds

\displaystyle\delta>0\quad\text{ over }\,\Omega,\qquad\delta<C\quad\text{ over }\,\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{0}),

(2.68)

\displaystyle\sqrt{-1}\partial\overline{\partial}\delta>c\omega,

where
\omega
is the Hermitian form associated to
g^{TX}
.

Proposition 2.22
.

Any Kähler manifold of bounded geometry
(X,g^{TX})
admits uniform Stein atlas.
Moreover,
r_{1}>r_{0}>0
,
c,C>0
from Definition
2.21
depend only on
C_{1}
from the bound (
2.1
) and
r_{X}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\alpha_{x_{0}}
was defined in (
2.59
).
By Lemma
2.18
, there is a uniform constant
r_{1}>0
, which depends only on
C_{1}
from the bound (
2.1
) and
r_{X}
, such that
\sqrt{-1}\partial\overline{\partial}\alpha_{x_{0}}>\frac{1}{2}\omega
over
\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{1})
.
We now take
r_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{r_{1}}{2}
, and let
\delta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{1}{r_{1}^{2}-\alpha_{x_{0}}}
.
By the above and (
2.57
), we deduce that
\delta
is strictly plurisubharmonic.
Since it is clearly exhaustive, we deduce that
\Omega\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{1})
is strongly pseudoconvex.
By (
2.57
), we conclude that the requirements of Definition
2.21
are satisfied for the above choice and
c\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{1}{2r_{1}^{2}}
,
C\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{2}{r_{1}^{2}}
.
∎

Proposition 2.23
.

For any Kähler manifold of bounded geometry
(X,g^{TX})
, there are
r_{c},C>0
, such that for any
x_{0}\in X
, there are holomorphic coordinates
\chi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{c})\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
, verifying
h_{i}(x_{0})=0
,
|h_{i}|<C
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, and such that
dh_{i}(x_{0})
form an orthonormal frame of
(T^{(1,0)*}X,g^{T^{*}X})
.
Moreover,
r_{c},C>0
, depend only on
C_{n+7}
from the bound (
2.1
) and
r_{X}
.

Proof.

The main idea of the proof is to first construct smooth functions
h^{\prime}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, as in Proposition
2.23
and then by using Hörmander’s
L^{2}
-estimates to perturb
h^{\prime}_{i}
to holomorphic coordinates
h_{i}
.

Let
r_{0},r_{1}>0
,
\Omega
,
\delta
be as in Definition
2.21
.
First, we define
h^{\prime}_{i}
in geodesic coordinates
\phi_{x_{0}}
by

h^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=z_{i}\rho\Big(\frac{|Z|}{r_{0}}\Big),

(2.69)

where
\rho
is a bump function as in (
1.10
).
By bounded geometry condition, there is
C_{1}>0
, which depends only on
r_{0}
and
C_{0}
from (
2.1
), such that

\int_{\Omega}|h^{\prime}_{i}|^{2}dv_{g^{TX}}\leq C_{1}.

(2.70)

Directly from Lemma
2.16
, applied for
Y\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{x_{0}\}
, we see that there is
C_{2}>0
, which depends only on
r_{0}
and
C_{1}
from (
2.1
), such that over
\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{0})
, we have

|\overline{\partial}h^{\prime}_{i}|\leq C_{2}|Z|^{2}.

(2.71)

From this, the fact that
{\rm{supp}}\,\overline{\partial}h^{\prime}_{i}\subset\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{0})
and the first estimate from (
2.68
), we conclude that for any
c>0
, there is a uniform constant
C_{3}
, which depends only on
r_{0}
,
C
from the first line in (
2.68
), and
C_{1}
from (
2.1
), such that for
\delta_{\{x_{0}\}}
, from Lemma
2.19
, we have

\int_{\Omega}|\overline{\partial}h^{\prime}_{i}|^{2}\exp(-c\delta)\cdot\exp(-(2n+1)\delta_{\{x_{0}\}})dv_{g^{TX}}\leq C_{3}.

(2.72)

Now, as
(X,g^{TX})
is of bounded geometry, by Lemma
2.19
, there is
c>0
depending only on
C_{1}
from (
2.1
) and
c
from the second line of (
2.68
), such that for
E\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=K_{X}^{-1}
,
h^{E}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(h^{K_{X}})^{-1}\cdot\exp(-c\delta)\cdot\exp(-(2n+1)\delta_{\{x_{0}\}})
, the curvature
R^{E}
of
(E,h^{E})
over
\Omega
satisfies
\sqrt{-1}R^{E}\geq\omega\otimes{\rm{Id}}_{E}
,
where the positivity here is in the sense of Nakano, cf.
[
14
, Definition VII.6.3]
.
By (
2.72
) and the result
[
12
, Théorème 0.2]
, which states that
\Omega
admits a complete Kähler metric, we may apply
[
12
, Théorème 5.1]
for
X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Omega
,
g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\overline{\partial}h^{\prime}_{i}
and
(E,h^{E})
as above to deduce that there are sections
f_{i}\in L^{2}(\Omega,K_{\Omega}\otimes E)
, such that we have
\overline{\partial}f_{i}=g
, and

\int_{\Omega}|f_{i}|^{2}\exp(-c\delta)\cdot\exp(-(2n+1)\delta_{\{x_{0}\}})dv_{g^{TX}}\\
\leq\int_{\Omega}|\overline{\partial}h^{\prime}_{i}|^{2}\exp(-c\delta)\cdot\exp(-(2n+1)\delta_{\{x_{0}\}})dv_{g^{TX}}.

(2.73)

We now put
h_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=h^{\prime}_{i}-f_{i}
.
We trivially have
\overline{\partial}h_{i}=0
.
Also, by (
2.68
), (
2.70
), (
2.72
) and (
2.73
), we obtain

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{0})}|h_{i}|^{2}dv_{g^{TX}}\leq C_{4},

(2.74)

where
C_{4}
depends only on
r_{0}
,
C
from the first line in (
2.68
), and
C_{1}
from (
2.1
).
By the standard regularity result,
[
12
, Lemme 6.9]
,
h_{i}
is smooth and holomorphic on
\Omega
, and the equation
\overline{\partial}h_{i}=0
holds in the usual sense.
From this, (
2.72
), (
2.73
) and the non-integrability of
|Z|\exp(-(2n+1)\delta_{\{x_{0}\}})
and
\exp(-(2n+1)\delta_{\{x_{0}\}})
near
x_{0}
, we deduce that
f_{i}(x_{0})=0
and
df_{i}(x_{0})=0
.
Hence
h_{i}(x_{0})=h^{\prime}_{i}(x_{0})
and
dh_{i}(x_{0})=dh^{\prime}_{i}(x_{0})
.
Thus,
\partial h_{i}(x_{0})
form an orthonormal basis of
(T^{(1,0)*}X,g^{T^{*}X})
.

From Lemma
2.20
and (
2.74
), we conclude that there is a constant
C_{5}>0
, which depends only on
r_{0},r_{1}
and
C_{n+7}
from (
2.1
), such that

\|h_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{3}(\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{0}))}\leq C_{5}
.
We conclude that there is
r_{c}>0
, which depends only on
C_{n+7}
from the bound (
2.1
) and
r_{X}
, such that
(h_{1},\ldots,h_{n})
form holomorphic coordinates over
\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{c})
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
(E,h^{E})
be a Hermitian vector bundle of bounded geometry over a Kähler manifold of bounded geometry
(X,g^{TX})
.
Then there are
r_{0},C>0
, such that for any
x_{0}\in X
, there is a local holomorphic frame
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})\in H^{0}(\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{0}),E)
of
E
, such that

\big\|f_{i}\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{X}(r_{0}))}\leq C,

(2.75)
