and
(f_{1}(x_{0}),\ldots,f_{r}(x_{0}))
is an orthonormal frame of
(E_{x_{0}},h^{E}_{x_{0}})
.
Moreover,
C
depends only on
r_{0}
,
c,C
from (
2.68
) and
C_{n+7}
from (
2.1
) and (
2.7
).

Proof.

The proof can be deduced from Hörmander’s
L^{2}
-estimates and Lemmas
2.14
,
2.16
,
2.19
in exactly the same way as in the proof of Proposition
2.23
.
∎

Theorem 2.25
.

Assume that the Kähler triple
(X,Y,g^{TX})
is of bounded geometry. Then there are
r_{c},C>0
, such that for any
y_{0}\in Y
, there are holomorphic coordinates
\chi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(r_{c})\to\mathbb{C}
,
h_{i}(x_{0})=0
,
|h_{i}|<C
,
i=1,\ldots,n
,
h_{m+1}|_{Y},\ldots,h_{n}|_{Y}=0
, and such that
\partial h_{i}(x_{0})
form an orthonormal frame of
(T^{(1,0)*}X,g^{T^{*}X})
.
Moreover,
r_{c},C
depend only on
r_{X}
and
C_{n+7}
from (
2.1
) and (
2.3
).

Remark 2.26
.

We see that there is
r_{c}^{1}>0
, which depends only on
C_{n+7}
from the bounds (
2.1
), (
2.3
),
r_{Y}
,
r_{X}
,
r_{\perp}
, such that
\mathbb{B}_{0}^{\mathbb{C}^{n}}(r_{c}^{1})\subset\operatorname{Im}\chi
.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to the proof of Proposition
2.23
. The only difference is that instead of the weight
(2n+1)\delta_{\{x_{0}\}}
in (
2.72
), one has to consider
(2m+1)\delta_{\{x_{0}\}}+2(n-m)\delta_{Y}
.
∎

3
Operator algebras on manifolds of bounded geometry

In this section, we prove that the set of operators on manifolds of bounded geometry, admitting certain bounds on the Schwartz kernels, forms an algebra under the composition.

This section is organized as follows.
In Section
3.1
, we show that the set of operators with exponential decay of the Schwartz kernel forms an algebra on manifolds of bounded geometry.
Then in Section
3.2
, we give explicit formulas for the Schwartz kernels of the orthogonal Bergman projector and the extension operator on the pair
(\mathbb{C}^{n},\mathbb{C}^{m})
.
We also state the composition rules for operators with related kernels.
Finally, in Section
3.3
, we prove that the set of operators, whose Schwartz kernel admits Taylor-type expansion, forms an algebra under the composition.

3.1
Algebra of operators with exponential decay of the Schwartz kernel

In this section, we show that the set of operators with exponential decay essentially forms an algebra on manifolds of bounded geometry.

Let
(M,g^{TM})
be a complete manifold with a positive bound on the injectivity radius,
r_{M}>0
. We note
n\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\dim M
.
We fix an arbitrary sequence of Hermitian vector bundles
(E_{p},\nabla^{E_{p}},h^{E_{p}})
,
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, on
M
, endowed with a fixed connection.
We fix
s>0
and assume that the Ricci curvature
{\rm{Ric}}_{g^{TM}}
of
(M,g^{TM})
satisfies the bound

{\rm{Ric}}_{g^{TM}}\geq-(n-1)s.

(3.1)

Clearly, (
3.1
) is satisfied for some
s>0
once
(M,g^{TM})
is of bounded geometry.

Let us fix a volume form
dv_{M}
on
M
, such that the condition, analogous to (
1.4
), is satisfied with respect to the metric
g^{TM}
.
Let us fix
q\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and a sequence of operators
A_{p}^{1},\ldots,A_{p}^{q}
,
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, acting on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M,E_{p})
by the convolutions with smooth kernels
A_{p}^{1}(x_{1},x_{2}),\ldots,A_{p}^{q}(x_{1},x_{2})\in E_{p,x_{1}}\otimes E_{p,x_{2}}^{*}
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
, with respect to the volume form
dv_{M}
.
We assume that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there are
c,C_{h}>0
,
h=1,\ldots,q
, such that for any
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
, we have

\big|A_{p}^{h}(x_{1},x_{2})\big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(M\times M)}\leq C_{h}\cdot p^{\frac{n+k}{2}}\cdot\exp\big(-c\sqrt{p}\cdot{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})\big),

(3.2)

and there is
h
among
1,\ldots,q
, such that even stronger bound holds

\big|A_{p}^{h}(x_{1},x_{2})\big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(M\times M)}\\
\leq C_{h}\cdot p^{\frac{n+k}{2}}\cdot\exp\Big(-c\sqrt{p}\cdot\big({\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})+{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},W)+{\rm{dist}}(x_{2},W)\big)\Big),

(3.3)

where
W
is a closed subset in
M
, and the
\mathscr{C}^{k}
-norm is taken in the sense of Theorem
1.5
.

Lemma 3.1
.

There is
C_{0}>0
, which depends only on
s,r_{M},n
and
c
from (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), such that for any
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
\sqrt{p}>4(n-1)\frac{\sqrt{s}}{c}
, the following operator is well-defined

D_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=A_{p}^{1}\circ\cdots\circ A_{p}^{q},

(3.4)

and for
C\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\prod_{h=1}^{q}C_{h}
, the following bound holds

\big|D_{p}(x_{1},x_{2})\big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(X\times X)}\\
\leq C_{0}^{q}Cp^{\frac{n+k}{2}}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{c}{8}\sqrt{p}\cdot\big({\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})+{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},W)+{\rm{dist}}(x_{2},W)\big)\Big).

(3.5)

In the proof of Lemma
3.1
and elsewhere, the following proposition plays a crucial role.

Proposition 3.2
.

There is a constant
C^{\prime}>0
, which depends only on
n
,
s
,
r_{M}
, such that for any
x_{0}\in M
,
l>2(n-1)\sqrt{s}
, the following bound holds

\int_{M}\exp\big(-l{\rm{dist}}(x_{0},x)\big)dv_{g^{TM}}(x)<\frac{C^{\prime}}{l^{n}}.

(3.6)

Proof.

The main idea of our proof is to use Bishop-Gromov inequality to bound the volumes of balls and spheres in our manifold.
We denote by
v(n,s,r)
the volume of a ball of radius
r
in the space form of constant curvature
-s
,
s>0
, cf.
[
36
, p. 69]
.
From
[
36
, pp. 69, 269]
, we see that

v(n,s,r)={\rm{Vol}}(S^{n-1})\cdot\int_{0}^{r}\Big(\frac{\sinh(\sqrt{s}r)}{\sqrt{s}}\Big)^{n-1}dr,

(3.7)

where
{\rm{Vol}}(S^{n-1})
is the volume of the unit sphere
S^{n-1}
, endowed with the standard Riemannian metric.
From (
3.7
), we see that for any
s>0
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
r>0
, we have

v(n,s,r)\leq C\exp\big((n-1)\sqrt{s}r\big).

(3.8)

Bishop-Gromov inequality
[
36
, Lemma 9.36]
states that the following function is non-increasing

\real\ni r\mapsto\frac{{\rm{Vol}}(\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{M}(r))}{v(n,s,r)}.

(3.9)

Moreover, the limit of (
3.9
), as
r\to 0
, is equal to
1
.
In particular, for any
r\geq 0
, we have

{\rm{Vol}}(\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{M}(r))\leq v(n,s,r).

(3.10)

We now decompose the integral in (
3.6
) into two parts: over
\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{M}(r_{M})
and over its complement,
V
.
For the second part, we have the following bound

\int_{V}\exp\big(-l{\rm{dist}}(x_{0},x)\big)dv_{g^{TM}}(x)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\exp(-ilr_{M}){\rm{Vol}}\Big(\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{M}((i+1)r_{M})\setminus\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{M}(ir_{M})\Big).

(3.11)

However, from (
3.8
) and (
3.10
), we see that

{\rm{Vol}}\Big(\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{M}((i+1)r_{M})\setminus\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{M}(ir_{M})\Big)\leq C\exp\big((i+1)(n-1)\sqrt{s}r_{M}\big).

(3.12)

By combining (
3.11
) and (
3.12
), for
l>2(n-1)\sqrt{s}
, we easily get the bound

\int_{V}\exp\big(-l{\rm{dist}}(x_{0},x)\big)dv_{g^{TM}}(x)\leq C\exp\Big(-\frac{lr_{M}}{2}\Big).

(3.13)

It is now only left to bound the integral over
\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{M}(r_{M})
.
The coarea formula gives us for
r<r_{M}
the following identity

{\rm{Vol}}(\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{M}(r))=\int_{0}^{r}{\rm{Vol}}(S_{x_{0}}^{M}(r^{\prime}))dr^{\prime},

(3.14)

where
S_{x_{0}}^{M}(r)
is the volume of the
r
-sphere around
{x_{0}}
.
By differentiating the function (
3.9
), and using (
3.14
), we see that (
3.9
) is equivalent to the following bound

\frac{{\rm{Vol}}(S_{x_{0}}^{M}(r))}{{\rm{Vol}}(\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{M}(r))}\leq\frac{t(n,s,r)}{v(n,s,r)},

(3.15)

where
t(n,s,r)=\frac{d}{dr}v(n,s,r)
.
From (
3.7
), (
3.10
) and (
3.15
), we deduce

{\rm{Vol}}(S_{x_{0}}^{M}(r))\leq{\rm{Vol}}(S^{n-1})\cdot\Big(\frac{\sinh(\sqrt{s}r)}{\sqrt{s}}\Big)^{n-1}.

(3.16)

From (
3.16
), we see that there is
C_{0}>0
, which depends only on
s
and
r_{M}
, such that

\int_{0}^{r_{M}}\exp(-lr){\rm{Vol}}(S_{x_{0}}^{M}(r))dr\leq\frac{C_{0}}{l^{n}}.

(3.17)

Now, from the coarea formula as in (
3.14
), we have

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{x_{0}}^{M}(r_{M})}\exp\big(-l{\rm{dist}}(x_{0},x)\big)dv_{g^{TM}}(x)=\int_{0}^{r_{M}}\exp(-lr){\rm{Vol}}(S_{x_{0}}^{M}(r))dr.

(3.18)

From (
3.13
), (
3.17
) and (
3.18
), we conclude.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

For simplicity of the presentation, we only present the proof for
k=0
, as the general case is treated in an analogous way.
We trivially have the following identity

D_{p}(x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{M^{\times(q-1)}}A_{p}^{1}(x_{1},z_{1})\cdot A_{p}^{2}(z_{1},z_{2})\cdot\ldots\cdot A_{p}^{q}(z_{q-1},x_{2})dv_{M}(z_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot dv_{M}(z_{q-1}).

(3.19)

Now, the triangle inequality readily implies

\displaystyle{\rm{\displaystyle dist}}(x_{1},z_{1})+{\rm{dist}}(z_{1},z_{2})+\cdots+{\rm{dist}}(z_{q-1},x_{2})\geq{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2}),

(3.20)

\displaystyle{\rm{\displaystyle dist}}(x_{1},z_{1})+\cdots+{\rm{dist}}(z_{q-1},x_{2})+{\rm{dist}}(z_{i},W)\geq{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},W).

Then for
C
as in (
3.5
), by (
3.2
), (
3.3
), (
3.19
) and (
3.20
), the following bound holds

\big|D_{p}(x_{1},x_{2})\big|\leq Cp^{\frac{n}{2}}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{c}{4}\sqrt{p}\cdot\big({\rm{dist}}(x_{1},W)+{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})\big)\Big)\cdot\\
\cdot\int_{M}p^{\frac{n}{2}}\exp\Big(-\frac{c}{2}\sqrt{p}\cdot{\rm{dist}}(z_{q-1},x_{2})\Big)\cdot\int_{M}p^{\frac{n}{2}}\exp\Big(-\frac{c}{2}\sqrt{p}\cdot{\rm{dist}}(z_{q-2},z_{q-1})\Big)\cdot\ldots\\
\cdot\int_{M}p^{\frac{n}{2}}\exp\Big(-\frac{c}{2}\sqrt{p}\cdot{\rm{dist}}(z_{1},z_{2})\Big)dv_{M}(z_{1})\cdots dv_{M}(z_{q-1}).

(3.21)

By triangle inequality, we also have

{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},W)+{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})\geq{\rm{dist}}(x_{2},W).

(3.22)

By applying Proposition
3.2
and (
1.4
), for the integrals over
z_{1},\ldots,z_{q-1}
from (
3.21
), and (
3.22
), we get (
3.5
) for
k=0
from (
3.21
) for
C_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C^{\prime}
from Proposition
3.2
.
∎

We fix a smooth submanifold
H\subset M
such that
(M,H,g^{TM})
is of bounded geometry.
We will use the following consequence of Proposition
3.2
in what follows.

Corollary 3.3
.

There are
c,C^{\prime}>0
, which depend only on
n
,
m
,
r_{M}
,
r_{N}
,
r_{\perp}

s
,
r_{M}
, and
C_{0}
from (
2.1
) and (
2.3
), such that for any
y_{0}\in H
,
l>c
, the following bound holds

\int_{H}\exp\big(-l{\rm{dist}}_{M}(y_{0},y)\big)dv_{g^{TH}}(y)<\frac{C^{\prime}}{l^{m}}.

(3.23)

Remark 3.4
.

One can easily construct an example of a triple
(M,H,g^{TM})
of bounded geometry, for which the immersion
\iota\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H\to M
is not a quasi-isometry. Hence Corollary
3.3
doesn’t follow directly from Proposition
3.2
.

Proof.

Directly from the coordinate-wise description of bounded geometry condition, cf. Section
2.1
, we see that there are
\epsilon_{0},c>0
, depending only on
R
from (
2.5
) and
C_{0}
, for which (
2.1
), (
2.3
) hold, such that for any
y_{0},y_{1}\in H
, verifying
{\rm{dist}}_{M}(y_{0},y_{1})<\epsilon_{0}
, we have

{\rm{dist}}_{M}(y_{0},y_{1})>c{\rm{dist}}_{H}(y_{0},y_{1}).

(3.24)

We let
\epsilon\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{1}{2}\min\{\epsilon_{0},R\}
.
Similarly to the proof of Proposition
3.2
, we decompose the integration into two parts: over
\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{H}(\epsilon)
and over its complement,
V
.
From (
3.24
), we then conclude that there is a constant
C^{\prime\prime}
, depending only on
R
and
C_{0}
, for which (
2.1
), (
2.3
) hold, such that

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{H}(\epsilon)}\exp\big(-l{\rm{dist}}_{M}(y_{0},y)\big)dv_{g^{TH}}(y)<\frac{C^{\prime\prime}}{l^{m}}.

(3.25)

Now, let us estimate the integral over
V
.
For this, remark that for
x\in\mathbb{B}_{H}^{M}(\frac{\epsilon}{2\max(1,c)})
and
y_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\pi_{0}(x)
, where
\pi_{0}
is as introduced before (
1.7
), we have

{\rm{dist}}_{M}(y_{0},y_{1})\geq{\rm{dist}}_{M}(y_{0},x)-\frac{\epsilon}{2\max(1,c)}.

(3.26)

But since for any
x\in\mathbb{B}_{H}^{M}(\frac{\epsilon}{2\max(1,c)})
, such that
y_{1}\in V
, we have
{\rm{dist}}_{M}(y_{0},y_{1})\geq\frac{\epsilon}{\max(1,c)}
by (
3.24
), we conclude by (
3.26
) that we have

{\rm{dist}}_{M}(y_{0},y_{1})\geq\frac{1}{2}{\rm{dist}}_{M}(y_{0},x).

(3.27)

From this, we deduce that there is a constant
C^{\prime\prime\prime}
, depending only on
R
and
C_{0}
, for which (
2.1
), (
2.3
) hold, such that

\int_{V}\exp\big(-l{\rm{dist}}_{M}(y_{0},y)\big)dv_{g^{TH}}(y)<C^{\prime\prime\prime}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{B}_{V}^{M}(\frac{\epsilon}{2\max(1,c)})}\exp\big(-\frac{l}{2}{\rm{dist}}_{M}(y_{0},x)\big)dv_{g^{TM}}(x).

(3.28)

We conclude by (
3.13
), (
3.25
) and (
3.28
).
∎

3.2
Model operators; Fock-Bargmann space and kernel calculus

In this section, we consider the model situation of the complex vector space, for which an explicit formula for the Schwartz kernels of Bergman projectors and the extension operator can be given.
We then use those explicit formulas to give a description for the compositions of the operators, the Schwartz kernels of which can be expressed using the above kernels.
This section (as well as the next one) is motivated in many ways by the works of Ma-Marinescu
[
31
]
,
[
30
]
, and it motivates the formulation of Theorems
1.6
.

Endow
X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{C}^{n}
with the standard metric and consider a trivialized complex line bundle
L_{0}
on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
We endow
L_{0}
with the Hermitian metric
h^{L_{0}}
, given by

\|1\|_{h^{L_{0}}}(Z)=\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi}{2}|Z|^{2}\Big),

(3.29)

where
Z
is the natural real coordinate on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and
1
is the trivializing section of
L_{0}
.
An easy verification shows that (
3.29
) implies that (
1.2
) holds in our setting.
Recall that
[
30
, §4.1.6]
shows that the Kodaira Laplacian
\mathscr{L}
on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X,L_{0})
, multiplied by
2
, and viewed as an operator on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
using the orthonormal trivialization, given by
1\cdot\exp(\frac{\pi}{2}|Z|^{2})
, is given by

\mathscr{L}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}b_{i}^{+},

(3.30)

where
b_{i}
,
b_{i}^{+}
are
creation
and
annihilation
operators, defined as

b_{i}=-2\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}+\pi\overline{z}_{i},\qquad b_{i}^{+}=2\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}+\pi z_{i}.

(3.31)

A classical calculation, cf.
[
30
, Theorem 4.1.20]
, shows that the orthonormal basis with respect to the induced
L^{2}
-norm of
\ker\mathscr{L}
is given in the orthonormal trivialization above by

\Big(\frac{\pi^{|\beta|}}{\beta!}\Big)^{1/2}z^{\beta}\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi}{2}|Z|^{2}\Big),\qquad\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n}.

(3.32)

In particular,
[
30
, (4.1.84)]
, the Bergman kernel
\mathscr{P}_{n}
of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is given by

\mathscr{P}_{n}(Z,Z^{\prime})=\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(|z_{i}|^{2}+|z^{\prime}_{i}|^{2}-2z_{i}\overline{z}^{\prime}_{i}\big)\Big),\quad\text{for }Z,Z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}.

(3.33)

Also, we see that the Schwartz kernel of the orthogonal Bergman kernel, corresponding to the projection onto holomoprhic sections orthogonal to those which vanish along
\mathbb{C}^{m}
, is given by

\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})=\sum_{\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{m}}\Big(\frac{\pi^{|\beta|}}{\beta!}\Big)z^{\beta}\overline{z}^{\prime\beta}\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi}{2}(|Z|^{2}+|Z^{\prime}|^{2})\Big).

(3.34)

By simplifying the above expression using (
3.33
), we see that
\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})
corresponds precisely to the quantity, defined in (
1.26
).

Let us calculate the
L^{2}
-extension operator
\mathscr{E}_{n,m}
, extending each element from
(\ker\mathscr{L})|_{Y}
to an element from
\ker\mathscr{L}
with the minimal
L^{2}
-norm.
From (
3.32
), we easily see that for
Z_{Y}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
,
Z_{N}\in\mathbb{C}^{n-m}
and
g\in(\ker\mathscr{L})|_{Y}
, we have

(\mathscr{E}_{n,m}g)(Z_{Y},Z_{N})=g(Z_{Y})\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi}{2}|Z_{N}|^{2}\Big).

(3.35)

We extend
\mathscr{E}_{n,m}
to the whole
L^{2}
-space by
g\mapsto(\mathscr{E}_{n,m}\circ\mathscr{P}_{m})g
. From (
3.35
), we see that the kernel of
\mathscr{E}_{n,m}
corresponds precisely to the quantity, defined in (
1.20
).

Now, a lot of calculations in this article will have something to do with compositions of operators having Schwartz kernels, given by the product of polynomials with the above kernels.
For that reason, the following lemma will be of utmost importance in what follows.

Lemma 3.5
.

For any polynomials
A_{1}(Z,Z^{\prime}),A_{2}(Z,Z^{\prime})
,
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
, there is a polynomial
A_{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[A_{1},A_{2}]
, the coefficients of which are polynomials of the coefficients of
A_{1},A_{2}
, such that

(A_{1}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp})\circ(A_{2}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp})=A_{3}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}.

(3.36)

Moreover,
\deg A_{3}\leq\deg A_{1}+\deg A_{2}
. Also, if both polynomials
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
are even or odd (resp. one is even, another is odd), then the polynomial
A_{3}
is even (resp. odd).

Similarly, there is a polynomial
A^{\prime}_{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{K^{\prime}}_{n,m}[A_{1},A_{2}]
with the same properties as
A_{3}
, such that

(A_{1}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n})\circ(A_{2}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp})=A^{\prime}_{3}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}.

(3.37)

Also, for any polynomials
A_{1}(Z,Z^{\prime}),A_{2}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y})
, where
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
,
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
, there is a polynomial
A^{\prime\prime}_{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{K^{\prime\prime}}_{n,m}[A_{1},A_{2}]
in
(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})
, with the same properties as
A_{3}
, such that

(A_{1}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp})\circ\mathscr{E}_{n,m}\circ(A_{2}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{m})=A^{\prime\prime}_{3}\cdot\mathscr{E}_{n,m}.

(3.38)

Finally, for any polynomials
A_{1}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y}),A_{2}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y})
, where
Z\in\real^{2n}
,
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
, there is a polynomial
A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{K^{\prime\prime\prime}}_{n,m}[A_{1},A_{2}]
in
(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})
, with the same properties as
A_{3}
, such that

(A_{1}\cdot\mathscr{E}_{n,m}^{\perp})\circ(A_{2}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{m})=A^{\prime\prime\prime}_{3}\cdot\mathscr{E}_{n,m}.

(3.39)

Proof.

First of all, since
\mathscr{P}_{n,n}^{\perp}=\mathscr{P}_{n}
, for
n=m
, (
3.36
) was proved in
[
30
, Lemma 7.1.1, (7.1.6)]
by the use of so-called kernel calculus.
Let us now show that the general case of (
3.36
) can be reduced to this special one.
For this, remark that by (
1.26
), we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})=\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi}{2}|Z^{\prime}_{N}|^{2}\Big),

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})=\mathscr{P}_{n}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y}),

(3.40)

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})=\mathscr{P}_{n}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime})\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi}{2}|Z_{N}|^{2}\Big),

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime})=\mathscr{P}_{n}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}).

We decompose the polynomials
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
as follows

A_{1}(Z,Z^{\prime})=\sum_{\alpha}Z_{N}^{\alpha}\cdot A_{1}^{\alpha}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}),\qquad A_{2}(Z,Z^{\prime})=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}A_{2}^{\alpha^{\prime}}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})Z^{\prime}_{N}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}},

(3.41)

where
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2(n-m)}
,
|\alpha|\leq\deg A_{1},|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq\deg A_{2}
.
Now, by (
3.40
) and (
3.41
), we have

\Big((A_{1}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp})\circ(A_{2}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp})\Big)(Z,Z^{\prime})=\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi}{2}\big(|Z_{N}|^{2}+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|^{2}\big)\Big)\cdot\\
\cdot\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}Z_{N}^{\alpha}Z^{\prime}_{N}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\cdot\Big((A_{1}^{\alpha}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n})\circ(A_{2}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n})\Big)(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}).

(3.42)

From (
3.40
) and (
3.42
), we conclude

\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[A_{1},A_{2}](Z,Z^{\prime})=\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}Z_{N}^{\alpha}Z^{\prime}_{N}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{n,n}[A_{1}^{\alpha},A_{2}^{\alpha^{\prime}}](Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}).

(3.43)

Now, (
3.36
) follows from (
3.43
) and the fact that (
3.36
) holds for
n=m
.

Along the same lines, from (
3.40
), we obtain

\mathcal{K^{\prime}}_{n,m}[A_{1},A_{2}](Z,Z^{\prime})=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}Z^{\prime}_{N}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{n,n}[A_{1},A_{2}^{\alpha^{\prime}}](Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y}),

(3.44)

which implies (
3.37
) since (
3.36
) holds for
n=m
.

Now, by (
3.35
), we can write

\mathscr{E}_{n,m}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})=\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi}{2}|Z_{N}|^{2}\Big)\cdot\mathscr{P}_{m}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}).

(3.45)

From (
3.36
), (
3.40
) and (
3.45
), we deduce

\mathcal{K^{\prime\prime}}_{n,m}[A_{1},A_{2}](Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})=\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\Big[A_{1},\mathcal{K}_{m,m}[1,A_{2}]\Big](Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y}).

(3.46)

Now, (
3.38
) follows from (
3.46
) and the fact that (
3.36
) holds.

Similarly, using the notations from (
3.41
), we deduce

\mathcal{K^{\prime\prime\prime}}_{n,m}[A_{1},A_{2}](Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})=\sum_{\alpha}Z_{N}^{\alpha}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{m,m}\big[A_{1}^{\alpha},A_{2}\big](Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}),

(3.47)

which clearly implies (
3.39
).
∎

Remark 3.6
.

Directly from the definitions, for a polynomial
P
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[A_{1}\cdot P(Z^{\prime}),A_{2}]=\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[A_{1},P(Z)\cdot A_{2}],

(3.48)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[A_{1},A_{2}\cdot P(Z^{\prime})]=\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[A_{1},A_{2}]\cdot P(Z^{\prime}).

Using the kernel calculus from
[
30
, §7.1]
or the explicit calculations, one can verify, cf.
[
30
, (7.1.10)]
, that for
i,j\leq m
, the following holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[1,z_{i}z_{j}]=z_{i}z_{j},

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[1,z_{i}\overline{z}_{j}]=\frac{1}{\pi}\delta_{ij}+z_{i}\overline{z}^{\prime}_{j},

(3.49)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[1,\overline{z}_{i}\overline{z}_{j}]=\overline{z}^{\prime}_{i}\overline{z}^{\prime}_{j},

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[1,P_{i}(Z)z_{i}]=\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[1,P_{i}(Z)]z_{i},

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[1,P_{i}(Z)\overline{z}_{i}]=\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[1,P_{i}(Z)]\overline{z}^{\prime}_{i},

where the polynomial
P_{i}(Z)
doesn’t involve the variables
z_{i}
and
\overline{z}_{i}
.
Finally, for any
k=2m+1,\ldots,2n
, from the trivial fact
\int Z\exp(-\pi|Z|^{2})dZ=0
, we get

\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\Big[A_{1}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}),Z_{k}\cdot A_{2}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y})\Big]=0.

(3.50)

3.3
Algebra of operators with Taylor-type expansion of the Schwartz kernel

The main goal of this section is to prove that the set of operators, acting on the sections of a trivial vector bundle over
\mathbb{C}^{n}
by the convolution with Schwartz kernel admitting Taylor-type expansion and exponential decay away from
\mathbb{C}^{m}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
, forms an algebra under composition.
We also extend this result for operators on general triples of bounded geometry.

Let us now state precisely our results.
The proofs will be given in the end of this section.
We fix
q\in\mathbb{N}
,
q\geq 2
, and operators
\mathcal{G}_{t}
,
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{t}^{q}
,
t\in[0,1]
, acting on the sections of the trivial vector bundle
\mathbb{C}^{r_{0}}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
over
\mathbb{C}^{n}
by the convolutions with smooth kernels
\mathcal{G}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{1}(Z,Z^{\prime}),\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{t}^{q}(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\text{End}{(\mathbb{C}^{r_{0}})}
with respect to the volume form
dv_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}
on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
We assume that there are
c_{0},q_{1}>0
, such that for any
l\in\mathbb{N}
, there are
C>0
,
Q_{h,1}\geq 0
,
h=1,\ldots,q
, such that for any
t\in[0,1]
,
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\mathcal{A}_{t}^{h}(Z,Z^{\prime})\bigg|\leq C\Big(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q_{h,1}+q_{1}l}\exp\Big(-c_{0}\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|\big)\Big),

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\mathcal{G}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})\bigg|\leq C\Big(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q_{1,1}+q_{1}l}\exp\Big(-c_{0}|Z-Z^{\prime}|\Big).

(3.51)

Lemma 3.7
.

The operators
\mathcal{D}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{A}_{t}^{1}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{A}_{t}^{q}
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{G}_{t}\circ\mathcal{A}_{t}^{2}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{A}_{t}^{q}
are well-defined and have smooth Schwartz kernels
\mathcal{D}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})
with respect to
dv_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}
.
Moreover, for any
l\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
t\in[0,1]
,
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, we have

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\mathcal{R}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})\bigg|\leq C\Big(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q_{1,1}+\cdots+Q_{q,1}+q_{1}l}\cdot\\
\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{c_{0}}{8}\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|\big)\Big),

(3.52)

where
\mathcal{R}_{t}
designates either
\mathcal{D}_{t}
or
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{t}
.

Now, assume, in addition to (
3.51
), that for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
h\in 1,\ldots,q
, there are
\mathcal{J}_{r}^{h}(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\text{End}{(\mathbb{C}^{r_{0}})}
polynomials in
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
, such that
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{h}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{J}_{r}^{h}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}
(resp.
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{r}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{J}_{r}^{1}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n}
) appear as coefficients of Taylor-type expansion for
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{h}
(resp.
\mathcal{G}_{t}
).
More precisely, we assume that there are
\epsilon_{0},c_{1},q_{1},q_{2}>0
, such that for any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
,
h=1,\ldots,q
, there are
C>0
,
Q_{h,2}\geq 0
, such that for any
t\in[0,1]
,
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{t}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, we have

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\mathcal{A}_{t}^{h}(Z,Z^{\prime})-\sum_{r=0}^{k}t^{r}\mathcal{F}_{r}^{h}(Z,Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|\\
\leq Ct^{k+1}\Big(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q_{h,2}+q_{1}l+q_{2}k}\exp\Big(-c_{1}\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|\big)\Big).

(3.53)

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\mathcal{G}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})-\sum_{r=0}^{k}t^{r}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{r}(Z,Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|\\
\leq Ct^{k+1}\Big(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q_{1,2}+q_{1}l+q_{2}k}\exp\big(-c_{1}|Z-Z^{\prime}|\big).

(3.54)

Define polynomials
\mathcal{J}_{r,0}(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\text{End}{(\mathbb{C}^{r})}
(resp.
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}_{r,0}(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\text{End}{(\mathbb{C}^{r})}
),
r\in\mathbb{N}
, in
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
, as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{r,0}=\sum_{\lambda}\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\Big[\mathcal{J}_{\lambda_{1}}^{1},\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\Big[\mathcal{J}_{\lambda_{2}}^{2},\cdots,\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\big[\mathcal{J}_{\lambda_{q-1}}^{q-1},\mathcal{J}_{\lambda_{q}}^{q}\big]\cdots\Big],

(3.55)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{\prime}_{r,0}=\sum_{\lambda}\mathcal{K^{\prime}}_{n,m}\Big[\mathcal{J}_{\lambda_{1}}^{1},\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\Big[\mathcal{J}_{\lambda_{2}}^{2},\cdots,\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\big[\mathcal{J}_{\lambda_{q-1}}^{q-1},\mathcal{J}_{\lambda_{q}}^{q}\big]\cdots\Big],

where
\lambda
runs over all partitions
(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{q})
of
r
by natural numbers
\lambda_{i}
, and
\mathcal{K}_{n,m}
,
\mathcal{K^{\prime}}_{n,m}
are as in Lemma
3.5
.
We let
\mathcal{F}_{r,0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{J}_{r,0}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}
(resp.
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{r,0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{J}^{\prime}_{r,0}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}
).

Lemma 3.8
.

In the notations of (
3.51
), (
3.53
), for any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
t\in]0,1]
,
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{2t}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, and for
Q\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\max\{\sum_{h=1}^{q}Q_{h,1},\sum_{h=1}^{q}Q_{h,2}\}
,
c_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\min\{c_{0},c_{1}\}
, the following estimate holds

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(R_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})-\sum_{r=0}^{k}t^{r}\mathcal{G}_{r,0}(Z,Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|\\
\leq Ct^{k+1}\Big(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q+q_{1}l+q_{2}k}\exp\Big(-\frac{c_{2}}{8}\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|\big)\Big),

(3.56)

where
\mathcal{R}_{t}
(resp.
\mathcal{G}_{r,0}
) designates either
\mathcal{D}_{t}
or
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{t}
(resp. either
\mathcal{F}_{r,0}
or
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{r,0}
).

Now, let us formulate similar results for general complex manifolds.
More precisely, let
(X,Y,g^{TX})
be a triple of bounded geometry.
Let
dv_{X}
be a volume form over
X
, satisfying (
1.4
).
We fix a Hermitian line (resp. vector) bundle
(L,h^{L})
(resp.
(F,h^{F})
) on
X
.

Let us fix
q\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and a sequence of operators
A_{p}^{1},\ldots,A_{p}^{q}
,
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, acting on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
by the convolutions with smooth kernels
A_{p}^{1}(x_{1},x_{2}),\ldots,A_{p}^{q}(x_{1},x_{2})\in(L^{p}\otimes F)_{x_{1}}\otimes(L^{p}\otimes F)_{x_{2}}^{*}
with respect to the volume form
dv_{X}
.
We assume that
A_{p}^{1},\ldots,A_{p}^{q}
satisfy the assumptions (
3.2
) and (
3.3
) for
n\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=2n
and
W\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=Y
.

We fix
y_{0}\in Y
and trivialize
(L,h^{L})
(resp.
(F,h^{F})
) and associated dual vector bundles in a neighborhood of
y_{0}
using Fermi coordinates and parallel transport with respect to
\nabla^{L}
(resp.
\nabla^{F}
) as we did before Theorem
1.6
.
Assume that for any
h=1,\ldots,q
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, there are
J_{r,h}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
polynomials in
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
, such that for
F_{r,h}^{\perp}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{r,h}^{\perp}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}
, the following holds.

There are
\epsilon_{0},c_{1},q_{1},q_{2}>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
,
h=1,\ldots,q
, there are
C>0
,
Q_{h,3}\geq 0
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z=(Z_{Y},Z_{N})
,
Z^{\prime}=(Z^{\prime}_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{N})
,
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
Z_{N},Z^{\prime}_{N}\in\real^{2(n-m)}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon_{0}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, the following bound holds

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{p^{n}}A_{p}^{h}\big(\psi_{y_{0}}(Z),\psi_{y_{0}}(Z^{\prime})\big)-\sum_{r=0}^{k}p^{-\frac{r}{2}}F_{r,h}^{\perp}(\sqrt{p}Z,\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime})\kappa_{X}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z)\kappa_{X}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|\\
\leq Cp^{-\frac{k+1-l}{2}}\Big(1+\sqrt{p}|Z|+\sqrt{p}|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q_{h,3}+q_{1}l+q_{2}k}\cdot\\
\cdot\exp\Big(-c_{1}\sqrt{p}\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|\big)\Big).

(3.57)

Define polynomials
J_{r,D}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, in
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
, as follows

J_{r,D}^{\perp}=\sum_{\lambda}\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\Big[J_{\lambda_{1},1}^{\perp},\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\Big[J_{\lambda_{2},2}^{\perp},\cdots,\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\big[J_{\lambda_{q-1},q-1}^{\perp},J_{\lambda_{q},q}^{\perp}\big]\cdots\Big],

(3.58)

where
\lambda
runs over all partitions
(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{q})
of
r
by natural numbers
\lambda_{i}
, and let
F_{r,D}^{\perp}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{r,D}^{\perp}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}
.

Lemma 3.9
.

In the notations of (
3.2
), (
3.3
), (
3.57
), for any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z=(Z_{Y},Z_{N})
,
Z^{\prime}=(Z^{\prime}_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{N})
,
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
Z_{N},Z^{\prime}_{N}\in\real^{2(n-m)}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{2}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, for
D_{p}
as in (
3.4
),
Q\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{h=1}^{q}Q_{h,3}
,
c_{2}=\min\{c_{0},c_{1}\}
, we have

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{p^{n}}D_{p}\big(\psi_{y_{0}}(Z),\psi_{y_{0}}(Z^{\prime})\big)-\sum_{r=0}^{k}p^{-\frac{r}{2}}F_{r,D}^{\perp}(\sqrt{p}Z,\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime})\kappa_{X}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z)\kappa_{X}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|\\
\leq Cp^{-\frac{k+1-l}{2}}\Big(1+\sqrt{p}|Z|+\sqrt{p}|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q+q_{1}l+q_{2}k}\cdot\\
\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{c_{2}}{8}\sqrt{p}\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|\big)\Big).

(3.59)

Let
dv_{Y}
be a volume form over
Y
, satisfying (
1.4
).
We fix an operator
C_{p}
,
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, acting on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
by the convolution with smooth kernel
C_{p}(y_{1},y_{2})
,
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
, with respect to the volume form
dv_{Y}
.
Assume there is
c_{2}>0
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that

\big|C_{p}(y_{1},y_{2})\big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(Y\times Y)}\leq Cp^{m+\frac{k}{2}}\exp\big(-c_{2}\sqrt{p}{\rm{dist}}(y_{1},y_{2})\big).

(3.60)

Assume that for a fixed
y_{0}\in Y
, for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
, there are
J_{r,C}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y})\in\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
polynomials in
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{m}
, such that for
F_{r,C}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{r,C}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{m}
, the following condition holds.
There are
\epsilon_{0},c_{3},q_{1},q_{2}>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
, there are
C>0
,
Q_{C}\geq 0
, such that for
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
|Z_{Y}|,|Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\leq\epsilon_{0}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2m}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, the following bound holds

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z_{Y}^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}_{Y}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{p^{m}}C_{p}\big(\psi_{y_{0}}(Z_{Y}),\psi_{y_{0}}(Z^{\prime}_{Y})\big)-\sum_{r=0}^{k}p^{-\frac{r}{2}}F_{r,C}(\sqrt{p}Z_{Y},\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime}_{Y})\kappa_{Y}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z_{Y})\kappa_{Y}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z^{\prime}_{Y})\bigg)\bigg|\\
\leq Cp^{-\frac{k+1-l}{2}}\Big(1+\sqrt{p}|Z_{Y}|+\sqrt{p}|Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\Big)^{Q_{C}+q_{1}l+q_{2}k}\exp\Big(-c_{3}\sqrt{p}|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\Big).

(3.61)

Recall that
\kappa_{N}
and
{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}
were defined in (
1.7
) and (
1.9
) respectively.
We denote

D_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=A_{p}^{1}\circ(\kappa_{N}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0})\circ C_{p}.

(3.62)

Define polynomials
J_{r,D}^{E}(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, in
Z\in\real^{2n}
,
Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2m}
, by

J_{r,D}^{E}=\sum_{r_{0}=0}^{r}\mathcal{K^{\prime\prime}}_{n,m}[J_{r_{0},1}^{\perp},J_{r-r_{0},C}],

(3.63)

and let
F_{r,D}^{E}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{r,D}^{E}\cdot\mathscr{E}_{n,m}
.

Lemma 3.10
.

In the notations of (
3.2
), (
3.57
), (
3.60
), (
3.61
), for any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z=(Z_{Y},Z_{N})
,
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
Z_{N}\in\real^{2(n-m)}

|Z|,|Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\leq\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{2}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2m}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
,
Q\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=Q_{1,3}+Q_{C}
,
c_{4}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\min\{c_{0},c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\}
, we have

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}_{Y}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{p^{m}}D_{p}\big(\psi_{y_{0}}(Z),\psi_{y_{0}}(Z^{\prime}_{Y})\big)-\sum_{r=0}^{k}p^{-\frac{r}{2}}F_{r,D}^{E}(\sqrt{p}Z,\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime}_{Y})\kappa_{X}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z)\kappa_{Y}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z^{\prime}_{Y})\bigg)\bigg|\\
\leq Cp^{-\frac{k+1-l}{2}}\Big(1+\sqrt{p}|Z|+\sqrt{p}|Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\Big)^{Q+q_{1}l+q_{2}k}\exp\Big(-\frac{c_{4}}{8}\sqrt{p}\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|\big)\Big).

(3.64)

Proof of Lemma
3.7
.

The proof is completely analogous to the proof of Lemma
3.1
, done for
W\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
. The only change needed in the proof is that instead of Proposition
3.2
, one has to use that for any
c>0
,
Q\geq 0
, there is a constant
C
, such that for any
A\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}|Z|^{Q}\exp(-c|Z-A|)dZ_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dZ_{2n}\leq C(1+|A|^{Q}).

(3.65)

This fact can be shown easily by the change of variables
Z\mapsto Z+A
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.8
.

To simplify the presentation, we restrict ourselves to the case
l=0
, as the general case is treated in an analogous way.
We present first the proof that the asymptotic expansion (
3.56
) holds for
k=0
.
We only treat the asymptotic expansion of
\mathcal{D}_{t}
, as for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{t}
the proof is analogous.
Analogously to (
3.19
), we have

\mathcal{D}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})=\int_{(\real^{2n})^{\times(q-1)}}\mathcal{A}_{t}^{1}(Z,Z_{1})\mathcal{A}_{t}^{2}(Z_{1},Z_{2})\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathcal{A}_{t}^{q}(Z_{q-1},Z^{\prime})\cdot\\
\cdot dv_{\real{}^{2n}}(Z_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot dv_{\real{}^{2n}}(Z_{q-1})

(3.66)

We decompose the integral (
3.66
) into a sum of two integrals.
The first one is over the set
Q_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{B}_{0}^{\real{}^{2n}}(\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{t})^{\times(q-1)}
, and the second one is over its complement
Q_{t}^{c}
.
Let us bound the contribution in (
3.66
) coming from the integral on
Q_{t}^{c}
.
Similarly to (
3.20
), for any
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{2t}
, and any
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{s-1})\in Q_{t}^{c}
, we have

{\rm{dist}}(Z,Z_{1})+{\rm{dist}}(Z_{1},Z_{2})+\cdots+{\rm{dist}}(Z_{s-1},Z^{\prime})\geq\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{2t}.

(3.67)

By (
3.51
), similarly to the proof of Lemma
3.1
and (
3.65
), there is
C>0
, such that

\bigg|\int_{Q_{t}^{c}}\mathcal{A}_{t}^{1}(Z,Z_{1})\cdot\mathcal{A}_{t}^{2}(Z_{1},Z_{2})\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathcal{A}_{t}^{q}(Z_{q-1},Z^{\prime})dv_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}(Z_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot dv_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}(Z_{q-1})\bigg|\\
\leq C(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|)^{\sum_{h=1}^{q}Q_{h,1}}\exp\Big(-\frac{c}{8}\Big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|+\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{2t}\Big)\Big).

(3.68)

From the fact that for any
c,\epsilon>0
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a constant
C>0
, such that
\exp(-c\epsilon/t)<Ct^{k}
, for any
t\in]0,1]
, implies that the right-hand side of, (
3.68
) is majorated by the right-hand side of (
3.53
), and hence, the contribution of the integral over
Q_{t}^{c}
is negligible.

Now, let
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
be as in Lemma
3.5
.
By (
3.68
), we see that for any
\epsilon>0
, there is
C>0
, which depends only on
k,A_{1},A_{2}
, such that for
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2t}
, we have

\bigg|(A_{3}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp})(Z,Z^{\prime})-\int_{|Z_{1}|<\frac{\epsilon}{t}}(A_{1}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp})(Z,Z_{1})\cdot(A_{2}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp})(Z_{1},Z^{\prime})dv_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}(Z_{1})\bigg|\\
\leq C(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|)^{\deg A_{1}+\deg A_{2}}\exp\Big(-\frac{c}{8}\Big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|+\frac{\epsilon}{2t}\Big)\Big).

(3.69)

Of course, an estimate as in (
3.69
), holds for any number of polynomials.
From this and (
3.68
), we deduce the first part of Lemma
3.8
for
k=0
.

Now, let us extend the argument for general
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, in the notations of (
3.53
), let us denote

\mathcal{B}_{t}^{l,k^{\prime}}(Z,Z^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{r=0}^{k^{\prime}}t^{r}\mathcal{F}_{r}^{l}(Z,Z^{\prime}).

(3.70)

Then we decompose
\mathcal{D}_{t}
into a sum of elements
\mathcal{C}_{t}^{1}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{C}_{t}^{q}
, where each
\mathcal{C}_{t}^{l}
,
l=1,\ldots,q
, is either equal to
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{l}-\mathcal{B}_{t}^{l,k^{\prime}}
or to
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{l,k^{\prime}}
, and the sum of
k^{\prime}
for all the multiplicands adds up to
k
.
For the term, which consists of the compositions of
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{l}
, we apply (
3.68
) and (
3.69
) to conclude that it has an asymptotic expansion of the form (
3.56
) up to the error term of the form as in the right-hand side of (
3.68
).
From (
3.69
) and the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[\cdot,\cdot]
from Lemma
3.5
, the coefficients of this asymptotic expansion are given by polynomials
\mathcal{J}_{r,0}
, defined in (
3.55
).

For other terms, we decompose the integral into two parts: over
Q_{t}
and over
Q_{t}^{c}
.
To bound the contribution from
Q_{t}
, we use (
3.53
) and Lemma
3.7
.
To bound the contribution from
Q_{t}^{c}
, we use (
3.51
) and proceed as in (
3.68
).
By combining the two bounds, we see that they contribute no more than the right-hand side of (
3.53
).
This finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.9
.

The proof is very similar to the proof of Lemma
3.8
, so we only highlight the main steps.
We fix
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|<\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{2}
, and let
x=\psi_{y_{0}}(Z)
,
x^{\prime}=\psi_{y_{0}}(Z^{\prime})
.
We decompose the integral in the formula (
3.19
), into two parts.
The first one is over the set
Q\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(\epsilon_{0})^{\times(q-1)}
, and the second one is over its complement
Q^{c}
.
Similarly to (
3.68
), but relying on Proposition
3.2
, we deduce the bound

\bigg|\int_{Q^{c}}A_{p}^{1}(x,x_{1})\cdot A_{p}^{2}(x_{1},x_{2})\cdot\ldots\cdot A_{p}^{q}(x_{q-1},x^{\prime})dv_{X}(x_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot dv_{X}(x_{q-1})\bigg|\\
\leq Cp^{n+\frac{k}{2}}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{c}{8}\sqrt{p}\cdot\big({\rm{dist}}(x,x^{\prime})+{\rm{dist}}(x,Y)+{\rm{dist}}(x^{\prime},Y)+\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{2}\big)\Big),

(3.71)

But since for any
c,\epsilon>0
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that
\exp(-c\epsilon\sqrt{p})<Cp^{-k}
, for any
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, the right-hand side of (
3.71
) is majorated by the right-hand side of (
3.59
) and, hence, negligible.
Now, to deal with the integration over
Q
, we pass to
\psi_{y_{0}}
-coordinates.
The change of the variables introduces the
\kappa_{X}
factor for every volume form.
It will be canceled with the two
\kappa_{X}^{-1/2}
factors, which appear in the asymptotic expansion (
3.57
).
Once two factors are canceled, and we make a change of the variables
Z\mapsto\frac{Z}{\sqrt{p}}
, we reduce the problem to the estimates of the form (
3.69
).
The proof is now finished exactly as in the proof of Lemma
3.8
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.10
.

The proof is analogous to the proof of Lemma
3.9
with only one change: instead of relying on (
3.36
) in the estimate (
3.69
), one has to rely on (
3.38
).
The reason why the factor
\kappa_{N}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
appears in (
3.62
) is due to the identity

\kappa_{X}(Z)=\kappa_{N}(\psi_{y_{0}}(Z))\cdot\kappa_{Y}(Z_{Y}),

(3.72)

which implies that the term
\kappa_{X}
, appearing after the passage to
\psi_{y_{0}}
-coordinates, disappear with the terms
\kappa_{X}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and
\kappa_{Y}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, which appear in the Taylor-type expansions of
A_{p}
and
C_{p}
. ∎

4
Spectral bound for the restriction operator

The main goal of this section is to prove a spectral bound for the restriction operator.

More precisely, we conserve the notation from Section
1
and assume that the triple
(X,Y,g^{TX})
is of bounded geometry.
Consider the restriction operator
{\rm{Res}}_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H^{0,\perp}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, defined as
f\mapsto f|_{Y}
.
As we prove in Proposition
4.8
, the restriction to
Y
of an element from
H^{0,\perp}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
lies in
H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
.
Hence, we may view
{\rm{Res}}_{p}
as

{\rm{Res}}_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H^{0,\perp}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)\to H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F)).

(4.1)

The main result of this section goes as follows.

Theorem 4.1
.

There are
c,C>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
, we have

cp^{\frac{n-m}{2}}\leq\big\|{\rm{Res}}_{p}\big\|\leq Cp^{\frac{n-m}{2}},

(4.2)

where
\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert}
is the operator norm with respect to the
L^{2}
-scalar products (
1.3
) on
X
and
Y
.

Remark 4.2
.

a)
For compact manifolds, a similar statement appeared in Sun
[
41
, Theorem 3.3]
for
(F,h^{F})
trivial.
However, only the lower bound was discussed there, and there is a gap in the proof. See Sun
[
41
]
for the explanation of the error and its correction, relying on the current article.

b)
A more precise version of (
4.2
), containing the asymptotics of
\|{\rm{Res}}_{p}\|
, will be given in
[
18
]
.

This section is organized as follows.
In Section
4.1
, we calculate the first two terms of the Taylor expansion of the
\overline{\partial}
-operator in a shrinking tubular neighborhood of
Y
.
This will play a crucial role in our proof of the lower bound of Theorem
4.1
in Section
4.2
.
In Section
4.3
, we establish the upper bound of Theorem
4.1
.
Throughout the section the variables
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
t\in\real
are related by

t=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}.

(4.3)

4.1
Taylor expansion of the holomorphic differential near submanifold

The main goal of this section is to calculate the first two terms of the Taylor expansion of
\overline{\partial}^{L^{p}\otimes F}
-operator, considered in a shrinking neighborhood of
Y
of size
\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}
, as
p\to\infty
.
Our result is motivated by the Taylor expansions of the associated Dirac operator due to Bismut-Lebeau
[
4
, Theorem 8.18]
, which corresponds to trivial
(L,h^{L})
in our setting, and the Taylor expansion due to Dai-Liu-Ma
[
11
, Theorem 4.6]
, which corresponds to
Y
equal to a point in our setting.

More precisely, as in Section
1
, we consider a triple
(X,Y,g^{TX})
of bounded geometry.
By means of the exponential map as in (
1.7
), we identify a neighborhood of the zero section
\mathbb{B}_{r_{\perp}}(N)
in the normal bundle
N
, to a neighborhood
U\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(r_{\perp})
of
Y
in
X
.

Recall that the projection
\pi_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U\to Y
and the identifications of
L,F
to
\pi_{0}^{*}(L|_{Y}),\pi_{0}^{*}(F|_{Y})
in
\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(r_{\perp})
were defined before (
1.9
).
We similarly identify
TX
to
\pi_{0}^{*}(TX|_{Y})
over
\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(r_{\perp})
using the parallel transport with respect to the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{TX}
.
Remark that since
g^{TX}
is Kähler by (
1.2
), the decomposition
TX\otimes\mathbb{C}=T^{(1,0)}X\oplus T^{(0,1)}X
is preserved by
\nabla^{TX}
, cf.
[
30
, Theorem 1.2.8]
. In other words, the identification of
TX
with
\pi_{0}^{*}(TX|_{Y})
induces the identifications

\tau\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\pi_{0}^{*}(T^{(1,0)}X|_{Y})\to T^{(1,0)}X|_{U},\qquad\tau\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\pi_{0}^{*}(T^{(0,1)}X|_{Y})\to T^{(0,1)}X|_{U}.

(4.4)

We define the
1
-forms
\Gamma^{F}
,
\Gamma^{L}
with values in
\text{End}{(\pi_{0}^{*}(F|_{Y}))}
,
\text{End}{(\pi_{0}^{*}(L|_{Y}))}
as in (
2.36
) using the above isomorphisms.
Recall also that the connection
\nabla^{N}
on
N
was introduced before (
1.16
).
The connection
\nabla^{N}
induces the splitting

TN=N\oplus T^{H}N

(4.5)

of the tangent space of the total space of
N
. Here
T^{H}N
is the horizontal part of
N
with respect to the connection
\nabla^{N}
.
If
U\in TY
, we denote by
U^{H}\in T^{H}N
the horizontal lift of
U
in
T^{H}N
.

For
\epsilon>0
, we denote by
\mathbb{E}(\epsilon)
(resp.
\mathbb{E}
) the set of smooth sections of
\pi_{0}^{*}(L^{p}|_{Y}\otimes F|_{Y})
on
\mathbb{B}_{\epsilon}(N)
(resp. on the total space of
N
).
We also denote by
\mathbb{E}^{(0,1)}(\epsilon)
(resp.
\mathbb{E}^{(0,1)}
) the set of smooth sections of
\pi_{0}^{*}(T^{*(0,1)}X|_{Y})\otimes\pi_{0}^{*}(L^{p}|_{Y}\otimes F|_{Y})
on
\mathbb{B}_{\epsilon}(N)
(resp. on the total space of
N
).

Clearly, the above isomorphisms allow us to see
\overline{\partial}^{L^{p}\otimes F}
as an operator

\overline{\partial}^{L^{p}\otimes F}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{E}(r_{\perp})\to\mathbb{E}^{(0,1)}(r_{\perp}).

(4.6)

We fix a point
y_{0}\in Y
and an orthonormal frame
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2m})
(resp.
(e_{2m+1},\ldots,e_{2n})
) in
(T_{y_{0}}Y,g^{TY})
(resp. in
(N_{y_{0}},g^{N}_{y_{0}})
) such that (
1.17
) is satisfied.
Using the exponential coordinates on
Y
and the parallel transport of
(e_{2m+1},\ldots,e_{2n})
along the geodesics on
Y
, as in Fermi coordinates
\psi_{y_{0}}
in (
1.18
), we introduce complex coordinates
z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}
on
Y
and linear “vertical” coordinates
z_{m+1},\ldots,z_{n}
on
N
.
Using those coordinates, we define the operators

\overline{\partial}_{H}^{L^{p}\otimes F},\mathcal{L}_{N}^{L^{p}\otimes F}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{E}^{(0,1)},

(4.7)

by prescribing their action at a point
(y_{0},Z_{N})
,
Z_{N}\in\real^{2(n-m)}
, as follows

\overline{\partial}_{H}^{L^{p}\otimes F}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}d\overline{z}_{i}|_{y_{0}}\cdot\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}\big|_{y_{0}}\Big)^{H},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{N}^{L^{p}\otimes F}=\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}d\overline{z}_{i}|_{y_{0}}\cdot\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}+\frac{\pi z_{i}}{2}\Big).

(4.8)

The first differentiation in (
4.8
) is well-defined because
\pi_{0*}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}|_{y_{0}})^{H}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}|_{y_{0}}
is of type
(0,1)
, and the second derivation is well-defined because the vector bundles are trivialized along fibers of
\pi_{0}
.

We use notation (
4.3
), and for any
\epsilon>0
define the rescaling operator
F_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{E}(\epsilon)\to\mathbb{E}(\frac{\epsilon}{t})
for
f\in\mathbb{E}(\epsilon)
as follows

(F_{t}f)(y,Z_{N})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=f\big(y,tZ_{N}\big),\qquad(y,Z_{N})\in\mathbb{B}_{\frac{\epsilon}{t}}(N).

(4.9)

The operator
F_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{E}^{(0,1)}(\epsilon)\to\mathbb{E}^{(0,1)}(\frac{\epsilon}{t})
is defined in an analogous way.

Theorem 4.3
.

As
p\to\infty
, we have

F_{t}\circ\overline{\partial}^{L^{p}\otimes F}\circ F_{t}^{-1}=\frac{1}{t}\mathcal{L}_{N}^{L^{p}\otimes F}+\overline{\partial}_{H}^{L^{p}\otimes F}+O\big(t|Z_{N}|^{2}\nabla_{N}+t|Z_{N}|\nabla_{H}+t|Z_{N}|\big),

(4.10)

where
O(t|Z_{N}|^{2}\nabla_{N}+t|Z_{N}|\nabla_{H}+t|Z_{N}|)

is an operator of the form

\sum_{i=1}^{2m}a_{i}(t,y,Z_{N})\cdot dx_{i}|_{y_{0}}\cdot(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}|_{y_{0}})^{H}+\sum_{j=2m+1}^{2n}b_{j}(t,y,Z_{N})\cdot dx_{j}|_{y_{0}}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}+c(t,y,Z_{N}),

such that there is a constant
C>0
, for which
|a_{i}(t,y,Z_{N})|\leq Ct|Z_{N}|^{2}
,
|b_{j}(t,y,Z_{N})|\leq Ct|Z_{N}|
,
|c(t,y,Z_{N})|\leq Ct|Z_{N}|
holds for any
y\in Y
,
|Z_{N}|<r_{\perp}
,
i=1,\ldots,m
, and
j=m+1,\ldots,n
.

Proof.

To simplify the notations, we denote
\frac{\tau\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\tau(\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}|_{y_{0}})
and
\frac{\tau\partial}{\partial Z_{i}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\tau(\frac{\partial}{\partial Z_{i}}|_{y_{0}})
.
By (
4.4
), the action of the operator
\overline{\partial}^{L^{p}\otimes F}
, viewed as in (
4.6
), at a point
(y_{0},Z_{N})
,
|Z_{N}|<r_{\perp}
, is given by

\overline{\partial}^{L^{p}\otimes F}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}dz_{i}|_{y_{0}}\cdot\frac{\tau\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}dz_{i}|_{y_{0}}\cdot\Big(p\Gamma^{L}_{(y_{0},Z_{N})}\big(\frac{\tau\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}\big)+\Gamma^{F}_{(y_{0},Z_{N})}\big(\frac{\tau\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}\big)\Big).

(4.11)

A calculation from Bismut-Lebeau
[
4
, p. 94-96]
shows

F_{t}\circ\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}dz_{i}|_{y_{0}}\cdot\frac{\tau\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}\Big)\circ F_{t}^{-1}=\frac{1}{t}\Big(\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}d\overline{z}_{i}|_{y_{0}}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}\Big)+\overline{\partial}_{H}^{L^{p}\otimes F}\\
-\dim Y\cdot\nu^{*}(y)\wedge+O(t|Z_{N}|^{2}\nabla_{N}+t|Z_{N}|\nabla_{H}),

(4.12)

where the
O
-term is interpreted as in (
4.10
), and
\nu\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(Y,N)
is the dual of the
mean curvature
of
\iota
, defined as follows

\nu\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{1}{2m}\sum_{i=1}^{2m}A(e_{i})e_{i},

(4.13)

where the sum runs over an orthonormal basis
e_{1},\ldots,e_{2m}
of
(TY,g^{TY})
as in (
1.17
).
However, as the manifolds are Kähler (hence,
J
commutes with
A
), by (
2.35
), for
i=1,\ldots,m
, we have

A(e_{2i-1})e_{2i-1}=A(Je_{2i})Je_{2i}=JA(Je_{2i})e_{2i}=JA(e_{2i})Je_{2i}=-A(e_{2i})e_{2i}.

(4.14)

Hence, we have

\nu=0.

(4.15)

Remark that in
[
4
]
, no assumption of bounded geometry was made, and (
4.12
) was stated locally in
Y
.
However, in the proof of Bismut-Lebeau, the
O
-term comes from the expansion of the Christoffel symbols and the connection forms, and hence it can be bounded uniformly in
Y
by the results of Section
2.1
.

Now, from Lemma
2.11
and (
2.33
), we see that for
i=1,\ldots,2m
,
j=2m+1,\ldots,2n
, at the point
(y_{0},Z_{N})
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\tau\partial}{\partial Z_{j}}=\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial Z_{j}}+O(|Z_{N}|^{2}),

(4.16)

\displaystyle\frac{\tau\partial}{\partial Z_{i}}=\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial Z_{i}}-\sum_{l=1}^{2m}\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial Z_{l}}g^{TM}_{y_{0}}\big(A(e_{i})Z_{N},e_{l}\big)+O(|Z_{N}|^{2}).

From Lemma
2.14
and (
4.16
), we deduce

\displaystyle\Gamma^{F}_{(y_{0},Z_{N})}\big(\frac{\tau\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}\big)=O(|Z_{N}|),

\displaystyle\Gamma^{F}_{(y_{0},Z_{N})}\big(\frac{\tau\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{j}}\big)=O(|Z_{N}|),

(4.17)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{L}_{(y_{0},Z_{N})}\big(\frac{\tau\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}\big)=O(|Z_{N}|^{3}),

\displaystyle\Gamma^{L}_{(y_{0},Z_{N})}\big(\frac{\tau\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{j}}\big)=\frac{1}{2}R^{L}_{y_{0}}\big(Z_{N},\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{j}}\big)+O(|Z_{N}|^{3}).

Now, from (
4.11
), (
4.12
), (
4.15
) and (
4.17
), we get

F_{t}\circ\overline{\partial}^{L^{p}\otimes F}\circ F_{t}^{-1}=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}d\overline{z}_{i}\cdot\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}+\frac{1}{2}R^{L}_{y_{0}}\big(Z_{N},\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}\big)\Big)+\overline{\partial}_{H}^{L^{p}\otimes F}\\
+O(t|Z_{N}|^{2}\nabla_{N}+t|Z_{N}|\nabla_{H}+t|Z_{N}|).

(4.18)

Finally, by (
1.1
), we have
R^{L}_{y_{0}}(Z_{N},\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}})=\pi z_{i}
.
By this and (
4.18
), we conclude.
∎

4.2
Extension theorem, a proof of the lower bound in Theorem
4.1

In this section, we establish the lower bound in Theorem
4.1
.
For this, in the notations from Section
4.1
, we show that the following refined version of Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem holds.

Theorem 4.4
.

There are
C>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
and
g\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, there is
f\in H^{0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
, such that
f|_{Y}=g
and

\mathinner{\!\left\lVert f\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(X)}\leq\frac{C}{p^{\frac{n-m}{2}}}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert g\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(Y)}.

(4.19)

Proof of the lower bound in Theorem
4.1
.

Let
C
be as in (
4.19
). As
{\rm{E}}_{p}
is defined as the minimal extension with respect to the
L^{2}
-norm, for any
g\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, by Theorem
4.4
, we have

\big\|{\rm{E}}_{p}g\big\|_{L^{2}(X)}\leq\frac{C}{p^{\frac{n-m}{2}}}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert g\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(Y)}.

(4.20)

This means exactly that the lower bound in Theorem
4.1
holds for
c\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C^{-1}
.
∎

By the above, to settle the main goal of this section, we need to establish Theorem
4.4
.
The main idea of the proof of Theorem
4.4
is to pass through the general framework of the proof of Ohsawa-Takegoshi extension theorem, cf.
[
13
, §11]
. We choose a smooth extension of
g
over
X
, and then obtain the holomorphic extension by modifying the smooth one using a solution of
\overline{\partial}
-equation with singular weight, which forces the solution to annihilate along
Y
.

The novelty here is that instead of choosing an arbitrary smooth extension, as it is done in
[
13
, §11]
, we choose a specific one, given by the operator (
1.9
).
The incentive for doing so comes from the fact that we would like to get some estimates, related to this extension, which would be uniform in
p
, and this doesn’t seem doable for an arbitrary choice of the extension.
Choosing a specific extension allows us to use Theorem
4.3
to prove the uniform versions of some
L^{2}
-estimates, which are indispensable in the proof.
For those estimates and after, we need the following proposition.

Proposition 4.5
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there are
C>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
and
f\in H^{0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
, we have

\big\|\nabla^{k}f\big\|_{L^{2}(X)}\leq Cp^{\frac{k}{2}}\big\|f\big\|_{L^{2}(X)},

(4.21)

where
\nabla
is the covariant derivative with respect to the induced Chern and Levi-Civita connections.

For the proof of Proposition
4.5
and in many places later, the following result will be crucial.

Theorem 4.6

(Ma-Marinescu
[
32
, Theorem 1]
)
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there are
c,C>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that for
p\geq p_{1}
, we have

\Big|B_{p}^{X}(x_{1},x_{2})\Big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(X\times X)}\leq Cp^{n+\frac{k}{2}}\cdot\exp\big(-c\sqrt{p}\cdot{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})\big),

(4.22)

where
\mathscr{C}^{k}
-norm here is interpreted as in Theorem
1.5
.

Proof of Proposition
4.5
.

For compact manifolds, the statement is very classical.
For manifolds of bounded geometry, the proof can be obtained by a slight modification of the argument in
[
32
, Lemma 2]
.
We present here an alternative proof, based on Theorem
4.6
.
First of all, by Proposition
3.2
and Theorem
4.6
, there are
C>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that for any
x_{0}\in X
, for
p\geq p_{1}
, we have

\int_{X}\big|(\nabla^{k}B_{p}^{X})(x_{0},x)\big|dv_{X}(x)\leq Cp^{\frac{k}{2}},\qquad\int_{X}\big|(\nabla^{k}B_{p}^{X})(x,x_{0})\big|dv_{X}(x)\leq Cp^{\frac{k}{2}}.

(4.23)

Also, since
f=B_{p}^{X}f
,
\nabla^{k}f
admits the integral representation

\nabla^{k}f(x_{1})=\int_{X}(\nabla^{k}B_{p}^{X})(x_{1},x_{2})\cdot f(x_{2})dv_{X}(x_{2}).

(4.24)

We deduce (
4.21
) directly from (
4.23
), (
4.24
) and Young’s inequality for integral operators, cf.
[
40
, Theorem 0.3.1]
applied for
p,q=2
,
r=1
in the notations of
[
40
]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

Recall that the operator
{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}
was defined in (
1.9
).
We would like to verify that for any
g\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
,
y_{0}\in Y
, we have

\alpha(y_{0})=0,\quad\text{where}\quad\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\overline{\partial}^{L^{p}\otimes F}({\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}g).

(4.25)

Indeed, let us work in a neighborhood
V\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})
of
Y
in
X
.
Recall that
t\in\real_{+}
and
F_{t}
were defined in (
4.9
).
Then in the notations of (
1.9
), on
V
, we have

{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}g=F_{t}^{-1}\tilde{g},\qquad\tilde{g}(y,Z_{N})=g(y)\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi}{2}|Z_{N}|^{2}\Big).

(4.26)

Recall that
\overline{\partial}_{H}^{L^{p}\otimes F},\mathcal{L}_{N}^{L^{p}\otimes F}
were defined in (
4.8
).
A trivial calculation shows that on
V
, we have

\mathcal{L}_{N}^{L^{p}\otimes F}\tilde{g}=0.

(4.27)

Also, since
\nabla^{N}
preserves
g^{N}
, in the notations of (
4.8
), similarly to
[
4
, (8.97)]
, we have

\Big(\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}\big|_{y_{0}}\Big)^{H}\tilde{g}\Big)(y_{0},Z_{N})=\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}}g\Big)(y_{0})\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi}{2}|Z_{N}|^{2}\Big).

(4.28)

As a consequence of (
4.28
) and the fact that
g
is holomorphic, we obtain

\overline{\partial}_{H}^{L^{p}\otimes F}\tilde{g}=0.

(4.29)

From (
4.26
), (
4.27
), (
4.29
) and the fact that all the residue terms in Theorem
4.3
contain
|Z_{N}|
, we deduce (
4.25
).

Now, using the
L^{2}
-estimates, let us construct a holomorphic perturbation of
{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}g
, satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
4.4
.
Recall that
\delta_{Y}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\setminus Y\to\real
,
\alpha_{Y}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\to\real
, were defined in (
2.60
).
For
\epsilon>0
, let us now define the weight
\delta_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\setminus Y\to\real
as follows

\delta_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=2(n-m)\delta_{Y}-\epsilon p\alpha_{Y}.

(4.30)

By taking
\epsilon
small, by Lemma
2.19
, we see that there exists
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
, over
X
, the following inequality holds in the distributional sense

p\omega+\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}\delta_{p}>\frac{p}{2}\omega.

(4.31)

Let us fix
\epsilon
small enough, such that for any
|Z_{N}|<r_{\perp}
, we have

\frac{\pi}{2}|Z_{N}|^{2}-\epsilon\alpha_{Y}(y,Z_{N})\geq\frac{\pi}{4}|Z_{N}|^{2}.

(4.32)

We will now prove that there are
C_{1}>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
g\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
and
\alpha
defined in (
4.25
), we have

\int_{X}|\alpha|^{2}e^{-\delta_{p}}dv_{X}\leq C_{1}\|g\|_{L^{2}(Y)}^{2}.

(4.33)

Since
\alpha
has support over
\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(r_{\perp})
, we have

\int_{X\setminus\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})}|\alpha|^{2}e^{-\delta_{p}}dv_{X}=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(r_{\perp})\setminus\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})}|\alpha|^{2}e^{-\delta_{p}}dv_{X}

(4.34)

From this, by (
1.9
) and (
4.32
), there are
c,C_{2}>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
, we have

\int_{X\setminus\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})}|\alpha|^{2}e^{-\delta_{p}}dv_{X}\leq C_{2}\exp(-cp)\Big(\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}+\|\nabla g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}\Big).

(4.35)

Now, as
\alpha
has support in
\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{2})
, it is enough to work in
(y,Z_{N})
,
y\in Y
,
Z_{N}\in N_{y}
coordinates.
To estimate the integral over
\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})
, we use (
4.26
) and make the change of variables by
F_{t}
to get

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})}|\alpha|^{2}e^{-\delta_{p}}dv_{Y}\wedge dv_{N}\\
=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\frac{r_{\perp}}{4t}}(N)}\Big|\big(F_{t}\circ\overline{\partial}^{L^{p}\otimes F}\circ F_{t}^{-1}\big)\tilde{g}\Big|^{2}(y,tZ_{N})\frac{e^{\epsilon p\alpha_{Y}(y,Z_{N})}}{|Z_{N}|^{2(n-m)}}dv_{Y}\wedge dv_{N}.

(4.36)

By (
4.27
), (
4.28
), (
4.29
), (
4.32
), (
4.36
), and the fact that for
j=1,2
,

\int_{\real{}^{2(n-m)}}\frac{|Z_{N}|^{j}\exp(-\frac{\pi}{4}|Z_{N}|^{2})dv_{\real{}^{2(n-m)}}(Z_{N})}{|Z_{N}|^{2(n-m)}}<+\infty,

(4.37)

we conclude that there are
C_{3}>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
g\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
and
\alpha
as in (
4.25
), we have

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})}|\alpha|^{2}e^{-\delta_{p}}dv_{X}\leq C_{3}\cdot t^{2}\cdot\Big(\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}+\|\nabla g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}\Big).

(4.38)

From Proposition
4.5
and (
4.38
), we deduce (
4.33
).

From (
4.31
), the fact that we may resolve the
\overline{\partial}
-equation, see
[
12
, Théorème 5.1]
, the trivial fact that
\overline{\partial}^{L^{p}\otimes F}\alpha=0
and (
4.33
), we see that there are
C>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
g\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, there is
f_{0}\in L^{2}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
, such that

\overline{\partial}^{L^{p}\otimes F}f_{0}=\alpha,\qquad\int_{X\setminus Y}|f_{0}|^{2}e^{-\delta_{p}}dv_{X}\leq\frac{C}{p}\|g\|_{L^{2}(Y)}^{2}.

(4.39)

Let us prove that
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}g-f_{0}
verifies the assumptions of Theorem
4.4
.

From the
L^{2}
-condition and the standard regularity results,
[
12
, Lemme 6.9]
,
f
is a smooth holomorphic section.
In particular,
f_{0}
is smooth as well.
However, since
\exp(-2(n-m)\delta_{Y})
is not integrable, the
L^{2}
-bound (
4.39
) implies that
f_{0}|_{Y}=0
.
Hence, we conclude that
f|_{Y}=g
.
It is only left to verify that
f
satisfies the needed
L^{2}
-bound (
4.19
).

From the Gauss integral calculation, and the fact that our triple is of bounded geometry, there is
C_{4}>1
, such that we have

\frac{C_{4}^{-1}}{p^{\frac{n-m}{2}}}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert g\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(Y)}\leq\big\|{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}g\big\|_{L^{2}(X)}\leq\frac{C_{4}}{p^{\frac{n-m}{2}}}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert g\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(Y)}.

(4.40)

Let us now prove that there is
C_{5}>0
such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
g\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, the following bound holds

\int_{X}|f_{0}|^{2}e^{-\delta_{p}}dv_{X}\geq C_{5}p^{n-m}\int_{X}|f_{0}|^{2}dv_{X}.

(4.41)

This will be clearly enough for our needs, as from the
L^{2}
-bound in (
4.39
), (
4.40
) and (
4.41
), we would deduce the
L^{2}
-bound (
4.19
).

First of all, since
\alpha_{Y}\geq\min\{\frac{1}{2}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})^{2},\frac{1}{2}\}
on
X\setminus\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})
, there are
c,C_{6}>0
, such that

\int_{X\setminus\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})}|f_{0}|^{2}e^{-\delta_{p}}dv_{X}\geq C_{6}\exp(\epsilon cp)\int_{X\setminus\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})}|f_{0}|^{2}dv_{X}

(4.42)

It is now only left to give the lower bound for the integrand on the left-hand side of (
4.41
), where the integration is done over
\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})
.
But remark that from (
2.60
) and (
4.30
), over
\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})
, there is
C_{7}>0
, such that for any
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we have
e^{-\delta_{p}}\geq C_{7}p^{n-m}
.
From this, we deduce

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})}|f_{0}|^{2}e^{-\delta_{p}}dv_{X}\geq C_{7}p^{n-m}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(\frac{r_{\perp}}{4})}|f_{0}|^{2}dv_{X}.

(4.43)

From (
4.42
) and (
4.43
), we obtain (
4.41
).
∎

Remark 4.7
.

Our proof shows that there is
{\rm{E}}_{p}^{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))\to H^{0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
, verifying
({\rm{E}}_{p}^{1}g)|_{Y}=g
for
g\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, and such that (
1.11
) holds for
{\rm{E}}_{p}^{1}
instead of
{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}
.

4.3
Asymptotic trace theorem, a proof of the upper bound in Theorem
4.1

The main goal of this section is to show that the restriction to
Y
of an element from
H^{0,\perp}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
lies in
H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
and to give a proof of the upper bound in Theorem
4.1
.
The main idea of our proof is to study the Schwartz kernel of the restriction operator and to use the exponential bound on the Bergman kernel.

Let us explain more precisely how to relate the Schwartz kernel
{\rm{Res}}_{p}(y,x)
,
y\in Y
,
x\in X
, of the restriction operator, evaluated with respect to the volume form
dv_{X}
, with the Bergman kernel.
In fact, directly from the identity
{\rm{Res}}_{Y}\circ B_{p}^{X}={\rm{Res}}_{p}
, we obtain that

{\rm{Res}}_{p}(y,x)=B_{p}^{X}(y,x).

(4.44)

Proposition 4.8
.

There is
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that the restriction to
Y
of any element from
H^{0,\perp}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
lies in
H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.2
and Theorem
4.6
, there are
C>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that for any
x_{0}\in X
, for
p\geq p_{1}
, we have

\int_{Y}\big|B_{p}^{X}(y,x_{0})\big|dv_{Y}(y)\leq Cp^{n-m},

(4.45)

We deduce directly from (
4.23
), (
4.44
), (
4.45
) and Young’s inequality for integral operators, cf.
[
40
, Theorem 0.3.1]
applied for
p,q=2
,
r=1
in the notations of
[
40
]
, that Proposition
4.8
holds, and, moreover, there are
C>0
and
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for
p\geq p_{1}
,
\|{\rm{Res}}_{p}\|\leq Cp^{n-m}
.
∎

Proof of the upper bound in Theorem
4.1
.

The proof is essentially identical to the proof of Proposition
4.8
with only one change: instead of looking at
{\rm{Res}}_{p}
, we consider the operator
{\rm{Res}}_{p}\circ{\rm{Res}}_{p}^{*}
.
In fact, directly from (
4.44
) and the fact that
B_{p}^{X}\circ B_{p}^{X}=B_{p}^{X}
, we conclude that the Schwartz kernel
({\rm{Res}}_{p}\circ{\rm{Res}}_{p}^{*})(y,y^{\prime})
,
y,y^{\prime}\in Y
, of
{\rm{Res}}_{p}\circ{\rm{Res}}_{p}^{*}
, evaluated with respect to the volume form
dv_{Y}
, satisfies

({\rm{Res}}_{p}\circ{\rm{Res}}_{p}^{*})(y,y^{\prime})=B_{p}^{X}(y,y^{\prime}).

(4.46)

The proof now proceeds in the same way as in Proposition
4.8
with an additional remark
\|{\rm{Res}}_{p}\circ{\rm{Res}}_{p}^{*}\|=\|{\rm{Res}}_{p}\|^{2}
.
∎

5
Asymptotic expansions of the two kernels

The main goal of this section is to study the asymptotics of the Schwartz kernels of the orthogonal Bergman projector and the extension operator.
This section is organized as follows.
In Section
5.1
, we establish Theorem
1.7
.
We also show that despite the global nature of the orthogonal Bergman kernel, the asymptotic expansion of it depends only locally on the geometry of the problem.
In Section
5.2
, we prove that after certain reparametrization given by a homothety in Fermi coordinates, Bergman kernel admits a complete asymptotic expansion. In Section
5.3
, we establish Theorem
1.8
.
Finally, in Section
5.4
, we prove all the other results announced in Section
1
.
We use the notation (
4.3
) throughout the section.

5.1
Exponential decay and localization of the orthogonal Bergman kernel

The first main goal of this section is to prove that the orthogonal Bergman kernel has exponential off-diagonal decay, i.e. to establish Theorem
1.7
.
The second main goal is to establish that the asymptotic expansion of the orthogonal Bergman kernel is localized.

The main difficulty here is that the projection
B_{p}^{\perp}
is not the spectral projection associated to the Laplacian.
Hence, the methods, developed by Dai-Liu-Ma in
[
11
]
, cf.
[
30
, Proposition 4.1.5]
, based on the finite propagation speed property for the wave equation, cannot be applied.

One can remedy this by extending a slightly more technical approach through the
L^{2}
-estimates with varying weights, similar to what has been done by Lindholm
[
29
, Proposition 9]
.
We follow here an alternative approach.
The main idea of our proof of Theorem
1.7
is to construct the “approximate projection”
B_{p}^{\perp,a}
onto the
H^{0,\perp}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
(the precise meaning of this is given after (
5.4
)), such that the Schwartz kernel of it verifies the estimate analogous to (
1.25
).
Then by means of the spectral theory, essentially relying on Theorem
4.1
, we relate
B_{p}^{\perp,a}
and
B_{p}^{\perp}
, and show that the exponential estimate of the Schwartz kernel persist through this relation. An exponential estimate of the Bergman kernel, see Theorem
4.6
, plays a crucial role in our approach.
The advantage of this approach over the one of Lindholm is that it introduces some of the techniques, which will later play a crucial role in our proof of Theorem
1.8
, where
L^{2}
-estimates, it seems, do not apply.

We use the notations and assumptions from Theorem
1.7
.
The next proposition is crucial for our further estimates.

Proposition 5.1

(Ma-Marinescu
[
32
, Lemma 2]
)
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
f\in H^{0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
and
x\in X
, we have

\big|\nabla^{k}f(x)\big|\leq Cp^{\frac{n+k}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{2}(X)}.

(5.1)

Let us first explain the construction of the approximate projection.
Define
A_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}\circ{\rm{Res}}_{Y}\circ B_{p}^{X}
, where
{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}
is as in (
1.9
).
Then define
B_{p}^{\perp,a}
as follows

B_{p}^{\perp,a}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}L^{2}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)\to L^{2}(X,L^{p}\otimes F),\qquad B_{p}^{\perp,a}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=A_{p}^{*}\circ A_{p}.

(5.2)

Clearly, for any
f\in L^{2}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
, we have
\big<B_{p}^{\perp,a}f,f\big>_{L^{2}}=\big<A_{p}f,A_{p}f\big>_{L^{2}}
. Hence in the notations of (
1.24
), we have

\ker B_{p}^{\perp,a}=H^{0,0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)\oplus\ker B_{p}^{X}.

(5.3)

By Theorem
4.1
and (
4.40
), the operators
B_{p}^{\perp,a}
,
p\in\mathbb{N}
, posses a
uniform spectral gap
, i.e. there are
a,b>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
, we have

{\rm{Spec}}(B_{p}^{\perp,a})\subset\{0\}\cup[a,b].

(5.4)

The properties (
5.3
) and (
5.4
) justify the name “approximate projection”, as
B_{p}^{\perp}
is the only self-adjoint operator on
H^{0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
, satisfying (
5.3
) and (
5.4
) for
a,b=1
.
The property (
5.4
) is what we call by “spectral relation” between
B_{p}^{\perp,a}
and
B_{p}^{\perp}
.

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

Clearly, by Lemma
3.1
, Theorem
4.6
and (
1.9
), we see that there are
c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C_{1}>0
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
,

\big|B_{p}^{\perp,a}(x_{1},x_{2})\big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(X\times X)}\leq C_{1}p^{n+\frac{k}{2}}\exp\Big(-c\sqrt{p}\cdot\big({\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})+{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},Y)+{\rm{dist}}(x_{2},Y)\big)\Big).

(5.5)

We will now show that the estimate (
1.25
) follows formally from (
5.3
), (
5.4
) and (
5.5
).
To do so, from (
5.3
) and (
5.4
), we see that for
\epsilon\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1-\frac{a}{2b}
, and for any
r\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, the following bound holds

\Big\|\Big(B_{p}^{X}-\frac{B_{p}^{\perp,a}}{2b}\Big)^{r}-B_{p}^{X}+B_{p}^{\perp}\Big\|\leq\epsilon^{r}.

(5.6)

Since the operator under the norm of (
5.6
) vanishes on the orthogonal complement of
H^{0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
and takes values inside of
H^{0}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
, Proposition
5.1
and (
5.6
) then imply that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there are
C_{2}>0
,
l\in\mathbb{N}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we have

\Big|\Big(\Big(B_{p}^{X}-\frac{B_{p}^{\perp,a}}{2b}\Big)^{r}-B_{p}^{X}+B_{p}^{\perp}\Big)(x_{1},x_{2})\Big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(X\times X)}\leq C_{2}p^{n+k/2}\epsilon^{r}.

(5.7)

Now, using the trivial identity
(B_{p}^{X})^{r}=B_{p}^{X}
, we expand
(B_{p}^{X}-\frac{B_{p}^{\perp,a}}{2b})^{r}-B_{p}^{X}
into
2^{r}-1
summands, such that each summand contains
B_{p}^{\perp,a}
as a multiple. By Theorem
4.6
and (
5.5
), we may apply Lemma
3.1
for
W\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=Y
, to bound each of those summands.
Hence, there are
C_{3}>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
,
p\geq p_{1}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\Big|\Big(\Big(B_{p}^{X}-\frac{B_{p}^{\perp,a}}{2b}\Big)^{r}-B_{p}^{X}\Big)(x_{1},x_{2})\Big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(X\times X)}\\
\leq C_{3}^{r}p^{n+k/2}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{c}{8}\sqrt{p}\cdot\big({\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})+{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},Y)+{\rm{dist}}(x_{2},Y)\big)\Big).

(5.8)

Now, we fix
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
, and let

r\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigg\lceil\frac{c}{16\log(\max(C_{3},2))}\sqrt{p}\cdot\Big({\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})+{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},Y)+{\rm{dist}}(x_{2},Y)\Big)\bigg\rceil,

(5.9)

where
\lceil\cdot\rceil
is the ceil function.
Then in the right-hand side of (
5.8
), for this choice of
r
, the power of
C_{3}
becomes negligible with respect to the last multiplicand.
Hence, (
1.25
) holds by (
5.7
), (
5.8
), (
5.9
) and the inequality
\epsilon<1
.
∎

Now, in the second part of this section, we show that despite the global nature of the orthogonal Bergman kernel, the asymptotic expansion of it depends only on the local geometry of the problem.

More precisely, we fix
X,Y,(L,h^{L}),(F,h^{F}),dv_{X},dv_{Y}
as in Section
1
.
We denote by
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},(L^{\prime},h^{L^{\prime}}),(F^{\prime},h^{F^{\prime}}),dv_{X^{\prime}},dv_{Y^{\prime}}
some other manifold, submanifold, etc.
We define
g^{TX}
,
g^{TX^{\prime}}
as in (
1.2
).
We fix
y_{0}\in Y
,
y^{\prime}_{0}\in Y^{\prime}
and assume that there is a biholomorphism between
U\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(r_{0})
and
U^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{B}_{y^{\prime}_{0}}^{X^{\prime}}(r_{0})
for
r_{0}<r_{X},r_{Y}
, such that it induces a biholomorphism of
Y\cap U
to
Y^{\prime}\cap U^{\prime}
and sends
dv_{X},dv_{Y}
to
dv_{X^{\prime}},dv_{Y^{\prime}}
respectively.
We also assume that the biholomorphism can be extended to holomorphic isometries between
(L,h^{L}),(F,h^{F})
and
(L^{\prime},h^{L^{\prime}}),(F^{\prime},h^{F^{\prime}})
.
In particular, it is a local isometry between
(X,g^{TX},y_{0})
and
(X^{\prime},g^{TX^{\prime}},y^{\prime}_{0})
.

We denote by
B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime}
,
B^{\prime}_{p}{}^{\perp}
the Bergman projector and the orthogonal Bergman projector associated with
X^{\prime},(L^{\prime},h^{L^{\prime}}),(F^{\prime},h^{F^{\prime}}),dv_{X^{\prime}}
.
For
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
, we denote by
B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime}(x_{1},x_{2})
,
B^{\prime}_{p}{}^{\perp}(x_{1},x_{2})
the Schwartz kernels of those operators, evaluated with respect to
dv_{X^{\prime}}
.

Theorem 5.2
.

There is
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
r_{0}>0
as above, there is
c>0
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for
V\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(r_{0}/2)
,
x,x^{\prime}\in V
, and any
p\geq p_{1}
, we have

\big|(B_{p}^{\perp}-B^{\prime}_{p}{}^{\perp})(x,x^{\prime})\big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(V\times V)}\leq C\exp(-c\sqrt{p}).

(5.10)

The proof of Theorem
5.2
is based on the following proposition.

Proposition 5.3
.

There is
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
r_{0}>0
as above, there is
c>0
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for
V\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(r_{0}/2)
,
x,x^{\prime}\in V
, and any
p\geq p_{1}
, we have

\big|(B_{p}^{X}-B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime})(x,x^{\prime})\big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(V\times V)}\leq C\exp(-c\sqrt{p}).

(5.11)

Proof.

Our proof here is an easy modification of the proof of
[
32
, Theorem 1]
, so we only briefly explain the main steps.
First, we denote by
\Box_{p}
(resp.
\Box^{\prime}_{p}
) the Kodaira Laplacians on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
(resp.
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X^{\prime},L^{\prime}{}^{p}\otimes F^{\prime})
).
We would like to prove that there are constants
a,c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
u_{0}>0
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
u\geq u_{0}
,
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in V\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(r_{0}/2)
, the following estimates hold

\Big|\Big(\exp\Big(-\frac{u}{p}\Box_{p}\Big)-\exp\Big(-\frac{u}{p}\Box^{\prime}_{p}\Big)\Big)(x_{1},x_{2})\Big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(V\times V)}\\
\leq Cp^{n+k/2}\cdot\exp\Big(c_{1}u-\frac{ap}{u}\cdot\big({\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})+1\big)\Big).

(5.12)

\Big|\Big(\Box_{p}\exp\Big(-\frac{u}{p}\Box_{p}\Big)-\Box^{\prime}_{p}\exp\Big(-\frac{u}{p}\Box^{\prime}_{p}\Big)\Big)(x_{1},x_{2})\Big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(V\times V)}\\
\leq Cp^{n+1+k/2}\cdot\exp\Big(-c_{2}u-\frac{ap}{u}\cdot\big({\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})+1\big)\Big).

(5.13)

The proof is a direct modification of the proof of
[
32
, (3.1), (3.2)]
, which states in the same notations that

\displaystyle\Big|\exp\Big(-\frac{u}{p}\Box_{p}\Big)(x_{1},x_{2})\Big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(X\times X)}\leq Cp^{n+k/2}\cdot\exp\Big(c_{1}u-\frac{ap}{u}\cdot{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})\Big),

(5.14)

\displaystyle\Big|\Box_{p}\exp\Big(-\frac{u}{p}\Box_{p}\Big)(x_{1},x_{2})\Big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(X\times X)}\leq Cp^{n+1+k/2}\cdot\exp\Big(-c_{2}u-\frac{ap}{u}\cdot{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})\Big).

(5.15)

More precisely, remark first that, (
5.12
), (
5.13
) are consequences of (
5.14
) and (
5.15
) for
{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})>\frac{r_{0}}{2}
.
Now, in the case
{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})<\frac{r_{0}}{2}
, the proof of (
5.12
), (
5.13
) is the same as the proof of (
5.14
), (
5.15
) in
[
32
, proof of Theorem 4]
with only one change. In the notations of
[
32
, proof of Theorem 4]
, for
h
instead of
\sqrt{p}{\rm{dist}}(x_{1},x_{2})/\epsilon
, one should take
\sqrt{p}\frac{r_{0}}{2\epsilon}
.
Then, again in the notations of
[
32
, proof of Theorem 4]
, due to finite propagation speed of solutions of hyperbolic equations, cf.
[
32
, (3.9)]
, and the fact that the Laplacians
\Box_{p}
and
\Box^{\prime}_{p}
coincide in
U
, the difference
