(H_{u,h}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\Box_{p})-H_{u,h}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\Box^{\prime}_{p}))(x_{1},\cdot)
vanishes.
Once (
5.12
) and (
5.13
) are established, the proof now proceeds in exactly the same way as in
[
32
, proof of Theorem 4]
with one final modification: the estimates of the quantities associated to
\Box_{p}
should be replaced by the estimates of the difference of the same quantities associated to
\Box_{p}
and
\Box^{\prime}_{p}
.

Now, (
5.11
) follows from (
5.12
), (
5.13
) and the following identity, cf.
[
32
, (3.14)]
,

B_{p}^{X}-B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime}=\Big(\exp\Big(-\frac{u}{p}\Box_{p}\Big)-\exp\Big(-\frac{u}{p}\Box^{\prime}_{p}\Big)\Big)\\
+\int_{u}^{+\infty}\Big(\frac{1}{p}\Box^{\prime}_{p}\exp\Big(-\frac{u_{1}}{p}\Box^{\prime}_{p}\Big)-\frac{1}{p}\Box_{p}\exp\Big(-\frac{u_{1}}{p}\Box_{p}\Big)\Big)du_{1},

(5.16)

by using exactly the same estimates as in
[
32
, proof of Theorem 1]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.2
.

The proof is an easy modification of the proof of Theorem
1.7
.
The only essential difference is that instead of working with the approximate projection, we work with the difference of the two operators associated to different geometries.
We use the notation from the proof of Theorem
1.7
.
We denote by
B^{\prime}_{p}{}^{\perp,a}
the operator, constructed as
B_{p}^{\perp,a}
in (
5.2
), but for
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},(L^{\prime},h^{L^{\prime}}),(F^{\prime},h^{F^{\prime}}),dv_{X^{\prime}},dv_{Y^{\prime}}
.
We fix a smooth cut-off function
\rho_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}U\to[0,1]
(resp.
\rho_{1}
), equal to
1
on
V
(resp. on
\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(\frac{3}{4}r_{0})
), and
0
on
\partial\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(\frac{2}{3}r_{0})
(resp. on
\partial\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(r_{0})
).
Since
U
and
U^{\prime}
are identified by a fixed diffeomorphism, we may regard
\rho_{0}
,
\rho_{1}
as functions, defined on
X
or
X^{\prime}
by extending them by zero.
For
r\in\mathbb{N}
, we consider the difference

D_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\rho_{0}\Big(\Big(B_{p}^{X}-\frac{B_{p}^{\perp,a}}{2b}\Big)^{r}-B_{p}^{X}\Big)\rho_{0}-\rho_{0}\Big(\Big(B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime}-\frac{B^{\prime}_{p}{}^{\perp,a}}{2b}\Big)^{r}-B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime}\Big)\rho_{0}.

(5.17)

Once the brackets in (
5.17
) are opened, one can replace each multiplicand
A
in the resulting expression by the sum
\rho_{1}A\rho_{1}+(1-\rho_{1})A\rho_{1}+\rho_{1}A(1-\rho_{1})+(1-\rho_{1})A(1-\rho_{1})
.
We denote by
R_{p}
the sum of the terms, which contain at least one
(1-\rho_{1})
-term.
Clearly, by Lemma
3.1
applied for
W\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\partial\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(\frac{5}{7}r_{0})
, there is
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
x,x^{\prime}\in V
such that for
p\geq p_{1}
, for any
r_{0}>0
, there are
c_{1},C_{1}>0
, such that

\big|R_{p}(x,x^{\prime})\big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(V\times V)}\leq C_{1}^{r}p^{n+k/2}\cdot\exp(-c_{1}\sqrt{p}).

(5.18)

As
{\rm{supp}}\,\rho_{1}\subset U
, using the diffeomorphism between
U
and
U^{\prime}
, we may interpret all the operators
\rho_{1}B_{p}^{\perp,a}\rho_{1}
,
\rho_{1}(B^{\prime}_{p}{}^{\perp,a})\rho_{1}
,
\rho_{1}B_{p}^{X}\rho_{1}
,
\rho_{1}B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime}\rho_{1}
as operators, acting over the same space,
X
.
Let us now study the terms, where only
\rho_{1}
appear.
For those terms, it is easy to see that one can rearrange the summands so that the terms in (
5.17
) with
\rho_{1}
can be expressed as a sum of
(2^{r}-1)2^{r}
elements, each of which would contain as a multiplicand either
\rho_{1}(B_{p}^{\perp,a}-B^{\prime}_{p}{}^{\perp,a})\rho_{1}
, or
\rho_{1}(B_{p}^{X}-B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime})\rho_{1}
and one of
\rho_{1}(B_{p}^{\perp,a})\rho_{1}
or
\rho_{1}(B^{\prime}_{p}{}^{\perp,a})\rho_{1}
.
Then by Lemma
3.1
applied for
W\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\partial U
, Theorem
4.6
, Proposition
5.3
and (
5.5
), we see that there are
c_{2},C_{2}>0
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
x,x^{\prime}\in V
, we get the bound

\big|(D_{p}-R_{p})(x,x^{\prime})\big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(V\times V)}\leq C_{2}^{r}p^{n+k/2}\cdot\exp(-c_{2}\sqrt{p}).

(5.19)

We assume for simplicity that
C_{2}>C_{1}
and
c_{2}<c_{1}
.
By summing up (
5.18
) and (
5.19
), we finally deduce that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
x,x^{\prime}\in V
, we have

\big|D_{p}(x,x^{\prime})\big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(V\times V)}\leq 2C_{2}^{r}p^{n+k/2}\cdot\exp(-c_{2}\sqrt{p}).

(5.20)

Now, by taking a sum of (
5.7
) and the analogous estimate for
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},(L^{\prime},h^{L^{\prime}}),(F^{\prime},h^{F^{\prime}}),dv_{X^{\prime}},dv_{Y^{\prime}}
, we get that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C_{3}>0
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
x,x^{\prime}\in V
, we have

\big|(D_{p}+B_{p}^{\perp}-B^{\prime}_{p}{}^{\perp})(x,x^{\prime})\big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(V\times V)}\leq C_{3}p^{n+k/2}\epsilon^{r}.

(5.21)

We now adjust
r
as follows

r\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Big\lceil\frac{c}{4\log(\max(C_{2},2))}\sqrt{p}\Big\rceil.

(5.22)

Then the contribution of
C_{2}^{r}
in (
5.20
) gets eliminated by the last multiplicand in the right-hand side of (
5.20
).
The proof is now finished by (
5.20
) and (
5.21
).
∎

5.2
Bergman kernel asymptotics in Fermi coordinates

The main goal of this section is to prove that after a reparametrization given by a homothety with factor
\sqrt{p}
in Fermi coordinates, Bergman kernel admits a complete asymptotic expansion in powers of
\sqrt{p}
, as
p\to\infty
. The proof relies on the analogous result of Dai-Liu-Ma
[
11
]
, stated in geodesic coordinates, and the calculations from Sections
2.2
,
2.3
.

We use notations from Section
1
and assume that
(X,g^{TX})
is of bounded geometry.
Recall that
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(Y,T^{*}Y\otimes\text{End}{(TX|_{Y})})
,
R>0
,
B\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(Y,{\rm{Sym}}^{2}(T^{*}X|_{Y})\otimes TX|_{Y})
, and
\nu\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(Y,N)
were defined in (
1.16
), (
2.5
), (
2.9
) and (
4.13
) respectively.

We fix a point
y_{0}\in Y
and an orthonormal frame
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{2m})
(resp.
(e_{2m+1},\ldots,e_{2n})
) in
(T_{y_{0}}Y,g^{TY})
(resp. in
(N_{y_{0}},g^{N}_{y_{0}})
) as in (
1.17
).
Recall that Fermi coordinates,
\psi_{y_{0}}
, were defined in (
1.18
).
Recall that the function
\kappa_{X}
in a neighborhood of
y_{0}
, was defined in (
1.19
).
We fix an orthonormal frame
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})
of
(F_{y_{0}},h^{F}_{y_{0}})
and define the orthonormal frame
(\tilde{f}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}_{r})
of
(F,h^{F})
in a neighborhood of
y_{0}
, as in Section
2.1
.
Similarly, we trivialize
(L,h^{L})
.
The choice of those frames and the associated dual frames allows us to interpret
B_{p}^{X}(x_{1},x_{2})
as an element of
\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
for
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
in a neighborhood of
y_{0}
.
Recall that
\mathscr{P}_{n}
was defined in (
3.33
).

Proposition 5.4
.

For any
r\in\mathbb{N}
, there are
J_{r}(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
polynomials in
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
, satisfying the same assumptions as polynomials from Theorem
1.8
, such that for
F_{r}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{r}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n}
, the following holds.
There are
\epsilon,c>0
, such that for any
k,l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
y_{0}\in Y
,
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
,
Q^{3}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=3(n+k+l^{\prime}+2)+l
, the following bound holds

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{p^{n}}B_{p}^{X}\big(\psi_{y_{0}}(Z),\psi_{y_{0}}(Z^{\prime})\big)-\sum_{r=0}^{k}p^{-\frac{r}{2}}F_{r}(\sqrt{p}Z,\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime})\kappa_{X}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z)\kappa_{X}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(Y)}\\
\leq Cp^{-(k+1-l)/2}\Big(1+\sqrt{p}|Z|+\sqrt{p}|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q^{3}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}\exp\Big(-c\sqrt{p}|Z-Z^{\prime}|\Big),

(5.23)

where the
\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}
-norm is taken with respect to
y_{0}
.
Also, the following identity holds

J_{0}(Z,Z^{\prime})={\rm{Id}}_{F_{y_{0}}}.

(5.24)

Moreover, under the assumptions (
1.8
), we have

J_{1}(Z,Z^{\prime})={\rm{Id}}_{F_{y_{0}}}\cdot\pi\Big(g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(z_{N},A(\overline{z}_{Y}-\overline{z}^{\prime}_{Y})(\overline{z}_{Y}-\overline{z}^{\prime}_{Y})\big)+g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(\overline{z}^{\prime}_{N},A(z_{Y}-z^{\prime}_{Y})(z_{Y}-z^{\prime}_{Y})\big)\Big).

(5.25)

Before describing the proof of Proposition
5.4
, let us recall the relevant asymptotic expansion of Dai-Liu-Ma.
Recall first that for
x_{0}\in X
, the coordinates
\phi_{x_{0}}
in a neighborhood of
x_{0}
were defined in (
2.2
).
Let us define the function
\kappa^{\prime}_{X}
in a neighborhood of
x_{0}
by the following formula

(\phi_{x_{0}}^{*}dv_{X})(Z)=\kappa^{\prime}_{X}(Z)dZ_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dZ_{2n}.

(5.26)

We use the orthonormal frame
(\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{r})
of
(F,h^{F})
, defined in (
2.8
) in a neighborhood of
y_{0}
.
Similarly, we trivialize
(L,h^{L})
.
The choice of those frames as well as the associated dual ones allows us to interpret the Schwartz kernel of
B_{p}^{X}
as an element of
\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
for
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
in a neighborhood of
y_{0}
. We denote this element here by
B_{p}^{X\phi}(x_{1},x_{2})
to distinguish it from
B_{p}^{X}(x_{1},x_{2})
previously defined.

Theorem 5.5

(Dai-Liu-Ma
[
11
, Theorem 4.1.18]
, cf.
[
30
, Theorems 4.2.9 and Problem 6.1]
and
[
27
, Theorem 4.3]
)
.

For any
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
x_{0}\in X
, there are
J^{\phi}_{r}(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\text{End}{(F_{x_{0}})}
polynomials in
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
, satisfying the same assumptions as polynomials from Theorem
1.8
, such that for
F^{\phi}_{r}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J^{\phi}_{r}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n}
, the following holds.
There are
\epsilon,c>0
, such that for any
k,l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
C>0
, such that for any
x_{0}\in X
,
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|\leq\epsilon
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, and
Q^{4}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=2(n+k+l^{\prime}+2)+l
, we have

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{p^{n}}B_{p}^{X\phi}\big(\phi_{x_{0}}(Z),\phi_{x_{0}}(Z^{\prime})\big)-\sum_{r=0}^{k}p^{-\frac{r}{2}}F^{\phi}_{r}(\sqrt{p}Z,\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime})\kappa^{\prime}_{X}{}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z)\kappa^{\prime}_{X}{}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(X)}\\
\leq Cp^{-(k+1-l)/2}\Big(1+\sqrt{p}|Z|+\sqrt{p}|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q^{4}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}\exp\Big(-c\sqrt{p}|Z-Z^{\prime}|\Big),

(5.27)

where the
\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}
-norm is taken with respect to
y_{0}
.
Also, the following identity holds

J^{\phi}_{0}(Z,Z^{\prime})={\rm{Id}}_{F_{y_{0}}}.

(5.28)

Moreover, under the assumptions (
1.8
), we have

J^{\phi}_{1}(Z,Z^{\prime})=0.

(5.29)

Proof of Proposition
5.4
.

Recall that the diffeomorphism
h
was defined in (
2.6
), and the functions
\xi_{L}
,
\xi_{F}
were defined in (
2.8
).
Directly from the definitions, we obtain the following relation between the Schwartz kernels

B_{p}^{X}\big(\psi(Z),\psi(Z^{\prime})\big)=\exp\big(-p\xi_{L}^{*}-\xi_{F}^{*}\big)(\psi(Z^{\prime}))\cdot\\
B_{p}^{X\phi}\big(\phi(h(Z)),\phi(h(Z^{\prime}))\big)\cdot\exp\big(-p\xi_{L}-\xi_{F}\big)(\psi(Z)).

(5.30)

From (
5.30
), we see that to establish Proposition
5.4
, it is necessary to study the Taylor expansions of each term in (
5.27
) for
Z\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=h(Z)
,
Z^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=h(Z^{\prime})
.

From Theorem
5.5
, (
5.30
), the fact that from Proposition
2.5
,
h(Z)=Z+O(|Z|^{2})
, the fact that from Proposition
2.9
,
\xi_{F}(\psi(Z))=O(|Z|)
and
\xi_{L}(\psi(Z))=O(|Z|^{3})
, the fact that the above
O
-terms are uniform by the coordinate description of bounded geometry, and the fact that the coefficients of the higher order Taylor expansions can be expressed in terms of
R^{TX}
,
A
,
R^{F}
, as described in Propositions
2.5
,
2.9
, we deduce (
5.23
) and (
5.24
).

To establish (
5.25
), let us place ourselves in coordinates as in (
3.33
).
By (
1.32
), we see that

2\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\overline{z}^{\prime}_{i}=g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\Big(({\rm{Id}}-\sqrt{-1}J)Z,({\rm{Id}}+\sqrt{-1}J)Z^{\prime}\Big).

(5.31)

From this, Proposition
2.5
and (
1.32
), we get

\displaystyle|h(Z)|^{2}=|Z|^{2}+2g^{TX}_{y_{0}}(B(Z),Z)+O(|Z|^{4}),

(5.32)

\displaystyle 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}(z_{i}\overline{z}^{\prime}_{i})(h(Z),h(Z^{\prime}))=2\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\overline{z}^{\prime}_{i}+4g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(B(Z),\overline{z}^{\prime}\big)+4g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(z,B(Z^{\prime})\big)+O(|Z|^{4}).

From (
3.33
) and (
5.32
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{n}(\sqrt{p}h(Z),\sqrt{p}h(Z^{\prime}))=\mathscr{P}_{n}(\sqrt{p}Z,\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime})\cdot

(5.33)

\displaystyle\cdot\bigg(1-\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{p}}\Big(g^{TX}_{y_{0}}(B(\sqrt{p}Z),\sqrt{p}Z)+g^{TX}_{y_{0}}(B(\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime}),\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime})

\displaystyle-2g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(B(\sqrt{p}Z),\sqrt{p}\overline{z}^{\prime}\big)-2g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(\sqrt{p}z,B(\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime})\big)\Big)+O\Big(\frac{1}{p}|\sqrt{p}Z|^{4}\Big)\bigg).

Now, from Proposition
2.9
and (
1.2
), we get

\exp\big(-p\xi_{L}-\xi_{F}\big)(\psi(Z))=1-\frac{\sqrt{-1}\pi}{3\sqrt{p}}g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(J\sqrt{p}Z,B(\sqrt{p}Z)\big)+O\Big(|Z|^{2}+\frac{1}{p}|\sqrt{p}Z|^{4}\Big).

(5.34)

By Lemma
2.12
and (
4.15
), applied for
M\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X
,
H\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=Y
and
T\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\rm{Id}}
, we get

\kappa_{X}^{1/2}(Z)=1+O(|Z|^{2}),

(5.35)

where
\nu\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(Y,N)
was defined (
4.13
).
We now apply (
5.35
) for
X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X
and
Y\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{y_{0}\}
, to get

\kappa^{\prime}_{X}{}^{1/2}(Z)=1+O(|Z|^{2}).

(5.36)

From (
5.27
), (
5.30
), (
5.33
), (
5.34
), (
5.35
), (
5.36
) and an easy calculation, we deduce

\displaystyle J_{1}(Z,Z^{\prime})=-\pi{\rm{Id}}_{F_{y_{0}}}\cdot\bigg(g^{TX}_{y_{0}}(B(Z),Z)+g^{TX}_{y_{0}}(B(Z^{\prime}),Z^{\prime})

(5.37)

\displaystyle-2\Big(g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(B(Z),\overline{z}^{\prime}\big)+g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(z,B(Z^{\prime})\big)\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{3}\Big(g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(JZ,B(Z)\big)-g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(JZ^{\prime},B(Z^{\prime})\big)\Big)\bigg).

By using the fact that
A
commutes with
J
and takes its values in skew-adjoint matrices, we get

\displaystyle g^{TX}_{y_{0}}(Z,B(Z))=-\frac{1}{2}\Big(g^{TX}_{y_{0}}(z_{N},A(Z_{Y})\overline{z}_{Y})+g^{TX}_{y_{0}}(\overline{z}_{N},A(Z_{Y})z_{Y})\Big),

(5.38)

\displaystyle g^{TX}_{y_{0}}(JZ,B(Z))=\frac{3\sqrt{-1}}{2}\Big(g^{TX}_{y_{0}}(z_{N},A(Z_{Y})\overline{z}_{Y})-g^{TX}_{y_{0}}(\overline{z}_{N},A(Z_{Y})z_{Y})\Big).

Now, we use (
2.35
) and the fact that
A
commutes with
J
to deduce

A(Z_{Y})z_{Y}=A(z_{Y})z_{Y},\qquad A(Z_{Y})\overline{z}_{Y}=A(\overline{z}_{Y})\overline{z}_{Y}.

(5.39)

From (
2.35
), (
5.37
), (
5.38
) and (
5.39
), we deduce (
5.25
).
∎

5.3
Orthogonal Bergman kernel asymptotics, a proof of Theorem
1.8

The main goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.8
.
For this, we first use the localization property of the orthogonal Bergman kernel, established in Theorem
5.2
, to reduce our problem to a special case
X^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
Y^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
and trivial
L^{\prime},F^{\prime}
(but endowed with non trivial metrics).
Then on the pair
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
we make a homothety and show that the renormalization of the relevant operators converges to the perturbations of the model operators from Section
3.2
.

Let us first describe precisely the construction of
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},L^{\prime},F^{\prime}
and the metrics on them.
As we rely later on the complex structure of
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},L^{\prime},F^{\prime}
in an essential way, the construction through geodesic coordinates, as in
[
30
]
, wouldn’t suffice for our needs.
We rely here in our non-compact setting on the results of Section
2.5
, and we propose an approach, which in the compact case essentially coincides Dinh-Ma-Nguyen
[
16
, after (2.23)]
.

The idea is to use holomorphic coordinates and holomoprhic frames of the vector bundles around the fixed point
y_{0}\in Y
and construct the associated objects on the neighborhood of
0\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
using those trivializations.
Then we extend those objects by the partition of unity to
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
A special care has to be taken to preserve the positivity of the line bundle.
As we would like to compare the original setting with this localized one in a uniform way, according to Theorem
5.2
, it is fundamental to choose holomorphic coordinates and frames on geodesic balls of uniform size around
y_{0}
.
At this point, the results from Section
2.5
will play a crucial role.

We fix
y_{0}\in Y
.
Let
r_{c},r_{c}^{1}>0
and
\chi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(r_{c})\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
U\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(r_{c})
,
V\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Im}\chi
, as in Theorem
2.25
and Remark
2.26
.
We put
X^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
Y^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
.

Let holomorphic frame
\sigma
of
L
over
\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(r_{0})
,
r_{0}>0
, be constructed as in Lemma
2.24
.
Assume, for simplicity,
4r_{c}<r_{0}
(if this is not the case, put
r_{c}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{r_{0}}{8}
).
Define the function
\theta(Z)
over
V
by

\exp(-2\theta(Z))\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=h^{L}(\sigma,\sigma).

(5.40)

From Lemma
2.20
and (
2.75
), we see that there is
C>0
, which depends only on
r_{c}
and
C_{n+6}
from (
2.1
) and (
2.7
), such that

\big\|\theta\big\|_{\mathscr{C}^{2}(V)}\leq C.

(5.41)

Denote by
\theta_{0}^{[1]}
and
\theta_{0}^{[2]}
the first and the second order Taylor expansions of
\theta_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\theta\circ\chi^{-1}
at
0
.
For
r_{c}^{1}>\epsilon>0
, we now define
\theta_{\epsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\real
as follows

\theta_{\epsilon}(Z)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\rho\Big(\frac{|Z|}{\epsilon}\Big)\theta_{0}(Z)+\Big(1-\rho\Big(\frac{|Z|}{\epsilon}\Big)\Big)\cdot\Big(\theta_{0}(0)+\theta_{0}^{[1]}(Z)+\theta_{0}^{[2]}(Z)\Big),

(5.42)

where
\rho
is a bump function as in (
1.10
).
Let
h^{L^{\prime}}_{\epsilon}
be the metric on
L^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X^{\prime}\times\mathbb{C}
defined by

h^{L^{\prime}}_{\epsilon}(1,1)(Z)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\exp(-2\theta_{\epsilon}(Z)).

(5.43)

Let
R^{L^{\prime}}_{\epsilon}
be the curvature of the Chern connection
\nabla^{L^{\prime}}_{\epsilon}
on
(L^{\prime},h^{L^{\prime}}_{\epsilon})
.
By (
1.2
), (
5.41
), it is easy to see that there is a constant
\epsilon_{0}>0
, such that for any
\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
, we have

\inf\Big\{\sqrt{-1}R^{L^{\prime}}_{\epsilon,Z}(u,Ju)/|u|^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}u\in T_{Z}X^{\prime},Z\in X^{\prime}\Big\}\geq\pi,

(5.44)

where
|u|
is the norm of
u\in T_{Z}X^{\prime}
, calculated by a (trivial) identification of
T_{Z}X^{\prime}
with
T_{0}X^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
\epsilon_{0}
depends only on
r_{c}
and
C_{n+6}
from (
2.1
) and (
2.7
).
From now on, we fix such
\epsilon_{0}
and remove it from all subsequent subscripts.
We assume, for simplicity, that
4r_{c}^{1}<\epsilon_{0}
.
From (
5.44
), we see, in particular, that
(L^{\prime},h^{L^{\prime}})
is positive.
We denote by
g^{TX^{\prime}}
the metric on
X^{\prime}
, defined through
(L^{\prime},h^{L^{\prime}})
as in (
1.2
).
Of course, by (
5.42
),
\chi
is then a local holomorphic isometry, defined over
\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(\frac{r_{c}}{2})
, between
(X,g^{TX})
and
(X^{\prime},g^{TX^{\prime}})
.

Now, let
r_{0}>0
and a holomorphic frame
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})
of
F
over
\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(r_{0})
be as in Lemma
2.24
.
Assume, for simplicity, that
4r_{c}^{1}<r_{0}
.
Define the function
h^{F}_{ij}(x)\in\mathbb{C}
,
x\in U
, as follows
h^{F}_{ij}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=h^{F}(f_{i},f_{j})
.
Define the metric
h^{F^{\prime}}
on
F^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X^{\prime}\times\mathbb{C}^{r}
by

h^{F^{\prime}}(1_{i},1_{j})(Z)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=h^{F}_{ij}\Big(\rho\Big(\frac{|Z|}{r_{c}^{1}}\Big)\cdot Z\Big).

(5.45)

where
1_{l}
,
l\in 1,\ldots,r
is the constant vector in
F^{\prime}
, given by
(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots 0)
, where
1
is put in the
l
-th place.
The pair
(F^{\prime},h^{F^{\prime}})
is a Hermitian vector bundle on
X^{\prime}
.

Clearly, the triple
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},g^{TX^{\prime}})
and the Hermitian vector bundles
(L^{\prime},h^{L^{\prime}})
,
(F^{\prime},h^{F^{\prime}})
are of bounded geometry.
Moreover, the corresponding constants
C_{k}
from (
2.1
), (
2.3
), (
2.7
), associated to
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},g^{TX^{\prime}})
,
(L^{\prime},h^{L^{\prime}})
,
(F^{\prime},h^{F^{\prime}})
, can be bounded in terms of the corresponding constants
C_{k+n+6}
from (
2.1
), (
2.3
), (
2.7
), associated to
(X,Y,g^{TX})
,
(L,h^{L})
,
(F,h^{F})
.

We denote by
g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0}
,
h^{F^{\prime}}_{0}
the restrictions of
g^{TX^{\prime}}
,
h^{F^{\prime}}
to
0\in X^{\prime}
.
We interpret
g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0}
as metric on
X^{\prime}
by using the standard trivialization of
TX^{\prime}
coming from the linear structure.
Similarly, we interpret
h^{F^{\prime}}_{0}
as Hermitian metric on
F^{\prime}
over
X^{\prime}
by using the trivialization of
F^{\prime}
.

As the expansion from Theorem
1.8
is stated in Fermi coordinates, we need an analogue of those coordinates on
X^{\prime}
.
For technical reasons, which will become clear after (
5.54
), we need a global diffeomorphism of
X^{\prime}
, which coincides with Fermi coordinates in a small neighborhood of
0
.
For
0<\epsilon<\min\{r_{c},R\}
, where
R>0
is as in (
2.5
), we define
\psi_{\epsilon}(Z)
for
Z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
as follows

\psi_{\epsilon}(Z)=\psi(Z)\rho\Big(\frac{|Z|}{\epsilon}\Big)+Z\Big(1-\rho\Big(\frac{|Z|}{\epsilon}\Big)\Big),

(5.46)

where
\psi(Z)\in X^{\prime}
is the Fermi coordinates, defined as in (
1.18
), but for the triple
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},g^{TX^{\prime}})
.
From bounded geometry condition, we see that there is
\epsilon_{2}>0
such that for
\epsilon<\epsilon_{2}
, the derivative of
\psi_{\epsilon}(Z)
is invertible for all
Z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and
|D\psi_{\epsilon}-{\rm{Id}}|<\frac{1}{2}
.
Moreover,
\epsilon_{2}
depends only on
r_{X},r_{Y},r_{\perp}
and
C_{0}
from (
2.1
) and (
2.3
).
For simplicity, we assume that
4r_{c}^{1}<\epsilon_{2}
.
We fix such
\epsilon_{2}
and denote
\psi_{\epsilon_{2}}
by
\psi_{0}
from now on.
As
\psi_{0}(Z)
obviously satisfies
\psi_{0}(Z)\to\infty
, as
|Z|\to\infty
, by the invertibility of
D\psi_{0}
and Hadamards global inverse function theorem, cf.
[
28
, Theorem 6.2.4]
,
\psi_{0}
is a diffeomorphism.
Clearly, as Fermi coordinates preserve
Y^{\prime}
, by (
5.46
),
\psi_{0}|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}
is a diffeomorphism on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
.

We define the volume form
dv_{X^{\prime}}
on
X^{\prime}
as follows

dv_{X^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\rho\Big(\frac{|Z|}{r_{c}}\Big)(\chi^{-1})^{*}dv_{X}+\Big(1-\rho\Big(\frac{|Z|}{r_{c}}\Big)\Big)dZ_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dZ_{2n}.

(5.47)

Similarly to (
1.19
), we define the function
\kappa_{X^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X^{\prime}\to\real
as follows

(\psi_{0}^{*}dv_{X^{\prime}})(Z)=\kappa_{X^{\prime}}(Z)dZ_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dZ_{2n}.

(5.48)

Clearly, since
\psi_{0}(Z)=Z
, as
|Z|\to\infty
, we have
\kappa_{X^{\prime}}(Z)=1
, as
|Z|\to\infty
.

Now, let us fix
e\in L^{\prime}_{0}
and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in F^{\prime}_{0}
, and consider the orthonormal frames
\tilde{e}
and
\tilde{f}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}_{r}
, constructed as in Theorem
1.6
, for
L^{\prime},F^{\prime}
, but instead of
\psi
, using
\psi_{0}
.
As
\psi_{0}
is globally defined, those frames become also globally defined.

Let us now consider the Bergman projector
B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime}
(resp. the orthogonal Bergman projector
B^{\perp}_{p}{}^{\prime}
) associated to
X^{\prime},L^{\prime},F^{\prime}
.
This is a self-adjoint operator, acting on the
L^{2}
-space
L^{2}(dv_{X^{\prime}},h^{L^{\prime}{}^{\otimes p}\otimes F^{\prime}})
.
The above orthonormal frames allow us to see
B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime}
(resp.
B^{\perp}_{p}{}^{\prime}
) as a self-adjoint operator, acting on the
L^{2}
-space
L^{2}(dv_{X^{\prime}},h^{F^{\prime}}_{0})
.
We denote by
B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime}(x_{1},x_{2})
,
B^{\prime}_{p}{}^{\perp}(x_{1},x_{2})
the Schwartz kernels of those operators with respect to
dv_{X^{\prime}}
.
The following theorem essentially shows that it is enough to establish our main result of this section for
X^{\prime},L^{\prime},F^{\prime}
instead of
X,L,F
.

Lemma 5.6
.

There are
c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
y_{0}\in Y
,
p\geq p_{1}
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(\frac{r_{c}}{4})
, the following estimate holds

\Big|B_{p}^{\perp}(x_{1},x_{2})-B^{\prime}_{p}{}^{\perp}(x_{1},x_{2})\Big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(X\times X)}\leq C\exp(-c\sqrt{p}),

(5.49)

where we implicitly identified
x_{1},x_{2}
to points in
X^{\prime}
using
\chi
.

Proof.

It follows from Theorem
5.2
and the fact that
\chi
is a holomorphic diffeomorhism, which extends to isometries between
(L,h^{L})
,
(F,h^{F})
and
(L^{\prime},h^{L^{\prime}})
,
(F^{\prime},h^{F^{\prime}})
over
\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(\frac{r_{c}}{2})
.
∎

The advantage of passing from
X,L,F
to
X^{\prime},L^{\prime},F^{\prime}
is twofold.
First, since all the vector bundles are now trivialized, the operators
B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime}
and
B^{\perp}_{p}{}^{\prime}
might be considered as operators, acting on the same space (independent of
p
).
Second, as
X^{\prime}
is equal to
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, we can use the homothety on
X^{\prime}
in our analysis.
We will use both of those features in what follows.

We define
t>0
as in (
4.3
) and
S_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U,F_{y_{0}})\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}_{y_{0}}^{X}(tR),F_{y_{0}})
as follows

S_{t}f(Z)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=f\Big(\frac{Z}{t}\Big).

(5.50)

Clearly, for any
f,f^{\prime}\in L^{2}(g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0},h^{F^{\prime}}_{0})
, we have

\big<S_{t}f,S_{t}f^{\prime}\big>_{L^{2}(g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0})}=t^{2n}\big<f,f^{\prime}\big>_{L^{2}(g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0})}.

(5.51)

We also consider a map
U\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}L^{2}(g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0},h^{F^{\prime}}_{0})\to L^{2}(dv_{X^{\prime}},h^{F^{\prime}}_{0})
, defined as follows

(Uf)(Z)=\kappa_{X^{\prime}}^{-1/2}(\psi_{0}^{-1}(Z))\cdot f(\psi_{0}^{-1}(Z)).

(5.52)

An easy verification using (
5.48
) shows that
U
is well-defined and it is an isometry, i.e.

\big<Uf,Uf^{\prime}\big>_{L^{2}(dv_{X^{\prime}})}=\big<f,f^{\prime}\big>_{L^{2}(g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0})}.

(5.53)

We now consider another operators
\mathcal{B}_{t}
,
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\perp}
, acting on
L^{2}(g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0},h^{F^{\prime}}_{0})
as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=S_{t}^{-1}\circ U^{-1}\circ B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime}\circ U\circ S_{t},

(5.54)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\perp}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=S_{t}^{-1}\circ U^{-1}\circ B_{p}^{\perp}{}^{\prime}\circ U\circ S_{t}.

From (
5.51
) and (
5.53
), we see that
\mathcal{B}_{t}
,
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\perp}
are self-adjoint.

We denote by
\mathcal{B}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})
the Schwartz kernels of
\mathcal{B}_{t}
,
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\perp}
with respect to the volume form
dv_{g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0}}
on
X^{\prime}
.
An easy calculation shows that they are related to the Schwartz kernels
B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime}(Z,Z^{\prime})
,
B_{p}^{\perp}{}^{\prime}(Z,Z^{\prime})
of
B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime}
,
B^{\perp}_{p}{}^{\prime}
, evaluated with respect to
dv_{X^{\prime}}
as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})=t^{2n}B_{p}^{X}{}^{\prime}\big(\psi_{0}(tZ),\psi_{0}(tZ^{\prime})\big)\kappa_{X^{\prime}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(tZ)\kappa_{X^{\prime}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(tZ^{\prime}),

(5.55)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})=t^{2n}B_{p}^{\perp}{}^{\prime}\big(\psi_{0}(tZ),\psi_{0}(tZ^{\prime})\big)\kappa_{X^{\prime}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(tZ)\kappa_{X^{\prime}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(tZ^{\prime}),

Lemma 5.7
.

There is
\epsilon>0
, such that for any
l\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
C>0
, such that for any
Z\in\real^{2n}
,
|Z|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{t}
,
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
f\in\operatorname{Im}(\mathcal{B}_{t})
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|\leq l
, we have

\Big|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}}f(Z)\Big|\leq C\big\|f\big\|_{L^{2}(g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0})}.

(5.56)

Proof.

Clearly, by (
5.54
), we have

\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{B}_{t}\subset(S_{t}^{-1}\circ U^{-1})\big(H^{0}_{(2)}(X^{\prime},L^{\prime}{}^{p}\otimes F^{\prime})\big).

(5.57)

We conclude by this, Proposition
5.4
and (
5.55
).
∎

We will now introduce the operator
\mathcal{E}^{0}
, sending the sections of
F^{\prime}
over
Y^{\prime}
to the sections of
F^{\prime}
over
X^{\prime}
by the following formula

(\mathcal{E}^{0}f)(Z_{Y},Z_{N})=f(Z_{Y})\exp\Big(-\frac{\pi}{2}|Z_{N}|^{2}\Big).

(5.58)

Denote by
\mathcal{A}_{t}
the operator, acting on
L^{2}(g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0},h^{F^{\prime}}_{0})
, as follows

\mathcal{A}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{E}^{0}\circ{\rm{Res}}_{Y^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{B}_{t},

(5.59)

where
{\rm{Res}}_{Y^{\prime}}
is the restriction operator (which is well-defined in the considered composition as
\mathcal{B}_{t}
has smooth Schwartz kernel).
We define the operator
\mathcal{C}_{t}
on
L^{2}(g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0},h^{F^{\prime}}_{0})
, as follows

\mathcal{C}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{A}_{t}^{*}\circ\mathcal{A}_{t},

(5.60)

where
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{*}
is the adjoint of
\mathcal{A}_{t}
.
The operator
\mathcal{C}_{t}
will be our main tool in the proof of Theorem
1.8
.
Remark its similarity with (
5.2
).
Let us study some of its properties.

Lemma 5.8
.

The operator
\mathcal{C}_{t}
is self-adjoint, and there are constants
a,b>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

{\rm{Spec}}(\mathcal{C}_{t})\subset\{0\}\cup[a,b],

(5.61)

for any
p\geq p_{1}
.
Moreover, we have the following

(\ker\mathcal{C}_{t})^{\perp}=(S_{t}^{-1}\circ U)\big(H^{0,\perp}_{(2)}(X^{\prime},L^{\prime}{}^{p}\otimes F^{\prime})\big).

(5.62)

Proof.

The proof is identical to (
5.3
) and (
5.4
).
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

The Schwartz kernel
\mathcal{C}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{C}_{t}
with respect to
dv_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}
satisfies the following bound.
There are
c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k,l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, we have

\Big|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\mathcal{C}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})\Big|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(Y)}\leq C\exp\Big(-c\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|\big)\Big),

(5.63)

where the
\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}
-norm is calculated with respect to
y_{0}
, which was fixed in the beginning of the section.

Proof.

From Theorem
4.6
, (
5.55
) and (
5.59
), we conclude that a bound like (
5.63
) holds for
\mathcal{A}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})
instead of
\mathcal{C}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})
.
It is uniform in
\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}
-norm with respect to the choice of
y_{0}\in Y
because the construction of
X^{\prime}
,
Y^{\prime}
,
(L^{\prime},h^{L^{\prime}})
, etc. depends smoothly on
y_{0}
and the Bergman kernel expansion depends also smoothly on
y_{0}
.
We now conclude by Lemma
3.1
and (
5.60
).
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

For any
r\in\mathbb{N}
, there are
\mathcal{J}_{r}^{{}^{\prime}}(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\text{End}{(F^{\prime}_{0})}
polynomials in
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
with the same properties as in Theorem
1.6
, such that for
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{{}^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{J}_{r}^{{}^{\prime}}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}
, the following holds.

There are
\epsilon,c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k,l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z,Z^{\prime}\in T_{y_{0}}X
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{t}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, we have

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\mathcal{C}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})-\sum_{r=0}^{k}t^{r}\mathcal{F}_{r}^{{}^{\prime}}(Z,Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(Y)}\leq Ct^{k+1-m}\cdot\\
\cdot\Big(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q^{5}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}\exp\Big(-c\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|\big)\Big),

(5.64)

where
Q^{5}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=3(2n+k+l^{\prime}+4)+l
.
Also, the following identity holds

\mathcal{J}_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(Z,Z^{\prime})={\rm{Id}}_{F_{y_{0}}}.

(5.65)

Moreover, under the assumptions (
1.8
), we have

\mathcal{J}_{1}^{{}^{\prime}}(Z,Z^{\prime})={\rm{Id}}_{F_{y_{0}}}\cdot\pi\cdot\Big(g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(z_{N},A(\overline{z}_{Y}-\overline{z}^{\prime}_{Y})(\overline{z}_{Y}-\overline{z}^{\prime}_{Y})\big)+g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(\overline{z}^{\prime}_{N},A(z_{Y}-z^{\prime}_{Y})(z_{Y}-z^{\prime}_{Y})\big)\Big).

(5.66)

Remark 5.11
.

From (
5.63
), (
5.64
), (
5.65
) and the fact that
\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}
is the orthogonal projector by the results of Section
3.2
, we see that for
a,b
from Lemma
5.8
, we have
a\leq 1\leq b
.

Proof.

Recall that polynomials
J_{r}(Z,Z^{\prime})
and
\epsilon,c>0
were defined in Proposition
5.4
.
From Proposition
5.4
, (
3.40
), (
5.55
) and (
5.59
), we conclude that for

\mathcal{J}_{r,0}(Z,Z^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{r}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}),

(5.67)

we can define
\mathcal{F}_{r,0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{J}_{r,0}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}
, so that there is
C>0
, such that for any
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|<\frac{\epsilon}{t}
,
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we have

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\mathcal{A}_{t}(Z,Z^{\prime})-\sum_{r=0}^{k}t^{r}\mathcal{F}_{r,0}(Z,Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(Y)}\\
\leq Ct^{k+1-m}\cdot\Big(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q^{3}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}\exp\Big(-c\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|\big)\Big).

(5.68)

From Lemma
3.8
, (
5.60
) and (
5.68
), we conclude that (
5.64
) holds for
c\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{c}{8}
,
\epsilon\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{\epsilon}{4}
and

\mathcal{J}_{r}^{{}^{\prime}}(Z,Z^{\prime})=\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{r}\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[\overline{\mathcal{J}_{r_{0},0}(Z^{\prime},Z)},\mathcal{J}_{r-r_{0},0}(Z,Z^{\prime})],

(5.69)

where we borrowed the notation from Lemma
3.5
.
From (
5.24
), (
5.67
) and (
5.69
), we deduce (
5.65
).
From (
3.49
) and (
5.25
), we deduce

\mathcal{K}_{n,m}[\overline{\mathcal{J}_{0,0}(Z^{\prime},Z)},\mathcal{J}_{1,0}(Z,Z^{\prime})]={\rm{Id}}_{F_{y_{0}}}\cdot\pi\cdot g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(\overline{z}^{\prime}_{N},A(z_{Y}-z^{\prime}_{Y})(z_{Y}-z^{\prime}_{Y})\big).

(5.70)

Now, remark that the summands from the equation (
5.69
), for
r=1
, are the adjoints of each other.
From this and (
5.70
), we deduce (
5.66
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.8
.

Let us show that Theorem
1.8
follows from Lemmas
5.6
,
5.7
,
5.8
,
5.9
,
5.10
.
More precisely, from Lemma
5.6
and (
5.55
), we see that it is enough to establish that there are polynomials
J_{r}^{\perp}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
as in Theorem
1.8
, and
\epsilon,c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for
F_{r}^{\perp}
as in Theorem
1.8
and any
k,l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
C>0
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{t}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, the following bound holds

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})-\sum_{r=0}^{k}t^{r}F_{r}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(Y)}\\
\leq Ct^{k+1}\Big(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q^{2}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}\exp\Big(-c\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|\big)\Big).

(5.71)

We will establish now that there are polynomials
J_{r}^{\perp}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, as described above, such that for some constant
C_{1}>0
, the following bound holds

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})-\sum_{r=0}^{2k+1}t^{r}F_{r}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(Y)}\leq C_{1}t^{2k+2}\Big(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q^{\prime\prime}_{2k+1,l,l^{\prime}}},

(5.72)

where
Q^{\prime\prime}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=3(4(n+2)(k+1)+l^{\prime})+l
.
Before this, let us show that this will imply (
5.71
).
Remark that from Theorem
1.7
, the bound on the degrees of
J_{r}^{\perp}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, and the fact that the polynomials
J_{r}^{\perp}
depend smoothly on
y_{0}
, we see that there are
c,C_{2}>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
, such that for
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\real^{2n}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, the following bound holds

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})-\sum_{r=0}^{2k+1}t^{r}F_{r}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(Y)}\\
\leq C_{2}\Big(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{6k+l+l^{\prime}+6}\exp\Big(-c\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|\big)\Big).

(5.73)

From (
5.72
), (
5.73
) and Cauchy inequality, we deduce (
5.71
).
Hence, it is left to establish (
5.72
), on which we concentrate from now on.

From (
5.54
), we see that
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\perp}
,
\mathcal{B}_{t}
are the only self-adjoint operators on
L^{2}(g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0},h^{F^{\prime}}_{0})
such that

\displaystyle{\rm{\displaystyle Spec}}{\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\perp}}\subset\{0,1\},

\displaystyle(\ker\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\perp})^{\perp}=(S_{t}^{-1}\circ U)\big(H^{0,\perp}_{(2)}(X^{\prime},L^{\prime}{}^{p}\otimes F^{\prime})\big),

(5.74)

\displaystyle{\rm{\displaystyle Spec}}{\mathcal{B}_{t}}\subset\{0,1\},

\displaystyle(\ker\mathcal{B}_{t})^{\perp}=(S_{t}^{-1}\circ U)\big(H^{0}_{(2)}(X^{\prime},L^{\prime}{}^{p}\otimes F^{\prime})\big),

From this and Lemma
5.8
, we see that for
a,b
as in Lemma
5.8
, we have

\mathcal{B}_{t}^{\perp}=\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{t}}d\lambda,

(5.75)

where
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
is a circle not containing
\{0\}
inside of it, but containing
\{a,b\}
. By Remark
5.11
, we see that
\Omega
contains
1
inside of it.

Let us now show the existence of polynomials
J_{r}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
as in Theorem
1.8
and provide an algorithmic way of constructing them.
We denote by
\mathcal{C}_{0}
the operator, acting on
L^{2}(g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0},h^{F^{\prime}}_{0})
by the convolution with smooth kernel
\mathscr{P}_{n,m}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})\cdot{\rm{Id}}_{F^{\prime}_{0}}
.
By the results of Section
3.2
, the operator
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is an orthogonal projection, hence we have

\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{0}}=\frac{1-\mathcal{C}_{0}}{\lambda}+\frac{\mathcal{C}_{0}}{\lambda-1}.

(5.76)

Now, let us apply the resolvent formula

\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{t}}-\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{0}}=\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{t}}\big(\mathcal{C}_{t}-\mathcal{C}_{0}\big)\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{0}}.

(5.77)

By using (
5.76
) and (
5.77
) inductively, we see that for a given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we can represent

\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{t}}=\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{t}}A_{k}+B_{k},

(5.78)

where the operators
A_{k}
(resp.
B_{k}
) are the linear combinations with coefficients given by some universal rational functions (in
\lambda
) of the operators of the form

B\big(\mathcal{C}_{t}-\mathcal{C}_{0}\big)^{k_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{0}\cdots\mathcal{C}_{0}\big(\mathcal{C}_{t}-\mathcal{C}_{0}\big)^{k_{l}}B^{\prime},

(5.79)

where
l\in\mathbb{N}
,
k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{l}=k
, (resp.
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{l}\leq k
) and
B
,
B^{\prime}
are either the identity operators or
\mathcal{C}_{0}
.
Now, from Lemma
5.8
and (
5.74
), we deduce that

\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{t}}=\mathcal{B}_{t}\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{t}}+\frac{1-\mathcal{B}_{t}}{\lambda}.

(5.80)

We now rewrite (
5.78
) using (
5.80
) as follows

\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{t}}=\mathcal{B}_{t}\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{t}}A_{k}+\frac{1-\mathcal{B}_{t}}{\lambda}A_{k}+B_{k}.

(5.81)

Now, by Lemmas
3.7
,
5.10
, each of the terms in (
5.79
) admit Taylor-type expansions as in (
5.64
) for
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|<\frac{\epsilon}{2t}
, where
\epsilon
is as in Lemma
5.10
.
From this and Lemma
3.8
, we deduce that there are polynomials, having the same properties as described in Theorem
1.8
, which we denote by
J_{r}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
,
r\leq k
, such that for
F_{r}^{\perp}
, defined as in Theorem
1.8
, there are
\epsilon,c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C_{3}>0
, such that for
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z,Z^{\prime}\in T_{y_{0}}X
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2t}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, the following bound holds

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\Big(\int_{\Omega}B_{k}d\lambda\Big)(Z,Z^{\prime})-\sum_{r=0}^{k}t^{r}F_{r}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(Y)}\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq C_{3}t^{k+1}\Big(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q^{\prime\prime}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}.

(5.82)

Also, since by Lemma
5.10
, the first term of the Taylor-type expansion of
\mathcal{C}_{t}-\mathcal{C}_{0}
vanishes, and each term in
A_{k}
has exactly
k
multiplicands
\mathcal{C}_{t}-\mathcal{C}_{0}
, by Lemma
3.7
, we conclude that there is
c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k,l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C_{4}>0
, such that for
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z,Z^{\prime}\in T_{y_{0}}X
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2t}
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, the following bound holds

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\Big(\int_{\Omega}\frac{1-\mathcal{B}_{t}}{\lambda}A_{k}d\lambda\Big)(Z,Z^{\prime})\bigg)\bigg|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(Y)}\leq C_{4}t^{k}\Big(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|\Big)^{Q^{\prime\prime}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}.

(5.83)

We will now show that the first summand on the right-hand side of (
5.81
) is bounded by the term in the right-hand side of (
5.72
).
For this, let us fix
Z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
|Z^{\prime}|<\frac{\epsilon}{2t}
.
From Lemma
3.7
, similarly to (
5.83
), we deduce that there is
C_{5}>0
, such that we have

\Big|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha}}A_{k}(Z,Z^{\prime})\Big|\leq C_{5}t^{k}(1+|Z|+|Z^{\prime}|)^{Q^{\prime\prime}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}\exp\Big(-\frac{c}{8}\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|+|Z^{\prime}_{N}|\big)\Big).

(5.84)

From (
3.65
) and (
5.84
), we readily deduce that there is
C_{6}>0
, such that

\Big\|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}A_{k}(\cdot,Z^{\prime})\Big\|_{L^{2}(g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0})}\leq C_{6}t^{k}(1+|Z^{\prime}|)^{Q^{\prime\prime}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}.

(5.85)

However, by the choice of
\Omega
, there exists
c>0
, such that for any
\lambda\in\Omega
, we have

{\rm{Spec}}\Big(\mathcal{B}_{t}\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{t}}\Big)\subset\mathbb{B}_{0}^{\mathbb{C}}(c).

(5.86)

From (
5.85
) and (
5.86
), we deduce that there is
C_{7}>0
, such that

\Big\|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\Big(\mathcal{B}_{t}\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{t}}A_{k}\Big)(\cdot,Z^{\prime})\Big\|_{L^{2}(g^{TX^{\prime}}_{0})}\leq C_{7}t^{k}(1+|Z^{\prime}|)^{Q^{\prime\prime}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}.

(5.87)

From Lemma
5.7
and (
5.87
), we deduce that there is
C_{8}>0
, such that for any
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}|<\frac{\epsilon}{2t}
, we have

\Big|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z{}^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\Big(\mathcal{B}_{t}\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{t}}A_{k}\Big)(Z,Z^{\prime})\Big|\leq C_{8}t^{k}(1+|Z^{\prime}|)^{Q^{\prime\prime}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}.

(5.88)

From (
5.75
), (
5.78
), (
5.81
), (
5.82
), (
5.83
) and (
5.88
), we deduce (
5.72
).
Also, from (
5.82
), we deduce the general algorithm for the construction of the polynomials
J_{r}^{\perp}
.

Now it only left to prove (
1.28
) and (
1.29
).
For this, let us find explicit formula for
B_{1}
, as the polynomials
J_{0}^{\perp}
,
J_{1}^{\perp}
, can be then read off from (
5.82
).
For this, we apply once (
5.80
) and then twice (
5.77
) to get

\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{t}}=\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{0}}+\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{0}}\big(\mathcal{C}_{t}-\mathcal{C}_{0}\big)\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{0}}+\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{t}}\big(\mathcal{C}_{t}-\mathcal{C}_{0}\big)\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{0}}\big(\mathcal{C}_{t}-\mathcal{C}_{0}\big)\frac{1}{\lambda-\mathcal{C}_{0}}.

(5.89)

From (
5.76
), (
5.78
) and (
5.89
), we deduce that

B_{1}=\Big(\frac{1-\mathcal{C}_{0}}{\lambda}+\frac{\mathcal{C}_{0}}{\lambda-1}\Big)+\Big(\frac{1-\mathcal{C}_{0}}{\lambda}+\frac{\mathcal{C}_{0}}{\lambda-1}\Big)\big(\mathcal{C}_{t}-\mathcal{C}_{0}\big)\Big(\frac{1-\mathcal{C}_{0}}{\lambda}+\frac{\mathcal{C}_{0}}{\lambda-1}\Big).

(5.90)

Now, by Remark
5.11
and the choice of
\Omega
, we have the identities

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{\lambda}d\lambda=0,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{\lambda-1}d\lambda=1,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}d\lambda=0,

(5.91)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{(\lambda-1)^{2}}d\lambda=0,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{\lambda(\lambda-1)}d\lambda=1.

From (
5.90
) and (
5.91
), we deduce

\int_{\Omega}B_{1}d\lambda=\mathcal{C}_{0}+(1-\mathcal{C}_{0})(\mathcal{C}_{t}-\mathcal{C}_{0})\mathcal{C}_{0}+\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathcal{C}_{t}-\mathcal{C}_{0})(1-\mathcal{C}_{0}).

(5.92)

Hence, by using the fact that
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{2}=\mathcal{C}_{0}
, the fact that by Lemma
5.10
, the first term of the Taylor-type expansion of
\mathcal{C}_{t}-\mathcal{C}_{0}
vanishes, and (
5.92
), we deduce (
1.28
).

Now, let us establish (
1.29
).
By using the notations from Proposition
5.4
, Remark
3.6
and (
5.66
), we deduce from (
5.82
) and (
5.92
) the following identity

J_{1}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})=\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\big[\mathcal{J}_{1}^{{}^{\prime}},\mathcal{J}_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}\big]-2\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\Big[\mathcal{J}_{0}^{{}^{\prime}},\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\big[\mathcal{J}_{1}^{{}^{\prime}},\mathcal{J}_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}\big]\Big]+\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\big[\mathcal{J}_{0}^{{}^{\prime}},\mathcal{J}_{1}^{{}^{\prime}}\big].

(5.93)

We will now calculate each term in (
5.93
).
From (
3.49
), (
3.50
) and (
5.66
), we deduce

\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\big[\mathcal{J}_{1}^{{}^{\prime}},\mathcal{J}_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}\big](Z,Z^{\prime})={\rm{Id}}_{F_{y_{0}}}\cdot\pi g\big(z_{N},A(\overline{z}_{Y}-\overline{z}^{\prime}_{Y})(\overline{z}_{Y}-\overline{z}^{\prime}_{Y})\big).

(5.94)

From (
3.50
) and (
5.94
), we deduce

\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\Big[\mathcal{J}_{0}^{{}^{\prime}},\mathcal{K}_{n,m}\big[\mathcal{J}_{1}^{{}^{\prime}},\mathcal{J}_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}\big]\Big](Z,Z^{\prime})=0.

(5.95)

Now, (
1.29
) follows directly from (
5.93
), (
5.94
) and (
5.95
).
∎

5.4
Asymptotics of the extension, proofs of Theorems
1.1
,
1.5
,
1.6
,
1.10

The main goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.1
, Corollary
1.3
and Theorems
1.5
,
1.6
,
1.10
.
Theorem
1.1
, Corollary
1.3
and Theorem
1.10
will follow almost directly from Theorems
1.5
,
1.6
.
The main idea for the proof of Theorems
1.5
,
1.6
, is to find an algebraic expression for
{\rm{E}}_{p}
in terms of
B_{p}^{\perp}
,
B_{p}^{Y}
and
{\rm{Res}}_{Y}
and then to get the needed results by transferring the statements from Theorems
1.7
,
1.8
.

We conserve the notations from Section
1
.
Define the operators
G_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}L^{2}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))\to L^{2}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
and
I_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}L^{2}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))\to L^{2}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
as follows

G_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\rm{Res}}_{Y}\circ I_{p}-B_{p}^{Y},\qquad I_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=B_{p}^{\perp}\circ{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}\circ B_{p}^{Y}.

(5.96)

The formula for
{\rm{E}}_{p}
in terms of
B_{p}^{\perp}
,
B_{p}^{Y}
and
{\rm{Res}}_{Y}
is based on the infinite summation with the operator
G_{p}
.
To get a grip on this infinite sum, we need to study the properties of
G_{p}
.

Lemma 5.12
.

There are
c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
, the following estimate holds

\Big|G_{p}(y_{1},y_{2})\Big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(Y\times Y)}\leq Cp^{m+\frac{k-1}{2}}\cdot\exp\big(-c\sqrt{p}\cdot{\rm{dist}}_{X}(y_{1},y_{2})\big),

(5.97)

where the pointwise
\mathscr{C}^{k}
-norm is interpreted as in Theorem
1.5
.

Also, for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
y_{0}\in Y
, there are
J_{r,G}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y})\in\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
polynomials in
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
, satisfying the same properties as in Theorem
1.6
, such that for
F_{r,G}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{r,G}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{m}
, the following holds.
There are
\epsilon,c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k,l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
y_{0}\in Y
,
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
|Z_{Y}|,|Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\leq\epsilon
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2m}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, we have

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z_{Y}^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}_{Y}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{p^{m}}G_{p}\big(\psi_{y_{0}}(Z_{Y}),\psi_{y_{0}}(Z^{\prime}_{Y})\big)-\sum_{r=0}^{k}p^{-\frac{r}{2}}F_{r,G}(\sqrt{p}Z_{Y},\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime}_{Y})\kappa_{Y}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z_{Y})\kappa_{Y}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z^{\prime}_{Y})\bigg)\bigg|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(Y)}\\
\leq Cp^{-\frac{k+1-l}{2}}\Big(1+\sqrt{p}|Z_{Y}|+\sqrt{p}|Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\Big)^{2Q^{2}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}\exp\Big(-c\sqrt{p}|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\Big),

(5.98)

where
Q^{2}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}>0
is defined in Theorem
1.8
.
Moreover, we have

J_{0,G}=0.

(5.99)

And under assumption (
1.8
), we even have

J_{1,G}=0,

(5.100)

so that
p^{m+\frac{k-1}{2}}
in (
5.97
) in this case can be replaced by
p^{m-1+\frac{k}{2}}
.

Proof.

First of all, by Lemma
3.1
and Theorem
4.6
, there are
c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for
p\geq p_{1}
,
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
, the following estimate holds

\Big|G_{p}(y_{1},y_{2})\Big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(Y\times Y)}\leq Cp^{n+\frac{k}{2}}\cdot\exp\big(-c\sqrt{p}\cdot{\rm{dist}}_{X}(y_{1},y_{2})\big).

(5.101)

Now (
5.101
) implies (
5.97
) for
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
verifying
{\rm{dist}}_{X}(y_{1},y_{2})>R
, where
R
is defined in (
2.5
) (and for a different choice of
c,C
). Hence, it is enough to establish (
5.98
), (
5.99
) to get (
5.97
) for all
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
.
Let us concentrate on this now.

Let
J^{\prime}_{r,Y}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y})\in\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
be the polynomials in
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, given by Theorem
5.5
, applied for
X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=Y
.
Recall that polynomials
J_{r}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
, were defined in Theorem
1.8
.
We rewrite
I_{p}
in the following equivalent form

I_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\big(B_{p}^{\perp}\cdot\kappa_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)\circ\big(\kappa_{N}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}\big)\circ B_{p}^{Y}.

(5.102)

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let us now write the Taylor expansions of
\kappa_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
\kappa_{N}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
in a neighborhood of
y_{0}
as follows

\kappa_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}(Z)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\kappa_{N,[i]}^{\frac{1}{2}}(Z)+O(|Z|^{k+1}),\qquad\kappa_{N}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\kappa_{N,[i]}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z)+O(|Z|^{k+1}),

(5.103)

where
\kappa_{N,[i]}^{\frac{1}{2}}(Z)
,
\kappa_{N,[i]}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z)
are homogeneous polynomials of degree
i
.
We denote now

J_{r}^{\perp,\kappa}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{r_{0}=0}^{r}J_{r_{0}}^{\perp}(Z,Z^{\prime})\cdot\kappa_{N,[r-r_{0}]}^{\frac{1}{2}}(Z^{\prime}).

(5.104)

By Theorems
1.7
,
1.8
, Lemma
3.10
and Theorems
4.6
,
5.5
, we conclude that for

J_{r,I}^{E}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})=\sum_{r_{0}=0}^{r}\mathcal{K^{\prime\prime}}_{n,m}[J_{r_{0}}^{\perp,\kappa},J^{\prime}_{r-r_{0},Y}]

(5.105)

and for
F_{r,I}^{E}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{r,I}^{E}\cdot\mathscr{E}_{n,m}
, the following holds.
There are
\epsilon,c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k,l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
y_{0}\in Y
,
Z=(Z_{Y},Z_{N})
,
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
Z_{N}\in\real^{2(n-m)}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\leq\epsilon
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2n}
,
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2m}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, we have

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}_{Y}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{p^{m}}I_{p}\big(\psi_{y_{0}}(Z),\psi_{y_{0}}(Z^{\prime}_{Y})\big)-\sum_{r=0}^{k}p^{-\frac{r}{2}}F_{r,I}^{E}(\sqrt{p}Z,\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime}_{Y})\kappa_{X}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z)\kappa_{Y}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z^{\prime}_{Y})\bigg)\bigg|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(Y)}\\
\leq Cp^{-\frac{k+1-l}{2}}\Big(1+\sqrt{p}|Z|+\sqrt{p}|Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\Big)^{2Q^{2}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}\exp\Big(-c\sqrt{p}\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|\big)\Big).

(5.106)

From Theorem
5.5
, (
5.96
) and (
5.106
), we conclude that (
5.98
) holds for

J_{r,G}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{r_{0}=0}^{r}\kappa_{N,[r_{0}]}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z_{Y})\cdot J^{E}_{r-r_{0},I}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y})-J^{\prime}_{r,Y}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}).

(5.107)

From (
1.28
), (
5.28
) and (
5.105
), we conclude

J_{0,I}^{E}={\rm{Id}}_{F_{y_{0}}}\cdot\kappa_{N}^{\frac{1}{2}}(y_{0}).

(5.108)

From (
5.28
), (
5.107
) and (
5.108
), we obtain (
5.99
).

Now, we assume (
1.8
).
From (
1.29
), (
3.46
), (
3.48
), (
3.50
), (
4.15
), (
5.29
), (
5.35
), and (
5.105
), we get

J_{1,I}^{E}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})={\rm{Id}}_{F_{y_{0}}}\cdot\pi g^{TX}_{y_{0}}\big(z_{N},A(\overline{z}_{Y}-\overline{z}^{\prime}_{Y})(\overline{z}_{Y}-\overline{z}^{\prime}_{Y})\big).

(5.109)

We obtain (
5.100
) from (
5.107
) and (
5.109
).
∎

Lemma 5.13
.

There are
C>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
, we have

\mathinner{\!\left\lVert G_{p}\right\rVert}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{p}}.

(5.110)

Moreover, under assumption (
1.8
), in the above inequality, we can replace
\sqrt{p}
by
p
.

Proof.

First of all, by Corollary
3.3
and Lemma
5.12
, there are
C>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
y_{0}\in Y
, for
p\geq p_{1}
, we have

\int_{Y}\big|G_{p}(y_{0},y)\big|dv_{Y}(y)\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{p}},\qquad\int_{Y}\big|G_{p}(y,y_{0})\big|dv_{Y}(y)\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{p}}.

(5.111)

The result now follows from (
5.111
) and Young’s inequality for integral operators, cf.
[
40
, Theorem 0.3.1]
applied for
p,q=2
,
r=1
in the notations of
[
40
]
.
The second part is proved using (
5.100
) in exactly the same way, one only has to rely on the comment after (
5.100
).
∎

Lemma
5.13
now implies that there is
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for
p\geq p_{1}
, the infinite sum

T_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{i}G^{i}_{p}.

(5.112)

is well-defined as an operator on
L^{2}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
.

Lemma 5.14
.

The following identity holds

{\rm{E}}_{p}=I_{p}+I_{p}\circ T_{p}.

(5.113)

Proof.

Since
B_{p}^{\perp}
has values in
H^{0,\perp}_{(2)}(X,L^{p}\otimes F)
, and
G_{p}
vanishes on the kernel of
B_{p}^{Y}
by (
5.96
), it is enough to establish that

{\rm{Res}}_{Y}\circ\big(I_{p}+I_{p}\circ T_{p}\big)=B_{p}^{Y}.

(5.114)

This identity follows from the observation that for any
i\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we have

{\rm{Res}}_{Y}\circ B_{p}^{\perp}\circ{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}\circ G_{p}^{i}={\rm{Res}}_{Y}\circ I_{p}\circ G_{p}^{i},\qquad{\rm{Res}}_{Y}\circ B_{p}^{\perp}\circ{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}\circ G_{p}^{i}=G_{p}^{i+1}+G_{p}^{i},

(5.115)

which, on its turn, follows by an application of the right composition with
G_{p}^{i}
in (
5.96
),
and the identity
B_{p}^{Y}\circ G_{p}=G_{p}
, following from (
4.1
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

By Lemmas
3.1
,
5.12
, and the fact that for any
C>0
there is
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that for
p\geq p_{1}
, the sum
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(\frac{C}{\sqrt{p}})^{i}
converges, we deduce that there is
c>0
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for
p\geq p_{1}
, the following estimate for the Schwartz kernel holds

\Big|T_{p}(y_{1},y_{2})\Big|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(Y\times Y)}\leq Cp^{m+\frac{k-1}{2}}\cdot\exp\big(-c\sqrt{p}\cdot{\rm{dist}}_{X}(y_{1},y_{2})\big).

(5.116)

Moreover, under assumption (
1.8
), we can replace
p^{m+\frac{k-1}{2}}
by
p^{m-1+\frac{k}{2}}
.
We conclude by Theorem
1.7
, Lemmas
3.1
,
5.14
and (
5.116
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

Let us fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and establish Theorem
1.6
for it.
By Lemmas
3.9
,
5.12
, we conclude that for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
y_{0}\in Y
, there are
J_{r,T}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y})\in\text{End}{(F_{y_{0}})}
polynomials in
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
, satisfying the same properties as in Theorem
1.6
, such that for
F_{r,T}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{r,T}\cdot\mathscr{P}_{m}
, the following holds.
There are
\epsilon,c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k,l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C>0
, such that for any
y_{0}\in Y
,
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
|Z_{Y}|,|Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\leq\epsilon
,
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{2m}
,
|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|\leq l
, the following bound holds

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|+|\alpha^{\prime}|}}{\partial Z_{Y}^{\alpha}\partial Z^{\prime}_{Y}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{p^{m}}T_{p}\big(\psi_{y_{0}}(Z_{Y}),\psi_{y_{0}}(Z^{\prime}_{Y})\big)-\sum_{r=0}^{k}p^{-\frac{r}{2}}F_{r,T}(\sqrt{p}Z_{Y},\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime}_{Y})\kappa_{Y}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z_{Y})\kappa_{Y}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z^{\prime}_{Y})\bigg)\bigg|_{\mathscr{C}^{l^{\prime}}(Y)}\\
\leq Cp^{-\frac{k+1-l}{2}}\Big(1+\sqrt{p}|Z_{Y}|+\sqrt{p}|Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\Big)^{Q^{\prime\prime}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}}\exp\Big(-c\sqrt{p}|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\Big),

(5.117)

where
Q^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k,l,l^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=6(8(n+2)(2k+1)+l^{\prime})+2l
.
Moreover, we have

J_{0,T}=0.

(5.118)

And under assumption (
1.8
), we even have

J_{1,T}=0.

(5.119)

Now, by Lemma
3.10
, (
5.106
) and (
5.117
), we deduce the asymptotic expansion (
1.21
) for

J_{r}^{E}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{r,I}^{E}+\sum_{r_{1}=0}^{r}\mathcal{K^{\prime\prime\prime}}_{n,m}[J_{r_{1},I}^{E},J_{r-r_{1},T}].

(5.120)

From (
5.108
), (
5.118
) and (
5.120
), we deduce (
1.22
).
Moreover, under assumption (
1.8
), from (
5.109
), (
5.118
), (
5.119
) and (
5.120
), we deduce (
1.23
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

The main idea of the proof is to compare the Schwartz kernels of
{\rm{E}}_{p}
and
{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}
.
Let us denote
K_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\rm{E}}_{p}-{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}
.
From (
1.9
), remark that the Schwartz kernel,
{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}(x,y)
,
x=(y^{\prime},Z_{N})\in\mathbb{B}_{Y}^{X}(r_{\perp})
,
y\in Y
, of
{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}
, evaluated with respect to
dv_{Y}
, equals to

{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}(x,y)=\rho\Big(\frac{|Z_{N}|}{r_{\perp}}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(-p\frac{\pi}{2}|Z_{N}|^{2}\Big)\cdot B_{p}^{Y}(y^{\prime},y).

(5.121)

From this and Theorem
4.6
, we conclude that there are
c_{1},C_{1}>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
x\in X
,
y\in Y
, the following estimate holds

\Big|{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}(x,y)\Big|\leq C_{1}p^{m}\exp\big(-c_{1}\sqrt{p}{\rm{dist}}(x,y)\big).

(5.122)

From Theorem
1.5
and (
5.123
), we conclude that there are
c_{2},C_{2}>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
x\in X
,
y\in Y
, the following estimate holds

\Big|K_{p}(x,y)\Big|\leq C_{2}p^{m+\frac{k}{2}}\exp\big(-c_{2}\sqrt{p}{\rm{dist}}(x,y)\big).

(5.123)

Let us now denote by
J^{\phi}_{r,Y}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y})
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, the polynomials from Theorem
5.5
, applied for
X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=Y
.
From Theorems
1.6
,
5.5
, we deduce that for polynomials

J_{r,K}^{E}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{r}^{E}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})-\sum_{r_{0}=0}^{r}\kappa_{N,[r-r_{0}]}^{\frac{1}{2}}(Z_{Y})\cdot J^{\phi}_{r_{0},Y}(Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}),\quad r\in\mathbb{N},

(5.124)

and the functions
F_{r,K}^{E}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=J_{r,K}^{E}\cdot\mathscr{E}_{n,m}
over
\real{}^{2n}\times\real^{2m}
, the following holds.
There are
\epsilon,c>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
C_{3}>0
, such that for any
y_{0}\in Y
,
p\geq p_{1}
,
Z=(Z_{Y},Z_{N})
,
Z_{Y},Z^{\prime}_{Y}\in\real^{2m}
,
Z_{N}\in\real^{2(n-m)}
,
|Z|,|Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\leq\epsilon
, we have

\bigg|\frac{1}{p^{m}}K_{p}\big(\psi_{y_{0}}(Z),\psi_{y_{0}}(Z^{\prime}_{Y})\big)-\sum_{r=0}^{k}p^{-\frac{r}{2}}F_{r,K}^{E}(\sqrt{p}Z,\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime}_{Y})\kappa_{X}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z)\kappa_{Y}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(Z^{\prime}_{Y})\bigg|\\
\leq C_{3}p^{-\frac{k+1}{2}}\Big(1+\sqrt{p}|Z|+\sqrt{p}|Z^{\prime}_{Y}|\Big)^{Q^{1}_{k,0,0}}\exp\Big(-c\sqrt{p}\big(|Z_{Y}-Z^{\prime}_{Y}|+|Z_{N}|\big)\Big).

(5.125)

From (
1.22
), (
5.28
) and (
5.124
), we deduce

J_{0,K}^{E}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})=0.

(5.126)

Moreover, under assumption (
1.8
), from (
1.23
), (
5.29
) and (
5.124
), we deduce

J_{1,K}^{E}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})=J_{1}^{E}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y}).

(5.127)

In particular, from (
5.125
) and (
5.126
), we see that for some
C_{4}>0
, we can improve (
5.123
) as follows

\Big|K_{p}(x,y)\Big|\leq C_{4}p^{m-\frac{1}{2}}\exp\big(-c\sqrt{p}{\rm{dist}}(x,y)\big).

(5.128)

From (
5.128
) and the use of Young’s inequality for integral operators as in Section
4.3
, we deduce that there are
C_{5}>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
, we have
\|K_{p}\|\leq\frac{C_{5}}{p^{\frac{n-m+1}{2}}}
, which implies (
1.11
).
In an analogous way, from (
1.23
), (
5.123
), (
5.125
), (
5.126
) and (
5.127
), under additional assumptions (
1.8
) and
A=0
, we see that in (
1.11
), one can replace
p^{\frac{n-m+1}{2}}
by
p^{\frac{n-m+2}{2}}
.

Now, an easy calculation, using (
1.7
), shows that for any
g\in L^{2}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, we have

\big\|{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}g\big\|_{L^{2}(dv_{X})}=\big\|f(p,y)\cdot B_{p}^{Y}g\big\|_{L^{2}(dv_{Y})},

(5.129)

where the function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{N}\times Y\to\real
is defined as

f(p,y)^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\int_{\real{}^{2(n-m)}}\kappa_{N}(y,\sqrt{p}Z_{N})\cdot\exp(-p\pi|Z_{N}|^{2})\rho\Big(\frac{|Z_{N}|}{r_{\perp}}\Big)^{2}dZ_{2m+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dZ_{2n}.

(5.130)

From the calculation of Gaussian integral, as
p\to\infty
, we have

f(p,y)^{2}=\frac{\kappa_{N}(y)}{p^{n-m}}+O\Big(\frac{1}{p^{n-m+\frac{1}{2}}}\Big).

(5.131)

From (
1.11
), (
5.129
), (
5.130
) and (
5.131
), we deduce (
1.12
).

Now, consider the Toeplitz operator
T_{\kappa_{N},p}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}L^{2}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))\to L^{2}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, given by

T_{\kappa_{N},p}g\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=B_{p}^{Y}(\kappa_{N}\cdot B_{p}^{Y}g).

(5.132)

Then, we clearly have

\big\langle T_{\kappa_{N},p}g,g\big\rangle_{L^{2}(dv_{Y})}=\big\langle\kappa_{N}\cdot B_{p}^{Y}g,B_{p}^{Y}g\big\rangle_{L^{2}(dv_{Y})}.

(5.133)

Thus, by (
5.129
) and (
5.131
), we have

\big\|{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}\big\|_{L^{2}(dv_{X})}=\frac{1}{p^{\frac{n-m}{2}}}\big\|T_{\kappa_{N},p}\big\|^{\frac{1}{2}}+O\Big(\frac{1}{p^{\frac{n-m+1}{2}}}\Big).

(5.134)

Recall that for compact manifolds
Y
, Bordemann-Meinrenken-Schlichenmaier
[
7
, Theorem 4.1]
(for
(F,h^{F})
trivial) and Ma-Marinescu
[
31
, Theorem 3.19, (3.91)]
(for any
(F,h^{F})
) established that there is
C>0
, such that

\sup_{y\in Y}\kappa_{N}(y)-\frac{C}{\sqrt{p}}\leq\big\|T_{\kappa_{N},p}\big\|\leq\sup_{y\in Y}\kappa_{N}(y).

(5.135)

We argue that the proof from
[
31
]
continues to hold for non-compact manifolds as well.
Indeed, recall that the lower bound of (
5.135
) was proved in
[
31
]
using the asymptotic expansion of the peak section, localized at the point where the supremum of
\kappa_{N}
is achieved.
As the asymptotic expansion of the peak section is based on the asymptotic expansion of the Bergman kernel and the exponential bound on it, and both those results continue to hold on manifolds of bounded geometry by the results of
[
32
]
, cf. Theorems
4.6
,
5.5
, we see that (
5.135
) continues to hold in full generality, see
[
30
, §7.5]
, when the supremum of
\kappa_{N}
is achieved.
Now, if the supremum is not achieved, then the same proof gives us that for any
\epsilon>0
, there is
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for
p\geq p_{1}
, we have

\sup_{y\in Y}\kappa_{N}(y)-\epsilon\leq\big\|T_{\kappa_{N},p}\big\|

(5.136)

We deduce (
1.12
) by (
5.134
), (
5.135
) and (
5.136
).

Now it is only left to prove that if
A\neq 0
, then under additional assumption (
1.8
), one can not replace
p^{\frac{n-m+1}{2}}
by
p^{\frac{n-m+2}{2}}
.
For this, remark that as long as
A\neq 0
, by (
1.23
), the operator, acting on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with the kernel
F_{1}^{E}(Z,Z^{\prime}_{Y})
, has non-zero norm.
Then, by the calculations, similar to (
5.129
), we see that the operator, acting on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with the kernel
F_{1}^{E}(\sqrt{p}Z,\sqrt{p}Z^{\prime}_{Y})
, has norm of order
\frac{1}{p^{\frac{n-m}{2}}}
, as
p\to\infty
.
We deduce from this and Theorem
1.6
that if
A\neq 0
, then under additional assumption (
1.8
), one can not replace
p^{\frac{n-m+1}{2}}
by
p^{\frac{n-m+2}{2}}
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.3
.

First of all, since
B_{p}^{X}\circ{\rm{E}}_{p}={\rm{E}}_{p}
, we clearly have

\big\|{\rm{E}}_{p}-B_{p}^{X}\circ{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}\big\|\leq\big\|{\rm{E}}_{p}-{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}\big\|
.
Now, the statement is a direct consequence of this, Proposition
4.5
, applied for
B_{p}^{X}\circ{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}g
, and (
1.12
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.10
.

We let
K_{p}={\rm{E}}_{p}-{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}
.
From Corollary
3.3
and (
5.128
), there are
C>0
,
p_{1}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, such that for any
p\geq p_{1}
,
f\in H^{0}_{(2)}(Y,\iota^{*}(L^{p}\otimes F))
, we have

\big\|K_{p}f\big\|_{L^{\infty}(X)}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{p}}\cdot\big\|f\big\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}.

(5.137)

Remark also that by construction, we have

\big\|{\rm{E}}_{p}^{0}f\big\|_{L^{\infty}(X)}=\big\|f\big\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}.

(5.138)

From (
5.137
) and (
5.138
) we deduce Theorem
1.10
.
∎
